PREFACE 
 
 I, the Chairman of the Department -related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Home 
Affairs, having been authorized by the Committee to submit the report on its behalf, do hereby 
present this Two Hundred Fifty Fourth Report on “ Cyber Crime - Ramifications, Protection and 
Prevention”. 
2. The Committee, in its meeting held on 16 th October 2024 selected the subject “Cyber Crime - 
Ramifications, Protection and Prevention” for examination and subsequently held six meetingson 9 th 
January, 2025 and on 2 nd, 3 rd, 10 th, 11th and 22 nd July, 2025.During these meetings, the Committee 
heard the views of representatives from the Ministry of Home Affairs (MHA) and Indian Cyber 
Crime Coordination Centre (I4C), Ministry of Electronics & Information Technology (MeitY), 
Ministry of External Aff airs (MEA), Ministry of Corporate Affairs (MCA), Department of School 
Education & Literacy (DoSEL), Department of Higher Education (DoHE), Department of 
Telecommunications (DoT), Telecom Regulatory Authority of India (TRAI), Central Bureau of 
Investigation (CBI), National Investigation Agency (NIA), Department of Financial Services (DFS), 
Financial Intelligence Unit -India (FIU -IND), Reserve Bank of India (RBI), State Bank of India 
(SBI), Punjab National Bank (PNB), HDFC Bank, Securities and Exchange Board o f India (SEBI), 
National Stock Exchange (NSE), National Payments Corporation of India (NPCI), National Critical 
Information Infrastructure Protection Centre (NCIIPC), Centre for Development of Advanced 
Computing (C -DAC), Indian Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT -In), National 
Informatics Centre (NIC) and Domain experts on the subject, “ Cyber Crime - Ramifications, 
Protection and Prevention”. In addition, the Committee sought views/suggestions on the subject from 
individual/organisations/institutions/experts. 
 
3. The Committee while making its observations/recommendations has mainly relied upon the 
following documents:- 
i. Background Notes furnished and Presentations made by the representatives of MHA and I4C, 
MeitY, MEA, MCA, DoSEL, DoHE, DoT, TRAI, CBI, NI A, DFS, FIU -IND, RBI, SBI, 
PNB, HDFC Bank, SEBI, NSE, NPCI, NCIIPC, C -DAC, CERT -In, NIC, and Domain 
experts on the subject during the meetings of the Committee;  
ii. Replies furnished by the concerned Ministries/Departments and Organisations in response to 
the questionnaire forwarded by the Secretariat; 
iii. Replies to the queries/comments/suggestions of the Members, raised during the Committee's 
meetings, as furnished by the Ministries/Departments/Organisations and other stakeholders 
during various meetings of the Committeee; and 
iv. Verbatim transcripts of the meetings of the Committee held on 9 th January, 2025 and on 2nd, 
3rd, 10th, 11th and 22nd July, 2025. 
 
4. The Committee considered the draft Two Hundred Fifty Fourth Report and adopted the same 
in its meeting held on 18thAugust, 2025. 
 
5. For the facility of reference and convenience, observations and recommendations of the 
Committee have been printed in bold letters in the body of the Report. For further convenience, 
abbreviations used in the Report have been compiled in a table under the acronyms section.

1 
 
Part I: Background 
1.1 Introduction 
1.1.1 The evolution of cybercrime in India is deeply intertwined with the country’s 
remarkable digital transformation over the past two decades, especially during the last decade 
with the launch of Digital India Mission , India quickly embraced widespread internet 
connectivity, mobile technology and digital financial systems . The nature of criminal activity 
underwent a profound shift, the  traditional crimes began to give way to sophisticated, 
technology-driven offences, marking a significant change in the criminal landscape. In the 
early 2000s, cybercrime in India was relatively limited in scope and sophistication. Offenc es 
typically involved isolate d incidents such as hacking, online defamation and basic forms of 
financial fraud. These early cybercrimes were often perpetrated by individual hackers or 
small groups using rudimentary technological means. At that time, India’s legal and 
institutional frameworks to combat cybercrime were still in the early stages of development. 
The enactment of the Information Technology Act in 2000  laid down the essential 
groundwork by establishing the first comprehensive set of laws aimed at addressing the 
emerging challenges posed by cyber offences. This legislation served as the cornerstone for 
regulating electronic transactions and penalizing cybercrimes.  Considering the evolving 
nature of cyber threats, the Government made major amendments to  the Parent Act in 2008 
and 2023, to further strengthen its provisions and ensure continued relevance and 
effectiveness. However, as India’s internet user base expanded exponentially, the 
opportunities for cybercriminals grew correspondingly. Over time, th e surge in cybercrime 
has become increasingly apparent, reflecting a sharp upward trend as more people and 
organisations embraced digital technologies. With the rapid expansion of digital platforms, 
cybercriminal activities diversified well bey ond their or iginal scope. Offenc es such as 
financial scams, identity impersonation, ransomware incidents and widespread data breaches 
began to occur more frequently, impacting not just individuals and companies, but also vital 
national systems and infrastructure. 
1.1.2 By the mid -2010s, the cyber threat landscape had evolved further. Organised 
cybercriminal groups, often with international connections, began to emerge. These groups 
employed advanced tactics and tools to exploit vulnerabilities in financial institutions , 
corporate networks and Government systems. The rise of sophisticated cyber -espionage and 
ransomware attacks underscored the growing complexity and scale of the threat. The 
COVID-19 pandemic in 2020 marked another pivotal moment in India’s digital journey . 
With a surge in digital adoption due to lockdowns and remote work, there was a 
corresponding spike in cybercrime incidents. The increased reliance on digital platforms for 
banking, commerce and communication exposed new vulnerabilities and created fresh 
opportunities for cybercriminals. Online fraud, phishing scams, identity theft and data 
breaches became more prevalent, highlighting the risks associated with rapid digitalization. 
1.1.3 In response to these mounting challenges, the Indian Government took significant 
steps to strengthen its cyber security framework. Amendments to the Information Technology 
Act, 2000, the establishment of the Indian Cyber Crime Coordination Centre (I4C) and the 
launch of the National Cyber Crime Reporting Portal were key measures aimed at 
empowering law enforcement agencies and citizens alike. Despite these efforts, 
cybercriminals continue to adapt and innovate, leveraging advanced techniques such as social 
engineering and artificial intelligence to exploit both technological and human vulnerabilities. 

 
 
2 
 
1.1.4 Considering the current scenario, the Committee has sele cted the subject of Cyb er 
Crime – Ramifications, Protection and Prevention due to its growing relevance and impact on 
society. With the increasing dependence on digital platforms for communication, business 
and daily life, incidents of cyber attacks have surged dramatically. The  rise in sophisticated 
threats such as ransomware, data breaches and online fraud poses significant risks to 
individuals, organisations and even national security. Moreover, the quick advancements in 
technology, including artificial intelligence, have enab led cyber criminals to develop more 
complex and harder -to-detect methods. These developments have resulted in substantial 
financial losses and have eroded public trust in digital systems. Given these pressing 
concerns, it is crucial to understand and addre ss the challenges posed by cyber crime. By 
focusing on this subject, the Committee aims to promote awareness, encourage preventive 
measures and contribute to the development of effective strategies to combat cyber threats in 
today’s digital age. 
1.1.5 The Committee presents its findings and recommendations in the following chapters, 
drawing upon a wide range of inputs collected from various Ministries, organisations, banks, 
expert opinions and other key stakeholders. Throughout the course of its work, the 
Committee engaged in detailed consultations, held multiple rounds of discussions and 
carefully reviewed relevant data and policy documents to ensure a comprehensive 
understanding of the issues involved. By incorporating diverse perspectives and expert 
insights, the Committee aimed to address the complexities of the subject matter in a holistic 
manner.  
India’s Digital Revolution: A Decade of Transformation 
1.1.6 Over the past decade, India has undergone a remarkable digital transformation, 
positioning itself as a global leader in technology -driven growth. The launch of the Digital 
India initiative in 2015 marked a turning point, aiming to bridge the digital divide by building 
robust digital infrastructure, promoting digital literacy and making Government serv ices 
accessible online. Today, with nearly 970 million internet connections and 5G coverage in 
99.6% of districts, high-speed connectivity is a reality even in remote villages. The BharatNet 
project has played a pivotal role in this journey, connecting ove r 2,18,000 Gram Panchayats 
with high -speed internet and ensuring that the benefits of digitalization reach rural 
populations, thus fostering inclusive growth.  
1.1.7 A cornerstone of India’s digital journey is the adoption of digital payments and e -
governance. The Unified Payments Interface (UPI) has revolutionized financial transactions, 
recording nearly 1,867.7 crore transactions worth ₹24.77 lakh crore in April 2025.  UPI’s 
reach now extends to seven countries (Bhutan, France, Mauritius, Nepal, Singapore,  Sri 
Lanka and UAE ) demonstrating the global impact of India’s digital public infrastructure. 
Initiatives such as DigiLocker and UMANG (Unified Mobile Application for New –age 
Governance) have enabled millions to access essential documents and government se rvices 
online, breaking barriers of geography and bureaucracy. As a result, presently, the digital 
economy is estimated to contribute over 13% to the national GDP, with projections indicating 
it could account for nearly one-fifth of the economy by 2030. 
1.1.8 Central to this transformation is the Government’s focus on leveraging emerging 
technologies like artificial intelligence, the Internet of Things (IoT) and blockchain. These 
technologies are revolutionizing sectors such as agriculture, healthcare, fint ech and 
education. The establishment of AI Centres of Excellence and the integration of AI into 
public services reflect a commitment to not just keeping pace with global advancements but 

 
 
3 
 
setting new benchmarks in innovation and digital governance. Increase d budget allocations 
for digital transformation and skill development underscore the priority placed on creating a 
future-ready workforce and fostering a culture of innovation. 
1.1.9 Strategic initiatives such as Aadhaar, UPI and DigiLocker have collective ly 
established an extensive, resilient and secure digital public infrastructure, facilitating 
seamless online service delivery. The "Pradhan Mantri Gramin Digital Saksharta Abhiyan 
(PMGDISHA)" has been instrumental in fostering digital literacy across rura l areas, 
empowering millions with the skills needed to participate in the digital economy. With a 
telephone subscriber base of 1.19 billion and the lowest average mobile data cost globally, 
India has ensured digital affordability and accessibility for all.  These efforts reflect the 
Government’s unwavering commitment to digital inclusion, technological advancement and 
ensuring that the benefits of digitalization permeate every segment of society, firmly 
establishing India as a leader in the global digital ecosystem. 
1.2  Understanding Cybercrime 
1.2.1 The National Cyber crime Reporting Portal (NCRP) defines the Cyber crime as any 
unlawful act where  computer, computer network, or electronic device is used as a tool or 
target to commit or facilitate a crime. This definition includes both traditional crimes that are 
facilitated by technology, as well as new age crimes that specifically target digital systems.  
Traditional crimes in cyber space include theft, fraud, forgery, defamation and mischief, all 
of which can be committed using electronic devices or networks.  There are some new -age 
crimes that are emerging,  these offenc es are specifically enabled by technology, such as 
hacking, denial-of-service attacks, phishing, malware distribution, cyber terrorism, etc. 
1.2.2 Indian Cyber Crime Coordination Centre (I4C) in its replies to the Committee stated 
that the National Crime Records Bureau  (NCRB) in its report "Crime in India 2022" 
categorise cybercrime under three broad legal domains: 
Offences Under the Information Technology (IT) Act, 2000 
i. Ransomware 
ii. Other offences under the IT Act 
iii. Tampering with computer source documents (Sec. 65) 
iv. Computer-related offences (Sec. 66) 
 Dishonest receipt of stolen computer resources (Sec. 66B) 
 Identity theft (Sec. 66C) 
 Cheating by personation using computer resources (Sec. 66D) 
 Violation of privacy (Sec. 66E) 
v. Cyber terrorism (Sec. 66F) 
vi. Publication/transmission of obscene/sexually explicit acts (Sec. 67, 67A, 
67B) 
 Obscene material (Sec. 67) 
 Sexually explicit acts (Sec. 67A) 
 Child sexual exploitative and abuse material (CSEAM) (Sec. 67B) 
 Other subsections including retention by intermediaries (Sec. 67C) 
vii. Interception/monitoring/decryption (Sec. 69) 
viii. Unauthorised access to protected systems (Sec. 70) 
ix. Abetment to commit offence (Sec. 84B) 
x. Attempt to commit offence (Sec. 84C) 
xi. Other sections of the IT Act

1.3  Evolving Types of Cyber Crime 
1.3.1 Cybercrime encompasses a wide range of illegal activities carried out through 
computers, networks or the internet. Among the most common types are phishing, where 
attackers impersonate trusted entities to deceive individuals into revealing sensitive 
information such as bank details or login credentials. Related to this are vishing (voice 
phishing) and smishing (SMS phi shing), which use phone calls or text messages to trick 
users. Identity theft remains a major threat, involving the misuse of someone's personal data 
for fraudulent purposes. Ransomware attacks have increased significantly in recent years, 
where cybercriminals encrypt a victim’s files and demand payment to restore access. These 
often target institutions like hospitals, schools and businesses, leading to major operational 
and financial disruptions. Malware in various forms is also widely used to steal data o r 
damage systems. Distributed Denial of Service (DDoS) attacks, which flood a network with 
traffic to make it inaccessible, are another serious concern for businesses and Governments. 
1.3.2 Beyond these, cyber crime is evolving rapidly with the growth of t echnology. Newer 
forms like cryptojacking , secretly exploit a user’s devices to mine cryptocurrency, often 
without the victim even realizing it. Fileless malware operates in memory rather than being 
stored on disk, making it harder to detect. Social engine ering attacks which manipulate 
human behaviour rather than relying on technical flaws, continue to be highly effective, 
especially when combined with AI. Developers of malicious software increasingly use AI -
powered cyberattacks, including deepfakes to carr y out more convincing and hard -to-detect 

 
 
5 
 
crimes. Cyber criminals are also targeting Internet of Things (IoT) devices and automotive 
systems, raising safety and privacy concerns. The rise of cybercrime -as-a-service (CaaS) 
makes it easier for less -skilled in dividuals to launch powerful cyberattacks by purchasing 
tools and services from the dark web.  
1.3.3 Geopolitical tensions have intensified, with cyber tools increasingly being used to 
attack critical infrastructure, spread misinformation and disrupt essen tial public services. As 
cyber threats continuously grow in number and sophistication, staying informed, adopting 
preventive measures and building strong digital defen ces have become essential for 
individuals, organisations and Governments worldwide. 
Intricacies of Cyber crime  
1.3.4 Cyber crime encompasses a wide range of illegal activities conducted through 
computers and networks. It includes offences such as hacking, phishing, Denial-of-Service 
(DoS), cyber stalking, digital arrest scams , identity theft, online fraud, malware attacks, etc. 
Among these, ransomware attacks have emerged as particularly damaging, often targeting 
critical infrastructure and demanding substantial ransoms for data recovery. The rise of 
Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS) platforms shows how easily cybercriminals can access 
advanced tools to launch attacks, even with minimal technical expertise. The increasing 
complexity and interconnectivity of systems have provided cyber criminals with 
unprecedented opportunities to exploit vuln erabilities. The growing threat of Cyber crime is 
continuously expanding, day -by-day.  Cyber criminals now operate in a global marketplace 
where they can easily acquire sophisticated tools and services that facilitates their illegal 
attacks. The World Economic Forum has ranked Cyber crime among the top ten global risks.  
The overall cyber security environment is marked by rising volumes of attacks and increasing 
sophisticated technology, leaving many organisations feeling ill -equipped to effectively 
address these threats. 
1.4  Growing Importance of Cyber Crime Prevention 
1.4.1 Cybercrime prevention and protection have become increasingly vital as digital 
technologies permeate every aspect of life. With the rapid growth of internet use, cyber 
criminals exploit vulnerabilities to steal sensitive information, disrupt services and commit  
fraud, causing financial and reputational damage to individuals, businesses and Governments. 
The Government of India recognises this critical challenge and has launched multiple 
initiatives such as the Indian Cyber Crime Coordination Centre (I4C), which f osters 
coordination between law enforcement agencies to investigate and curb cyber threats 
effectively. Efforts also include spreading awareness through social media campaigns, SMS 
alerts and public outreach programs targeting cyber safety for women, child ren and the 
general public. Technical measures like the Cyber Swachhta Kendra for malware detection 
and free removal tools, mandatory reporting of cybersecurity incidents and regular training 
programmes for officials to strengthen the country’s cyber resil ience. Additionally, the 
Government’s National Critical Information Infrastructure Protection Centre  (NCIIPC) 
safeguards vital digital infrastructure, while schemes like Cyber Crime Prevention against 
Women and Children (CCPWC) focus on cyber crimes agains t women and children groups. 
These comprehensive actions emphasize prevention, timely response and capacity building 
across India’s cyber defence landscape. 
1.4.2 On the international front, collaboration and updated legal frameworks are essential to 
confront cybercrime, which often transcends national borders. Many countries have revised 

 
 
6 
 
their cyber laws to impose stricter penalties and enhance enforcement. India’s  rise to Tier 1 
status on the Global Cybersecurity Index in 2024 reflects its commitment to global standards 
and cooperation. Indian organisations and security agencies are increasingly adopting 
artificial intelligence and machine learning technologies to detect and counter sophisticated 
cyber threats, while adversaries also exploit these technologies, necessitating continuous 
vigilance and innovation. Global dialogues focus on harmonising cybersecurity policies, 
sharing threat intelligence and promoting ca pacity building, ensuring a coordinated response 
to cyber threats worldwide. Ensuring cybersecurity is no longer just about protecting data but 
is central to maintaining public trust, economic stability and national security in a highly 
connected digital world. 
***** 
 
  

 
 
7 
 
Part II: Ramifications of Cybercrime 
2.1  Financial Consequences for Individuals and Organisations 
2.1.1 Cybercrime in India has evolved into a threat with serious financial consequ ences for 
individual and organis ations, as highlighted by various Ministries and organisations. At the 
national level, the scale of financial losses is immense. The Ministry of Home Affairs (MHA) 
in it s background note informed the C ommittee that National Cybercrime Reporting Portal 
(NCRP), since its inception on  30th August 2019 to 28 th November 2024, received an 
aggregate of over 53.93 lakh complaints, these reported incidents collectively account for a 
defrauded amount of around ₹31,594 crore. Cyber Financial Crimes form the largest 
component of the crime repor ted accounting for about 85% of the cases reported on NCRP. 
There is a growing pattern of cybercrime being used for funding organised criminal networks, 
fake job rackets and even human trafficking operations overseas. The Central Bureau of 
Investigation (CBI) highlighted that Indian citizens have been trafficked abroad and coerced 
into committing cybercrimes from foreign -controlled "scam factories", especially in 
Southeast Asia. This includes fake loan apps, call center -based extortion rackets and even 
human trafficking using cryptocurrency payments. 
2.1.2 India’s vast and rapidly growing digital  economy has created enormous 
vulnerabilities. The Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology (MeitY) and 
Department of Telecommunications (DoT) highlighted the rise of AI -generated 
misinformation, deepfakes used for financial manipulation and defa mation and mass -scale 
SIM-based fraud. SIM swaps and header spoofing, allow impersonation of legitimate services 
like banks, Government agencies, or police, which enable cyber criminals  intimidate and 
extort money from victims under the pretext of fabricat ed legal action and keep them under 
digital arrests. The I4C team highlighted multiple cases of “digital arrests” where 
cybercriminals pretended to be Government officials and coerced victims to transfer money 
by creating fear. CBI has registered cases of targeted impersonation scams where fraudsters 
pose as law enforcement officers to intimidate and extort money from victims under the 
pretext of fabricated legal action and keep them under digital arrests. 
2.1.3 The MHA highlighted that the ransomware has e merged as one of the most 
debilitating forms of cybercrime, with attackers encrypting sensitive data and demanding 
exorbitant ransoms for its release from organisations and individuals both. The cross -border 
nature of these attacks complicates law enforcem ent efforts, highlighting the need for robust 
international cooperation and legal frameworks. Similarly, distributed denial -of-service 
(DDoS) attacks, which overwhelm online services with malicious traffic, continue to target 
businesses, Government service s and critical infrastructure, causing significant operational 
disruptions. 
2.1.4 Organisationally, financial institutions are facing substantial operational disruptions. 
The RBI highlighted the risk posed by mule accounts and lack of end -to-end transactio n 
monitoring in small banks and cooperative institutions. The Department of Financial Services 
(DFS) revealed gaps in fraud, response time and inadequate internal escalation mechanisms 
among many banks. Additionally, National Payments Corporation of India (NPCI) pointed to 
an uptick in frauds involving UPI, QR codes and payment gateways due to compromised 
APIs, third-party aggregators and absence of robust customer education. 
2.1.5 Centre for Development of Advanced Computing ( C-DAC) highlighted that data 
breaches are also major concern, with hackers targeting businesses, healthcare institutions 

 
 
8 
 
and Government databases to leak personal and financial information on the dark web. High -
profile breaches have exposed millions of Indians to identity theft and fin ancial fraud, 
highlighting weak cybers ecurity measures in many organis ations. It also apprised the 
Committee of online investment and trading scams luring victims with promises of high 
returns through fake stock tips, cryptocurrency schemes or Ponzi apps. 
2.1.6 The consequences of cyber crimes extend deeply into individuals’ lives. The 
Department of School Education  & Literacy (DoSEL)  and the Department of Higher 
Education (DoHE) both flagged a sharp rise in cyberbullying, digital extortion and image -
based abuse among teenagers, especially girls. Deepfake misuse for generating obscene 
material, social media impersonation and sextortion were cited as recurring issues. 
2.1.7 Further, SIM swap/SIM closure frauds allow criminals to take control of victims' 
mobile numbers, enabling un authorised access to bank accounts and OTPs. The Telecom 
Regulatory Authority of India (TRAI) and Department of Telecommunications (DoT) flagged 
misuse of telecom infrastructure for executing cyber frauds, including SIM swapping, header 
spoofing and anonymous international calls routed via spoofed domestic networks. These 
attacks not only lead to financial theft but also compromise nationa l security and trust in 
digital platforms. Cybercrime today has become a multi-layered threat affecting the economy, 
organisational integrity and individual well -being. The urgency to treat it as a systemic issue 
requiring inter-ministerial, international and technological response cannot be overstated. 
2.2  Social and Psychological Effects of Cybercrime 
2.2.1 Cybercrime extends far beyond financial or institutional loss. It has profound and 
lasting social and psychological impacts on individuals, families and communities. The 
inputs received from the Ministry of Home Affairs (MHA) , Ministry of Education (DoSEL 
and DoHE), MeitY and law enforcement agencies such as CBI and I4C underscore the 
severity of these consequences. 
Psychological Trauma and Victim Shaming 
2.2.2 Victims of cyberstalking, identity theft, sextortion, and deepfake obscene content 
often suffer anxiety, depression, and trauma. Individuals are targeted through social media 
platforms using morphed or AI-generated explicit materials, frequently leading to self-blame, 
social withdrawal, and limited access to psychological support services. The Ministry of 
Home Affairs highlighted that artificial intelligence and machine learning are expected to 
shape the future of cyber threats, with technologies like deepfakes posing serious risks to 
public trust and democratic institutions. 
2.2.3 In its responses, the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology (MeitY) 
emphasized that while social media and digital platforms commonly facilitate unlawful 
activities, cybercrimes are not limited to specific platforms and can occur across diverse 
digital environments. The misuse of the constitutional right to freedom of speech and 
expression enables the proliferation of vulgar, indecent, slanderous, and obscene  content, 
including fabricated narratives and morphed images, which adversely affect societal norms 
and public perception. 
2.2.4 Inputs from the Department of School Education and Literacy (DoSEL) noted a rise 
in cyberbullying among school children, contributing to emotional distress and declining 
academic performance. Harassment in online spaces, especially when ignored by platform  

 
 
9 
 
moderators, leads to a breakdown in trust and discourages open communication. Many 
victims avoid reporting due to stigma or fear of disbelief. 
2.2.5 The Ministry of Home Affairs has also highlighted the investment and trading scams 
including fraudulent st ock market schemes, cryptocurrency frauds, and online job traps that 
exploit individuals financially. Digital arrest scams, wherein fraudsters impersonate law 
enforcement officers to fabricate accusations and extort funds, not only cause psychological 
harm but also diminish public confidence in legitimate institutions. These techniques fall 
under broader social engineering practices, where scammers exploit fear, urgency, and 
emotional vulnerability to manipulate targets. Dating and romance scams further com pound 
this harm, destabilizing personal relationships and undermining trust in online interactions. 
Collectively, such cyber threats result in financial and emotional distress, diminish 
interpersonal engagement, and contribute to the erosion of institution al credibility and social 
trust across digital ecosystems. 
Youth Involvement in Illegal Digital Activities 
2.2.6 As informed by  DoSEL, the effects of cybercrime on children can vary from poor 
academic performance to serious mental health issues. School stu dents may face mental 
health issues like anxiety, depression, low self -esteem due to cyberbullying. Student’s online 
addiction may affect their studies leading to academic performance drop, distraction and fear. 
Their privacy may be compromised due to shar ing of personal data leading to identity theft. 
Children who engage in hacking or abusive behaviour may not only face juvenile legal 
consequences but also risk becoming socially isolated from real-world interactions. 
2.2.7 It was revealed to the Committee by MHA that, unemployed youngsters both men and 
women are recruited by international cyber crime gangs for illegal activities such as 
managing mule accounts or sharing pirated content. C -DAC informed the Committee that 
fake job scams exploit unemployed youth by offering fraudulent employment opportunities in 
exchange for registration fees or personal data. Call centre and crypto frauds operate through 
illegal call centres that dupe people into investing in fake crypto schemes or fake refund 
scams, causing massive financial losses. When caught, youth suffer reputational damage and 
are psychologically impacted by legal action and social isolation.  
2.2.8 Cybercrime increasingly contributes to mental health deterioration, disempowerment 
and social marginalizati on. Addressing this requires integrated victim support systems, 
psychological counseling and sensitised cybercrime reporting mechanisms. 
2.3  National Security and Cross-Border Implications 
2.3.1 Cybercrime today is no longer a mere law - and -order challenge but has evolved into 
a major concern for national security, with implications that go beyond borders. Various 
agencies such as the Ministry of External Affairs (MEA), Central Bureau of Investig ation 
(CBI), Department of Telecommunications (DoT) and I4C have highlighted that transnational 
cybercriminal syndicates pose serious threats to sovereignty and internal stability. These 
syndicates along with cyberterrorism and geopolitical threats are res haping the cybercrime 
landscape as a full-fledged national security concern.  
  

 
 
10 
 
Transnational Nature of Cybercrime 
2.3.2 In today’s hyper -connected digital environment, cybercrime has evolved into a 
transnational threat that challenges conventional notions  of jurisdiction and sovereignty. 
Malicious actors exploit asymmetries across national legal frameworks, regulatory systems 
and law enforcement capacities to conduct a wide range of illicit operations including 
hacking, financial fraud, data breaches and d isinformation campaigns. The transnational 
dynamics of cybercrime can be analyzed through four principal dimensions: 
a)  Cross-Border Operations 
2.3.3 Cybercriminals commonly operate across multiple jurisdictions, leveraging regulatory 
gaps and uneven enfo rcement capabilities. These actors may deploy infrastructure (e.g., 
command- and-control servers) in one country, initiate attacks from another and target 
victims located elsewhere creating substantial barriers to timely and coordinated legal action. 
b)  Convergence of Criminal Enterprises 
2.3.4 There is increasing convergence between cyber -enabled crimes and traditional 
criminal enterprises. The latest United Nation Office on Drugs and Crime ( UNODC) report 
on Southeast Asia underscores how organized crim e syndicates combine digital fraud 
schemes with underground banking and sophisticated money laundering mechanisms. 
Cybercriminals often utilise unregulated virtual asset service providers and informal financial 
channels to obfuscate the origin and movement of illicit funds. 
c)  Jurisdictional Complexities 
2.3.5 Disparities in legal definitions, investigatory frameworks and extradition 
arrangements among nations obstruct the seamless prosecution of cyber offenders. These 
challenges have contributed to the proliferation of safe havens jurisdictions where criminals 
operate with relative impunity due to the absence of harmonized cybercrime laws and limited 
cross-border cooperation. 
d)  Emergent Transnational Threats 
2.3.6 The increasing use of cryptocurrencies  to facilitate anonymous financial flows, the 
expansion of dark web marketplaces and the commercialization of cybercrime services 
(cybercrime-as-a-service or CaaS) have intensified the international scope of digital 
criminality. Southeast Asia, in particul ar, has emerged as a hotspot for new illicit service 
ecosystems offering phishing kits, ransomware variants, deepfake technologies and even 
“Money Laundering-as-a-Service” to transnational criminal networks. 
e) Case Illustrations of Transnational Dimensions of Cybercrime 
2.3.7 The Ministry of Home Affairs has highlighted several instances of cyber -enabled 
fraud with clear transnational implications: 
 September 2023 (Assam): In collaboration with the I4C, Assam Police dismantled 
eight i llegal call centers inv olved in multi -crore scams targeting domestic and 
international victims via fake pop-ups and toll-free numbers. 

 
 
11 
 
 May 2024 (Punjab): Punjab Police dismantled five unauthorised call centers operating 
in SAS Nagar which were involved in fraudulent schemes that targeted U.S. citizens. 
The operation led to the arrest of 155 individuals and led to the identification of 336 
victims in the United States based on digital forensic evidence. 
 April 2024 (Jharkhand): With I4C support, Cyber Crime Police Station Ranchi 
exposed a fraudulent scheme targeting elderly citizens in the United Kingdom and 
Australia. Criminals impersonated representatives of British Telecom and Telstra to 
deceive victims. 
 October 2024 (Odisha): Cyber PS Bhubaneswar, aided by I4C, intercepted an 
international tech support scam involving impersonation of Norton and McAfee 
representatives. The operation resulted in arrests and seizure of key digital assets. 
2.3.8 Cybercrime Networks and Human Trafficking: Implications for India 
1. Human Trafficking via Employment Fraud 
Recruitment of Indian nationals, primarily unemployed youth, with basic digital and 
language proficiency, through job advertisements circulated on platforms such as 
Google, Facebook, Instagram and Telegram. Recruits are lured with employment 
offers in South East Asia region, charged substantial fees for travel arrangements and 
subsequently coerced into digital fraud operations under exploitative conditions. 
Reports confirm physical abuse, confiscation of identity documents and ransom -like 
extortion for release. 
2. SIM Procurement and WhatsApp Fraud Infrastructure 
Fraudsters acquire Indian SIM cards through intermediaries operating shell entities, 
enabling bulk activation of WhatsApp accounts. These devices are couriered to scam 
centers abroad for impersonation -based fra ud. Foreign SIM cards are also used to 
engage Indian targets, further complicating traceability. 
3. Recruitment of Mule Account Operators 
Indian citizens are solicited via Telegram and WhatsApp to provide bank accounts for 
use in laundering proceeds. These accounts are misused to route fraud funds, typically 
in exchange for a commission. 
4. Financial Layering and Laundering Mechanisms 
Proceeds from fraud are laundered through multi -modal financial infrastructure, 
including: 
 Peer-to-peer cryptocurrency transactions 
 Purchase of physical assets (e.g., gold) 
 Offshore cash withdrawal points  
 International fintech platforms  
 
  

 
 
12 
 
Cross-Border Financial Laundering and Mule Accounts 
2.3.9  Cyber fraud proceeds are laundered through mule accounts and international 
channels. Funds are rapidly transferred abroad using crypto wallets or foreign shell 
companies. According to CBI, law enforcement agencies face delays due to lack of reciprocal 
data access agreements and divergent legal frameworks. 
Threats to Critical Infrastructure 
2.3.10 Multiple agencies including MeitY, CERT-In (Indian Computer Emergency Response 
Team) and I4C highlighted attacks on Critical Infrastructure via ransomware. These attacks 
aim to paralyse essential services and extract cryptocurrency ransoms. Some originated from 
IPs traced to foreign hostile actors, raising alarm over cyber warfare risks. 
Use of the Dark Web and Encrypted Channels for Illicit Trade 
2.3.11 Inputs from CBI and NIA point to an increase in use  of the dark web for narcotics, 
arms, fake IDs and child exploitation material. Messaging apps with end -to-end encryption 
and virtual private networks (VPNs) are used to plan illicit activitie s, including recruitment 
into extremist networks. This undermines law enforcement visibility and complicates 
evidence gathering. The misuse of cryptocurrencies to purchase weapons, conduct payments 
for illicit jobs and pay ransomware has blurred the line b etween economic crime and national 
threat. 
Diplomatic and Legal Challenges 
2.3.12 While the MEA has signed several MoUs (Memorandum of Understanding) on cyber 
cooperation, coordination on investigations and data access but extradition remains slow. It 
was acknowledged in the Committee meeting that the lack of standardised global frameworks 
makes it difficult for countries or organisations to work together quickly and effectively in 
real time. Cybercrime suspects often escape justice due to loopholes in juri sdiction and 
protocol. Cybercrime now intersects with national sovereignty, critical infrastructure safety 
and international diplomacy. India must invest in cyber diplomacy, cross -border treaties and 
real-time intelligence exchange to protect its digital borders. 
2.4  Erosion of Trust in Digital Services 
2.4.1 The rising frequency and complexity of cybercrimes in India may erode citizens' trust 
in digital platforms, e -governance services and even mobile -based financial infrastructure. 
The growing consumer fear and perception of vulnerability could stall the progress of Digital 
India if not adequately addressed. 
2.4.2 India’s digital economy has witnessed transformative growth through initiatives like 
Digital India, Unified Payments Interface (UPI) and mobile -first public services. However, a 
parallel and alarming rise in cybercrime now threatens to undermine this progr ess. 
Increasingly sophisticated scams ranging from phishing and QR code frauds to deepfake -
based harassment are instilling fear and mistrust in digital platforms among Indian citizens. 
Unless cybersecurity becomes a foundational aspect of the nation’s digi tal architecture, the 
ambitious goal of inclusive digital empowerment could falter. Frauds in the UPI ecosystem 
have potential to reduce user confidence in QR code payments, especially among the elderly 
and less digitally literate populations and hence sma ll merchants and senior citizens are 
reverting to cash due to distrust in UPI security. 

 
 
13 
 
2.4.3 Instances of fake websites impersonating Government portals as well as manipulated 
images on social media platforms can damage perceptions of digital safety. In p articular, the 
harassment of women through deepfake-based image distortion and social extortion has led to 
increased cyber trauma and privacy anxiety and hence it damages trust in social media and 
public communication. 
2.4.4 School-going children have beco me targets of inappropriate content and fake 
educational resources, leading to parental reluctance to engage with online learning solutions.  
Similarly, higher education institutions have been victims of ransomware attacks. Parents are 
increasingly hesitant to allow online learning or app -based schooling. Universities and public 
research institutes have faced ransomware and data breach attacks. Weak cyber hygiene and 
under-resourced IT infrastructure make institutions easy targets. 
2.4.5 According to RBI, ba nks and financial institutions face a trust deficit when OTPs or 
transaction alerts fail to prevent unauthorised debits. In many cases, the customer support 
systems in these institutions are either overburdened or ineffective in promptly reversing 
damages, thereby compounding the erosion of trust. 
2.4.6 A significant contributor to this phenomenon is the lack of visible accountability or 
swift enforcement. While victims may report to helplines such as 1930 or portals like 
cybercrime.gov.in, follow-ups are often delayed due to inter -jurisdictional challenges or lack 
of real -time investigation frameworks. As a result, citizens feel that justice in cybercrime 
cases is slow and uncertain. Online grievance mechanisms are often slow, under -coordinated 
and difficul t to access. Women and youth, key drivers of digital uptake, are especially 
impacted by safety concerns.  Unless cybersecurity becomes a fundamental element of user 
experience, the promise of inclusive digital growth will remain limited. 
2.4.7 The National Cybercrime Reporting Portal (NCRP) serves as the primary national 
platform for reporting cybercrime incidents, enabling citizens to register complaints through 
two key channels: the online portal accessible at www.cybercrime.gov.in and the Nat ional 
Cybercrime Helpline, reachable via the toll-free number 1930. The F igure below represents 
the overview of cybercrime complaints reported on NCRP and corresponding FIRs registered 
(*till 28th November 2024)

2.5 Emerging Threats – CaaS, AI-Based Tools, Cloud Vulnerabilities 
2.5.1 India’s digital transformation has ushered in unprecedented growth  and connectivity, 
but it has also exposed the nation to a new wave of cyber threats. Cybercrime has evolved 
from isolated acts of online fraud into an industrial-scale, service-driven ecosystem supported 
by technological advances like Crime -as-a-Service (CaaS), generative AI, deepfakes, botnets 
and cloud computing vulnerabilities. Inputs from key Government agencies including MeitY, 
C-DAC, I4C, MHA, DoT, DoHE and CERT -In underline, the urgent need to realig n India’s 
cybersecurity framework to changing need. 


 
 
15 
 
2.5.2 Cybercrime-as-a-Service (CaaS) has significantly changed how cybercriminals 
function. Much like Software -as-a-Service (SaaS) where companies offer software tools 
online for easy access, CaaS platfor ms provide ready -made cybercrime tools to anyone 
willing to pay. These services ranging from ransomware kits and phishing -as-a-service 
platforms to AI -driven deepfake generators and DDoS attack tools allow individuals with 
minimal technical skill to launch  sophisticated and large -scale attacks . By lowering the 
technical barriers, CaaS has made cybercrime more scalable, and harder to trace intensifying 
threats to personal privacy, institutional integrity and global cybersecurity. According to 
MeitY and CERT-In, this has lowered the barrier of entry for cybercriminals, allowing even 
technologically ill-equipped actors to commit large-scale frauds. 
2.5.3 Artificial Intelligence (AI), especially generative models is increasingly used to 
mimic voices and faces to perpetrate fraud. There are cases in which people are being tricked 
into transferring funds by video call with an AI -generated voice and image of a known 
contact. These attacks are extremely difficult to detect and challenge conventional forensic 
approaches. 
2.5.4 MHA emphasized that  the growing use of cryptocurrency for money laundering, the 
rise of dark web marketplaces and the increased deployment of crime -as-a-service (CaaS) 
models have amplified the international dimension of cybercrime. Southeast Asia, in 
particular, has become a testing ground for new criminal service economies, where 
specialized services such as phishing kits, ransomware tools, deepfake generators and Money 
Laundering as a service are readily available to transnational criminal groups. 
2.5.5 Moreover, the educa tion sector has inadvertently enabled some of these threats. As 
per the Department of Higher Education (DoHE), coding bootcamps and unsupervised digital 
learning platforms may unintentionally equip youth with advanced technical skills, some of 
which are diverted towards malicious activities. Thus, the role of digital ethics in curriculum  
is more important than ever. 
2.5.6 Cloud computing, which underpins a large part of India’s digital infrastructure, is also 
a growing target. CERT -In reported multiple data  breaches at hospitals and universities that 
were caused by misconfigured cloud settings or lack of encryption. These vulnerabilities are 
compounded by the lack of in -house cybersecurity teams in most public and semi -public 
institutions. 
2.5.7 Additionally, botnet attacks targeting IoT devices, routers and mobile networks were 
flagged by DoT and TRAI. As 5G expands, the number of attack vectors increases 
exponentially. Cybercriminals can potentially hijack autonomous vehicles, smart homes and 
connected industrial machines, posing both physical and economic threats. 
2.5.8 The nature of cybercrime is shifting rapidly and unpredictably. India’s regulatory and 
technical readiness must evolve to incorporate real -time intelligence, ethical AI regulation, 
cloud security auditing and cross -border enforcement mechanisms. Strong measures need to 
be taken, so that emerging technologies will not be vulnerable to exploitation and may not be 
taken over by criminals before security systems can catch up to protect against such misuse. 
***** 

 
 
16 
 
Part III: Existing Protection Mechanisms and 
Role of Key Institutions 
 
3.1.1 India’s cybercrime response relies on a multi -institutional framework where each 
institution plays a specialized role. At the forefront, the Indian Cyber Crime  Coordination 
Centre (I4C) under the Ministry of Home Affairs coordinates national law enforcement 
efforts, manages the National Cybercrime Reporting Portal (NCRP) and drives investigative 
support for complex offences. The Ministry of Electronics and Infor mation Technology 
(MeitY) is instrumental in policy formation, digital governance regulations and overall 
cybersecurity strategy. Under MeitY, the Indian  Computer Emergency Response Team 
(CERT-In) acts as the nodal agency for handling and responding to cyb ersecurity incidents, 
issuing guidelines and ensuring rapid reporting and mitigation of breaches. The National 
Critical Information Infrastructure Protection Centre (NCIIPC) focuses on safeguarding 
critical digital infrastructure in s ectors like energy, fi nance, telecom, etc . Key sectoral 
regulators such as the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) supervise cybersecurity norms for banks 
and financial institutions, while the Department of Telecommunications (DoT) and the 
Telecom Regulatory Authority of India (TRAI) u phold cyber resilience across the telecom 
sector. Financial and capital markets are further protected by SEBI, the roles and mandates of 
the organisations, along with the contributions of relevant Ministries, are explained in detail 
in the following paragraph. 
3.2 The Ministry of Electronics & Information Technology (MeitY) 
3.2.1 The Ministry of Electronics & Information Technology (MeitY) has been at the 
forefront of India’s efforts to prevent and address cybercrime. MeitY has implemented a 
multi-pronged s trategy centered on strengthening the national cyber ecosystem, increasing 
public awareness and fostering institutional capabilities. MeitY spearheads nationwide cyber 
hygiene and online safety campaigns such as the extensive ‘Safer Internet Day’ initiativ e 
reaching lakhs of beneficiaries through workshops, expert sessions and targeted messaging in 
regional lan guages. It partners with organis ations like National Informatics Centre (NIC), 
Centre for Development of Advanced Computing (C -DAC) and other key institutions to 
promote responsible digital behaviour, introduce cybersecurity best practices and disseminate 
information about reporting mech anisms like the national cybercrime helpline. Further, 
MeitY develops policies and frameworks such as the National Cyber Security Policy to 
secure information infrastructure, support sectoral crisis response, enhance law enforcement’s 
response capabilities and oversee indigenous security technology development. It also leads 
projects like the Information Security Education and Awareness (ISEA) programme to build 
a skilled cybersecurity workforce and empower Government, industry and society against 
emerging threats. MeitY’s initiatives include the establishment of Cyber Swachhta Kendra 
(CSK) for malware and botnet detection and tools for safe digital practices. 
3.2.2 Recognizing the evolving nature of cyber threats, MeitY has adopted a proactive 
approach to c ybercrime prevention and enhancing digital safety. It regularly consults with 
various Ministries, Departments, Commissions, intermediary platforms/social media 
platforms and industry bodies to ensure effective implementation and compliance with the 
provisions of the Information Technology Act, 2000 (IT Act) and the related rules. MeitY 
also continuously reviews the existing legal framework to identify and address gaps, 
emphasizing the role of the IT Act and the Information Technology (Intermediary Guideline s 
and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021 (IT Rules, 2021) as key instruments in 
regulating harmful and unlawful online content and promoting a safer digital environment. 

 
 
17 
 
Legal Framework for addressing Cybercrimes under IT Act, 2000 
3.2.3 IT Act, 2000 a nd its rules provide a robust legal structure to safeguard users from a 
wide range of cyber offences in India. The Act lists 18 specific cybercrimes, of which six are 
non-bailable and twelve are bailable, with punishments ranging from one year to life 
imprisonment depending on the nature and severity of the offence. IT Act, 2000 applies 
uniformly to offences involving digital information, whether generated by users or through 
AI tools, without making any distinction between the two. Offences covered include  
tampering with computer source documents (Section 65), hacking (Section 66), identity theft 
(Section 66C), cheating by impersonation (Section 66D), violation of privacy (Section 66E), 
cyber terrorism (Section 66F) and publishing or transmitting obscene or  sexually explicit 
material (Sections 67, 67A and 67B), among others. Further, the Act addresses non -
compliance with Government directions on interception, monitoring and blocking of 
information (Sections 69, 69A and 69B) and unauthorised access to protect ed systems 
(Section 70). Section 77B clarifies the bailable and cognizable nature of offences based on 
the prescribed punishment and Section 78 provides that a police officer not below the rank of 
Inspector is empowered to investigate any offence under the IT Act, 2000.  
Cybersecurity issues addressed under the IT Act, 2000 
3.2.4 India’s cybersecurity framework is rooted in the IT Act, 2000 which, along with its 
various rules, continues to adapt to the ever -evolving challenges in the digital realm. A 
significant aspect of this category is designating any computer resource as a “protected 
system” and the obligations to be followed by organisations thereto once a computer resource 
is recognised as a protected system. Critical Information Infrastructure (CII) m eans 
“Computer Resource, the incapacitation or destruction of which, shall have debilitating 
impact on National Security, Economy, Public Health or Safety”. Protected systems are a 
subset of CII and are notified by the Government, with the National Critica l Information 
Infrastructure Protection Centre (NCIIPC) serving as the nodal agency for their protectio n. 
NCIIPC supports these organis ations by providing threat intelligence, risk assessments, 
advisories, vulnerability reports, incident response, capacity  building and cross -sectoral 
knowledge sharing.   
3.2.5 The protection of cyber security in India is primarily overseen by the Indian 
Computer Emergency Response Team ( CERT-In) for addressing cybersecurity incidents, 
issuing preventive directions and opera ting platforms like the National Cyber Coordination 
Centre (NCCC) and Cyber Swachhta Kendra for real -time threat detection and user 
awareness. These agencies have wide-ranging powers to secure India’s cyberspace, including 
prescribing best practices and ca lling for information from digital entities. As cyber threats 
grow more sophisticated, such as through phishing and malicious applications impersonating 
banks, India’s cybersecurity apparatus responds by evolving its strategies, enforcing 
regulations and d isseminating timely warnings to protect both organis ations and individual 
users. 
Regulating Intermediaries and Enhancing Online Safety through the IT Rules, 2021 
3.2.6 The Information Technology (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics 
Code) Rules, 2021, notified by MeitY under the IT Act, 2000 establish a comprehensive 
regulatory framework to ensure accountability and safety on digital platforms in India. These  
rules impose specific legal obligations on intermediaries, especially social media platforms, 
to prevent the misuse of technology including AI for spreading unlawful content such as 
obscenity, misinformation, impersonation, hate speech and content threate ning India’s 

 
 
18 
 
sovereignty. Key provisions include prohibiting certain types of content, mandating 
immediate account termination for violators and requiring time -bound removal of unlawful 
information. The rules empower users, especially women and children, b y facilitating 
grievance redressal mechanisms with strict timelines and appellate Committees for 
unresolved complaints. They also require significant social media intermediaries to enable 
traceability of the first originator of unlawful messages, deploy au tomated tools to detect and 
curb harmful content and appoint designated officers in India to ensure regulatory 
compliance. Failure to comply causes intermediaries to lose safe harbour protection, exposing 
them to legal action. The Government has recently g iven clear instructions to intermediaries, 
such as social media platforms, to take strong action against unlawful content, including fake 
and malicious material created using AI, like synthetic media and deepfakes. These 
instructions remind intermediaries that they are responsible for preventing the spread of 
unfair and harmful content. They must also make it simple for users to report such content 
and respond quickly by removing it. 
Measures to address Misinformation and Deepfakes under IT Rules.  
3.2.7 MeitY has issued advisories on 26.12.2023 and 15.03.2024 under the Information 
Technology (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021 (IT Rules, 
2021) to address issues of misinformation, particularly AI -generated deepfakes and illega l 
loan and betting apps. MeitY emphasized a zero -tolerance policy on unlawful content, 
requiring intermediaries and platforms to ensure compliance with Rule 3(1)(b) of the IT 
Rules, which requires the intermediary to inform their rules and regulations, pri vacy policy 
and user agreement to the user in English or any language specified in the Eighth Schedule to 
the Constitution in the language of user’s choice and to make reasonable efforts to prevent 
users from publishing certain categories of unlawful conte nt, such as misinformation, 
obscene or content that promotes viol ence based on religion or caste. These advisories 
mandate intermediaries to clearly communicate prohibited content to users at registration and 
login, enable easy reporting of violations including deepfakes, promptly remove 
misinformation or impersonation content, comply with grievance committee orders, etc. 
Further, intermediaries must label synthetic or deepfake content with unique metadata for 
identification, restrict unlawful advertisements such as those for illegal loans and betting apps 
and inform users of legal consequences under the applicable laws.  
3.2.8 A subsequent advisory on 03.09.2024, following a Bombay High Court directive, 
required intermediaries to proactively and swiftly remove or disable morphed videos and fake 
profiles related to specific litigation without waiting for prescribed time limits, enhancing 
responsiveness to fake content on platforms. The relevant part of advisory is as following: 
 
“Hon’ble High Court of Bombay in INTERIM APPLICATION (L) NO. 21456 of 2024 in 
COM IPR SUIT (L) NO.21111 of 2024 in the matter of National Stock Exchange of India Ltd. 
vs. Meta Platforms, Inc. & Ors. has taken note of this. The court, while emphasising the duty 
of intermediaries under IT Rules 2021 to take prompt action on the complaints of ent ities like 
the National Stock Exchange of India about unauthorised use of their trademark on dubious 
webpages, profiles, accounts, content, social media groups and /or channels, directed the 
concerned intermediaries to delete or disable the fake informatio n such as morphed videos 
and profiles circulating on their platform relating to the plaintiff promptly within ten hours of 
receiving such complaint.” 
 

 
 
19 
 
3.2.9 Further, on 25.10.2024, MeitY issued an advisory addressing malicious hoax bomb 
threats to airlines , underlining intermediaries' due diligence obligations to promptly remove 
such misinformation that threatens public order and state security. This advisory stressed that 
failure to act removes the safe harbor protection under Section 79 of the IT Act, 200 0. 
Moreover, intermediaries must report offences related to threats against India's unity, 
integrity, security, or economic security under the Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023 
and provide timely information to authorised Government agencies for inv estigation and 
cybersecurity purposes, within stipulated timeframes (not later than 72 hours).  
Public-Private Partnerships Initiatives for Strengthening Cybersecurity 
3.2.10  In response to a question by the Committee, MeitY has given that it has undertaken 
several strategic initiatives in recent years to foster robust public -private partnerships (PPPs) 
aimed at strengthening cooperation with private sector companies, international partners and 
stakeholders to combat cyber threats. Among these init iatives, MeitY launched the Cyber 
Surakshit Bharat (CSB) programme in a PPP mode to educate and empower Chief 
Information Security Officers (CISOs) and broader IT community across Central and State 
Governments, banks and PSUs to address cybersecurity chall enges effectively. So far, a total 
of 1,662 CISOs and senior IT officers from various entities have been trained under this 
programme, enhancing the cyber readiness of critical Government and public institutions. 
3.2.11  Further, MeitY has established the National Centre of Excellence (NCoE) in Cyber 
Security in collaboration with the Data Security Council of India (DSCI). The NCoE’s 
primary objective is to position India as a leading cybersecurity hub by accelerating the 
identification and development of c ybersecurity technologies, catering to current market 
demands and preparing for future challenges. The Centre has significantly contributed to the 
growth of the cybersecurity ecosystem by accelerating 153 startups across multiple 
cybersecurity domains. The se initiatives collectively demonstrate MeitY’s commitment to 
building national cyber resilience through strategic partnerships and capacity building. 
Recommendation/Observation 
3.2.12  The Committee appreciates the comprehensive legislative framework prov ided 
by the Information Technology Act, 2000 (IT Act) and its associated rules, which 
collectively serve as the cornerstone for regulating digital platforms and safeguarding 
Indian cyberspace.  
3.2.13  The Committee observes that currently the existing law does not differentiate 
between synthetically generated and user -created content. Considering the increasing 
misuse of deepfakes and AI tools for generating harmful or misleading content, the 
Committee recommends that the legal framework be further strengthened to include 
express provisions in the legislation for handling content generated using Artificial 
Intelligence (AI) and similar emerging technologies. The Committee further 
recommends that to address issues of deepfake or obscene content being uploaded in the 
social media, MeitY should consider developing an innovative technological framework 
mandating all photos, videos and similar content shared on digital platforms to have a 
watermark as it would help to prove the origin of the content and make it more difficult 
to fake or edit/manipulate. The Committee is of the view that to make this initiative 
functional, MeitY should set up uniform technical standards for media provenance, 
while CERT -In would act as the coordinator for monitoring and issuing detection 
alerts.    

 
 
20 
 
3.2.14  The Committee is of the view that the grievance redressal system should be 
made more user -friendly and transparent to enhance public trust and ensure effective 
resolution of complaints. The Committee, therefore, recommends establishing a uniform 
format for filing complaints, setting clearly defined timelines for acknowledgment and 
resolution, and ensuring public disclosure of grievance statistics, including the number 
of complaints received, resolved and pending. The Committee further recommends that 
integrating the grievance redressal mechanism system with emerging data protection 
frameworks would help to create a more holistic regulatory environment that addresses 
concerns relat ed to privacy, content moderation and cybersecurity in a cohesive and 
coordinated manner. 
3.2.15  Recognising the challenges posed by technologies such as the metaverse, 
blockchain and generative AI, the Committee recommends the development of forward-
looking, flexible regulatory guidelines. The Committee is of the view that the safe 
harbour protections available to intermediaries under the IT Act, 2000 should be 
reviewed periodically to strike a balance between their liability immunity and the need 
for gre ater accountability, especially where platforms fail to act on unlawful content. 
The Committee also recommends a periodic review of enforcement mechanisms and 
penalty provisions under the IT Act, 2000 to ensure their deterrent effect remains 
robust.  
3.2.16  The Committee has observed that certain social media intermediaries at times 
fail to cooperate with law enforcement agencies and legal provisions in promptly 
removing unlawful content, including morphed videos, fake profiles, misinformation 
and content promoting violence based on religion or caste, thereby potentially abetting 
criminal activities and undermining public order. To address this critical gap, the 
Committee recommends that the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology, 
in consultatio n with the Ministry of Home Affairs and Ministry of Law and Justice, 
should consider amending the Information Technology Act, 2000, to introduce specific 
provisions that hold social media intermediaries legally accountable as when they fail to 
comply with lawful takedown orders within prescribed timelines. Such amendments 
should include graded penalties, including monetary fines and potential suspension of 
operations for persistent non -compliance, while ensuring that due process and appeal 
mechanisms are pr eserved to maintain the balance between content regulation and 
freedom of expression. 
3.2.17 The Committee notes that the OTT platforms have emerged as a primary source 
of entertainment in India, with a reach that surpasses traditional cinema, including a 
substantial audience of minors. The Committee believes that unlike films, which require 
mandatory pre -certification under the Cinematograph Act, OTT content currently 
operates under a self -classification system with post -release grievance redressal as per 
the IT (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021. The 
Committee fur ther believes that although the rules require age ratings, parental 
controls and a grievance mechanism, the lack of pre-release checks and weak age 
verification still leave minors exposed to harmful content. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Go vernment may consider establishment of a recognized panel of 
experts comprising child development specialists, educators, legal experts, social 
scientists and community representatives who will monitor the new OTT releases. The 
Committee is of the view tha t the Post Release Review Panel will watch the flagged or 
trending content post release based on user complaints submitted through a 
standardised grievance mechanism. The Committee recommends that the Panel shall 

 
 
21 
 
devise suitable norms and guidelines keeping in view the cultural ethos and sensitivity of 
the country. The Committee further recommends that the panel may also workout 
suitable penalties in case of infringement of these norms/guidelines. The Committee also 
recommends that OTT platforms must be req uired to adopt more strong technology 
enabled age verification systems and effective parental control  mechanisms, going 
beyond simple self -declaration, to better restrict minors' access to age -inappropriate 
material. The Committee is of the view that regio nal language content classification and 
parental guidance notices should be mandated to offer better protection for rural and 
semi-urban viewers. 
3.2.18  The Committee is of the view that enhanced coordination between 
Governmental agencies, regulators, tec hnology platforms and civil society would be 
beneficial to strengthen the country’s ability to respond to cyber challenges. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends the establishment of formal ways for these groups 
to work together for knowledge sharing, joint  decision-making and capacity building in 
the cyberspace domain. 
3.2.19  The Committee recommends that all apps should comply with a strict data 
protection and security standards as prescribed in applicable laws and regulations. The 
Committee also suggests  setting compliance requirements for app stores, including 
regular audits to prevent malicious or harmful apps from being distributed. The 
Committee also recommends for development of an indigenous app store to facilitate 
Indian startups. 
3.3  The Ministry of Information and Broadcasting (MIB) 
3.3.1 The Ministry of Information and Broadcasting (MIB) plays a crucial role in 
combating cybercrimes through its regulatory oversight and public awareness initiatives 
within the digital media environment. By enforci ng the Information Technology 
(Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021, MIB regulates 
publishers of news and current affairs on digital platforms as well as Over -the-Top (OTT) 
content providers. This regulatory framework ensures that digital content complies with 
ethical standards, preventing the spread of unlawful or harmful material that may promote 
cybercrime. 
3.3.2 To address grievances related to digital content, MIB has implemented a three -tier 
redressal mechanism. This syst em begins with self -regulation by publishers, followed by 
supervision from self -regulating bodies and finally oversight by a Government -led 
Committee, ensuring timely resolution of complaints and fostering safe digital media usage. 
Further, MIB’s regulatio n of online advertisements enhances transparency and helps curb 
misleading or harmful content that could contribute to cybercrimes. 
3.3.3 In addition, MIB actively promotes cyber safety awareness through mass media 
campaigns conducted via television, radio and digital platforms. These initiatives aim to 
educate the public on safe internet practices and the importance of cyber hygiene. Within the 
broadcasting sector, MIB ensures compliance with the Cable Television Networks 
(Regulation) Act , 1995, thereby preventing the transmission of content that might incite 
cybercrime or spread misinformation. The Ministry’s oversight of licensing for Direct -to-
Home (DTH) and cable operators serves as an additional preventive measure against the 
misuse of broadcast networks. Further support from the Central Bureau of Communication 
(CBC) bolsters these initiatives by encouraging the responsible use of digital media. The 

 
 
22 
 
Electronic Media Monitoring Centre (EMMC) monitors broadcast content to ensure 
compliance with content standards and to detect materials that could facilitate cybercrime.  
Recommendation/Observation 
3.3.4 The Committee is of the view to introduce a strong verification system for 
offshore advertisers. The Committee recommends that the offshor e advertisers must 
undergo a thorough digital verification process, like uploading official documents, 
completing a live video identification, etc., to prove their identity and must use zero -
trust frameworks during verification, where every access request from offshore 
advertisers (including changes to ads, payment details, or identity information) must be 
continuously authenticated and monitored to curb fraudulent and deceptive advertising 
targeting Indian users. 
3.4  Department of Telecommunications (DoT) 
3.4.1  The Department of Telecommunications (DoT) has taken significant measures to curb 
the rising misuse of telecom resources in cybercrime and financial fraud. Cybercriminals 
have been found exploiting mobile connections, often obtained through fake documents or 
rented from unsuspecting individuals, to send fraudulent calls, impersonate officials on 
platforms, like WhatsApp and conduct social engineering scams. These mobile numbers are 
sometimes linked with bank accounts to facilitate illicit financial transact ions, allowing 
fraudsters to deceive citizens and siphon off money.  
3.4.2  To tackle this growing threat, the DoT focused initially on enabling citizens to report 
such fraudulent activities easily. As a part of this effort, the Government launched the 
Sanchar Saathi portal and mobile app, a citizen -centric initiative that empowers mobile users 
to take control of their digital identities. Available on both  android, iOS and via a web portal, 
Sanchar Saathi offers various services including the ability to re port suspected fraud 
communications and unsolicited commercials; identify and deactivate unauthorised mobile 
connections issued in the user’s name; report lost or stolen mobile phones to block or trace 
them; verify the authenticity of new or second -hand  m obile devices; flag international calls 
appearing with Indian caller IDs; and find details about wireline internet service providers. 
By crowdsourcing data on suspected fraud, this platform has become an important tool in the 
national strategy against tele com-related frauds. Beyond empowering individuals, the DoT 
also brought together over 620 key stakeholders on a common platform called the Digital 
Intelligence Platform (DIP). This platform now connects law enforcement agencies, Central 
and State -level authorities, banks, telecom service providers (TSPs), digital apps and other 
relevant partners to enable seamless data sharing and collaborative analytics. 
3.4.3 In a reply submitted to the Committee, DoT has mentioned that currently there is no 
legal framework in place to verify whether a mobile number seeded for availing a service 
(such as in a bank account) actually belongs to the person in whose name the account is 
opened. This gap is often exploited for fraudulent activities. To address this, DoT has 
proposed draft amendment rules to amend the Telecommunications (Telecom Cyber 
Security) Rules, 2024, seeking public consultation. The proposed amendment aims to 
establish a Mobile Number Validation (MNV) platform, which will enable authorised entities 
and licensees those using telecommunication identifiers for customer identification or service 
provisioning to validate that the mobile number corresponds to the person recorded in their 
database. 

 
 
23 
 
3.4.4 This initiative is expected to bridge the current verificatio n gap and help prevent the 
misuse of telecom resources in cyber fraud and crimes. In terms of enforcement, DoT has 
specified that any violation of KYC and validation requirements by telecom operators will be 
addressed in accordance with the Telecommunicati ons Act, 2023. To ensure a balance 
between enhanced security measures and protection of user privacy, the proposed amendment 
mandates that Telecommunication Identifier User Entities (TIUEs), authorised entities and 
licensees must comply with applicable dat a protection laws for mobile number validation 
purpose.  
3.4.5 To bolster its anti-fraud measures, DoT has implemented the Centralized International 
Out Roamer (CIOR) system, which identifies and blocks international spoof calls that falsely 
display Indian  caller IDs. CIOR had an immediate impact of blocking 1.35 crore spoofed 
calls within just 24 hours of its launching and has achieved a 98.5% reduction in such 
deceptive calls. Additionally, an indigenous AI -based solution namely Artificial Intelligence 
and Facial Recognition powered Solution for Telecom SIM Subscriber Verification (ASTR) 
helps prevent misuse of SIM cards by identifying SIMs obtained through fake identities. 
Another tool, the Financial Fraud Risk Indicator (FRI), launched in May 2025, uses multi-
source analysis to classify mobile numbers into risk categories such as medium, high or very 
high enabling banks, NBFCs and UPI providers to take timely preventive actions. 
3.4.6 To further secure mobile identity, a Mobile Number Validation Service ( MNVS) is 
also being piloted, which aims to verify mobile number ownership and prevent use of mobile 
connections in mule accounts. The DoT has backed all these digital interventions with large -
scale public awareness campaigns across languages and platforms,  partnering with banks, 
telecom service providers and Government institutions to maximize reach. Together, these 
innovative steps form a robust defence framework against the misuse of India’s telecom and 
financial systems.

initiatives of DoT are as under: -
