Recommendation/Observation 
3.4.8 The Committee commends the DoT for its significant initiatives to tackle 
cybercrime using telecom resources, highlighting the successful Sanchar Saathi portal 
that engages citizens in protecting digital identities. The Committee notes the strong 
impact of technologies like the Centr alized International Out Roamer (CIOR) system 
blocking over 1.35 crore spoofed calls in a day and reducing such incidents by 98% 
along with the use of AI, facial recognition and the Financial Fraud Risk Indicator 
(FRI) for innovative prevention. The Commit tee also acknowledges the Digital 
Intelligence Platform (DIP) for fostering real -time collaboration among key sectors to 
fight cybercrime. To keep pace with evolving fraud techniques, the Committee 
recommends deeper integration and automation of data flow between existing systems 
such as the Sanchar Saathi portal, Digital Intelligence Platform and law enforcement 
databases. 
3.4.9 Considering the importance of public awareness in cybercrime prevention, the 
Committee observes that sustained multilingual and c ross-platform campaigns should 
be intensified particularly in regional and rural areas so that more citizens know how to 
report fraud and reclaim control over their mobile credentials. The Committee 
recommends that it would be valuable to take the Mobile N umber Validation Service 
(MNVS) beyond the current stage and implement it nationwide in collaboration with 
banks, NBFCs and fintech platforms to limit the use of mobile numbers in fraudulent or 
mule accounts. 
3.4.10  The Committee notes that the regulatory  frameworks governing the use of 
telecom channels such as automatic calls, bulk SMSs and Voice over Internet  Protocol 
(VoIP) to be strengthened in order to prevent misuse of these services for phishing 
campaigns. The Committee recommends continued investment in indigenous AI 
technologies like Artificial Intelligence and Facial Recognition powered Solution for 
Telecom SIM Subscriber Verification (ASTR) to ensure that SIM card issuance with a 
focus on preventing identity fraud at the point of issue.  
3.4.11 The DoT informed the Committee that about 570 banks and financial 
institutions (FIs) use the Digital Intelligence Platform (DIP) to access Mobile Number 
Revocation List (MNRL) and financial fraud indicators for acting on suspicious bank 
accounts, leading to action on 20.89 lakh accounts. The Committee commends these 
efforts to tackle telecom-linked financial fraud and recommends that efforts should also 
be made to ensure swift sharing of risk alerts and revocations from all these institutions, 
thus minimizing manual delays in addressing fraud. 
3.5  Telecom Regulatory Authority of India (TRAI) 
3.5.1 India’s telecom landscape has not only become one of the largest in the world but also 
one of the most dynamic, giving rise to unprecedented opportunities as well as complex 
cybercrime challenges. As connectivity spread rapidly growing from just over 14.5 million  
phone connections in 1997 to more than 1,200 million by March 2025, the scope and 
sophistication of cyber threats targeting telecom networks also multiplied. The wide 
proliferation of the internet, with over 944 million subscribers by 2025 and the afforda ble 
availability of smart phones have turned the mobile network into an essential highway for 
commerce, governance, finance, education and public services. However, this digital 

 
 
25 
 
revolution has opened new avenues for misuse and cybercrime, including spam, f raudulent 
calls and digital scams. 
3.5.2 Addressing these threats, the Telecom Regulatory Authority of India (TRAI) has 
consistently acted to protect consumers and the integrity of the telecom network. One of 
TRAI’s most significant efforts in cybercrime p revention is the Telecom Commercial 
Communication Customer Preference Regulation (TCCCPR) of 2018. This regulation marks 
a paradigm shift in the regulatory approach to unsolicited commercial communication. It not 
only clearly defines what constitutes spam and fraudulent communications but also 
establishes concrete mechanisms to identify and punish offenders. To empower the public, 
TRAI has enabled users to specify preferences regarding commercial communications and to 
report violations swiftly, notably thro ugh the TRAI Do Not Disturb (DND) App, which 
makes it easier for citizens to report spam or suspected fraud directly. 
Combating Telecom Misuse  
3.5.3 Recognizing that cybercriminals frequently alter their methods, TRAI has informed 
the Committee that it has carried out regular amendments to strengthen the framework. In 
February 2025, TRAI amended TCCCPR (Telecom Commercial Communications Customer 
Preference Regulation) to strengthen consumer protection against Unsolicited Commercial 
Communication (UCC). T hese amendments set out stricter criteria for commercial 
communications, enhanced complaint redressal systems, imposed higher accountability on 
senders and telemarketers, introduced tighter thresholds for identifying misuse and enacted 
stricter controls to  prevent the use of 10 -digit numbers by unregistered telemarketers for 
illegal activities. The regulations also require Telecom Service Providers (TSPs) to actively 
monitor telecom resources, promptly disconnect and blacklist offenders and maintain 
comprehensive records of their actions for regulatory review. 
3.5.4 Technological innovation has been a key player in TRAI’s cybercrime prevention 
strategy. It informed the Committee that the implementation of a blockchain based regulatory 
framework for spam cont rol under TCCCPR represents a significant technological leap, 
making tracking and accountability in commercial messaging far more robust. This system 
creates a tamper -proof digital ledger of messaging transactions, enabling authorities to trace 
and act aga inst violators with much greater efficiency and transparency. However, as 
malicious actors continue to innovate, TRAI remains vigilant by actively soliciting 
suggestions from the ecosystem, as seen in the 2024 consultation paper on further refining the 
TCCCPR framework. 
3.5.5 In addition to regulatory and technological measures, TRAI understands that 
cybercrime and fraudulent communications are multi -sectoral threats demanding 
collaboration. As such, it established the Joint Committee of Regulators (JCoR), comprising 
regulatory agencies from domains like finance, securities, insurance, consumer affairs and IT, 
along with supporting Ministries. This body facilitates information sharing and coordinated 
strategies to deal with spam, scams and cybercrimes that c ut across regulatory jurisdictions. 
For example, JCoR’s focus on creating a joint action plan to fight fraudulent calls and 
messages has led to synchronized responses and policy formulation among diverse agencies. 
3.5.6 Concrete enforcement actions have fu rther underlined TRAI’s commitment to 
cybercrime prevention. Consequent to release of TRAI’s direction dated 13 th August, 2024, 
Access Service Providers are now empowered to disconnect all telecom resources such as 
voice lines and network access used by un registered senders and Unregistered Telemarketers 

 
 
26 
 
(UTMs) engaging in spam and fraud. Access Providers have taken widespread actions which 
have led to a significant reduction in the complaints registered  against the spam calls (Table -
3.2).

there will be complete ban on acquisition of new telecom resources etc. Consequent to t his

Enhanced Message Traceability 
3.5.8 TRAI also informed the Committee that  to strengthen the fight against spam and 
misuse of telecom services, TRAI introduced several important measures. First, to enhance 
message traceability, a directive issued on 20 th August 2024 required that the entire trail of 
every message from sender or principal entity to  the end recipient must be traceable from 1 st 
November 2024 onwards. Access Providers were given time to upgrade their technical 
systems and full implementation was accomplished from 11th December 2024.  
Guidelines for Senders 
3.5.9 To sensitize the Sender s about the regulatory requirement before making commercial 
communications through Voice Calls or SMS, important aspects of guidelines as per 
TCCCPR-2018 Regulations were circulated to all the Senders in June 2024 through RBI, 
SEBI, Insurance Regulatory an d Development Authority of India (IRDAI), Department of 
Financial Services (DFS), Department of Economic Affairs (DEA), Department of Consumer 
Affairs (DoCA)  and all Access Providers  for effective implementation of these guidelines in 
letter and spirit for prevention of UCC as well as potential misuse by fraudsters. 
Collaborative Regulatory efforts through Joint Committee of Regulators 
3.5.10  Collaboration across different regulators has been boosted through the Joint 
Committee of Regulators (JCoR), esta blished in December 2021. The Committee consists of 
members from SEBI, RBI, TRAI, Ministry of Consumer Affairs, IRDAI, Pension Fund 
Regulatory and Development Authority (PFRDA) and MeitY, focuses to study regulatory 
challenges in the digital world. Conside ring the increasing menace of financial scams, the 
focus of the last few meetings has been on joint action plans to curb unsolicited 
communications using telecom resources. 
Stringent Measures against Spammers/ Senders 
3.5.11  To effectively combat spam and  Unsolicited Commercial Communications (UCC), 
TRAI has mandated strict measures for Access Providers. If a sender is found repeatedly 
violating regulations, all their telecom resources must be suspended. For a first -time breach, 
outgoing services from all telecom connections of the sender will be barred for 15 days. For 
further violations, all telecom resources, including PRI (Primary Rate Interface)/SIP (Session 
Initiation Protocol) trunks, will be disconnected for one year across all providers and the 
sender will be blacklisted.  
3.5.12  In addition, any call or message intended to deceive customers is now officially 
classified as UCC related to telecom misuse. This designation empowers swift regulatory 
intervention including prompt disconnection and black listing, especially since blockchain 
technology enables faster tracing and enforcement. Further, senders are now prevented from 
using normal 10 -digit numbers for telemarketing. Instead, all commercial communications 
must use specific headers or designated number series. The existing “140” series will be used 
for promotional calls and the new “1600” series will be used for transactional and service 
calls. This change will help recipients easily recognise the nature of the commercial call from 
the caller ID, offering enhanced transparency and consumer protection. 
  

 
 
28 
 
Public Awareness Programmes 
3.5.13  In a reply to a question, TRAI has stated to the Committee that as part of its ongoing 
mission to enhance consumer protection and cyber hygiene, TRAI has undertaken a series of 
wide-ranging initiatives aimed at educating the public on telecom -related cyber threats and 
preparing for emerging risks in the digital communication ecosystem. Between 2024 and 
2025 alone, TRAI has conducted a remarkable 654 consume r awareness activities across 
India. These included 46 Consumer Outreach Programmes (COPs), 6 workshops and 6 
seminars by regional offices. In addition, 261 Consumer Awareness Programmes (CAPs) 
were held through registered consumer organisations, while Tel ecom Service Providers 
(TSPs) organised 335 Consumer Educational Workshops (CEWs). TRAI’s awareness 
campaigns further incorporated mass outreach mediums such as promotions of the Do Not 
Disturb (DND) app, radio and media interviews, active social media cam paigns, SMS 
advisories on spam and fraud and special regional programs all of which emphasized the 
growing threat of telecom-based fraud and how users can safeguard themselves. 
3.5.14  On collaborative engagements, TRAI has involved various NGOs and educat ional 
institutions to support spam prevention, header misuse awareness and UCC (unsolicited 
commercial communication) reporting. It regularly engages NGOs through regional offices 
and consultation processes designed to includ e feedback from consumer organi sations. 
Further, awareness programs are routinely conducted in schools and colleges and TRAI is 
currently exploring a partnership with the National Commission for Women (NCW) for joint 
initiatives. TRAI has not yet directly collaborated with MeitY's Infor mation Security 
Education and Awareness (ISEA) project, but will explore such collaborations in the future. 
3.5.15  Looking ahead, TRAI is actively responding to the rising sophistication of cyber 
threats emerging from advancements in 5G, satellite interne t and AI/ML technologies. It 
supports AI-based spam filters, promotes digital consent and traceability pilots and advocates 
for regulatory collaboration to fill systemic gaps. Nonetheless, TRAI acknowledges 
constraints stemming from its stagnant workforce strength. Despite exponential growth in 
telecom and broadcasting ecosystems, TRAI continues to operate with only 273 employees a 
number unchanged since 2012. TRAI has submitted a proposal to the Department of 
Telecommunications (DoT) to enhance the sanctio ned strength to 396 to better address 
complex, technology -driven policy challenges. Further, TRAI has also mentioned to the 
Committee that there is urgent requirement of new verticals to address critical issues such as 
telecom/cybersecurity including quant um safe communication and to develop an economic 
analysis wing to effectively assess policies related to emerging technologies. 
Recommendation/Observation 
3.5.16  The Committee appreciates TRAI’s commendable efforts in organizing over 650 
consumer awarenes s initiatives and driving proactive public outreach to promote 
telecom cybersecurity awareness. The collaborative efforts involving Telecom Service 
Providers (TSPs), NGOs and educational institutions reflect a strong commitment to 
inclusive digital literac y. Since TRAI has not yet directly collaborated with MeitY's 
Information Security Education and Awareness (ISEA) project,  the Committee 
recommends that TRAI should be encouraged to partner with MeitY’s  ISEA project to 
expand digital safety outreach through role -based learning modules and online self -
learning platforms. Further, the Committee recommends that in order to strengthen its 
capacity in dealing with emerging regulatory and technological issues, the Ministry of 

 
 
29 
 
Communications may be approached to expedite the proposal to enhance TRAI’s staff 
strength.  
3.5.17  With respect to the awareness programmes conducted by TRAI, the Committee 
is of the view that TRAI can also work with universities and rese arch groups to create a 
system to check how effective its awareness programs are working, especially for people 
in rural areas and other vulnerable groups. The Committee feels that a dedicated 
budget may also be considered for cyber literacy pilot projects  in schools and 
communities, with regular reviews jointly conducted by DoT, MeitY and TRAI. 
Further, the Committee suggests that the Government may consider the creation of new 
expert verticals within TRAI to address cyber threats, quantum -safe networks an d the 
economic challenges of fast -developing telecom technologies, enabling a secure digital 
transition for the country. 
3.5.18  Considering the steady increase in complaints related to Unregistered 
Telemarketers (UTMs), the Committee is of the view that i t is essential to focus on 
targeted actions to address this challenge more effectively. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that measures should be taken to enhance real -time detection mechanisms 
to identify and block UTMs proactively. The Committee sugge sts that a centralized 
blacklist database should be maintained and it needs to be shared across all telecom 
service providers to ensure swift disconnection of offending numbers. 
3.6  Reserve Bank of India 
Transformation and Emerging Risks 
3.6.1 India’s rapid expansion of digital financial services has significantly enhanced 
financial inclusion and consumer convenience. However, this growth has also triggered a rise 
in cyber -enabled frauds, especially those driven by social engineering tactics, limited 
customer awareness, and the exploitation of technological vulnerabilities. In response to this 
evolving threat landscape, the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) continues to reinforce its 
regulatory and supervisory frameworks to protect consumers and ensure the int egrity of the 
financial system. 
Regulatory and Prevention Measures 
3.6.2 RBI has issued targeted directives to curb cyber -frauds across the financial sector. In 
January 2025, the RBI issued a circular directing all regulated entities (REs) to cleanse their  
customer databases by cross -referencing against the Department of Telecom’s Mobile 
Number Revocation List  (MNRL) and to implement dedicated numbering series for official 
outbound calls, thereby reducing the risk of caller-ID spoofing. Compliance with the Telecom 
Regulatory Authority of India’s ( TRAI’s) communication standards is compulsory, and 
institutions must prominently feature their customer -care contact details across all public 
channels. Complementing these technical safeguards, the RBI has intensi fied its customer -
awareness initiatives. Under its July 2024 update, supervised entities (SEs) are also required 
to heighten scrutiny of suspicious transactions, with a particular focus on accounts lacking 
full KYC documentation and those potentially used as money mules. 
  

 
 
30 
 
Customer Due Diligence and Fraud Risk Management 
3.6.3 In the meeting with the representatives of RBI, the Committee was informed that  RBI 
has laid down a comprehensive regulatory framework to ensure strict adherence to Customer 
Due Diligence (CDD) and Fraud Risk Management, aimed at safeguarding the integrity of 
the financial system. The RBI’s Master Direction on Know Your Customer, revi sed on 12 
June 2025, mandates that regulated entities (REs) conduct rigorous identity verification at 
onboarding, maintain continuous monitoring of customer accounts, and periodically refresh 
KYC data to swiftly detect unusual activity. Its Master Direction on Fraud Risk Management, 
effective from 15th July 2024,  obliges supervised entities (SEs) to remain vigilant against 
suspicious transactions especially those tied to non-KYC compliant or suspected money mule 
accounts and to strengthen internal policies, controls, and recruitment procedures to deter 
employee involvement in fraud. An advisory issued on 19 th November 2024 further instructs 
banks to bolster staff accountability, reinforce systems, and enhance digital onboarding 
processes such as Video-based Customer Identification (V-CIP).  
3.6.4 On the supervisory  front, RBI employs multiple layers of oversight to ensure 
compliance and risk mitigation. Prudential supervision by Senior Supervisory Managers 
(SSMs) covers operational risks including KYC, Anti-Money Laundering (AML), Combating 
the Financing of Terroris m (CFT) and cybersecurity. RBI conducts full -scope KYC/AML 
inspections that encompass customer due diligence, transaction monitoring, identification of 
money mule activity and preventive controls. Thematic assessments and supervisory 
evaluations of major banks help identify gaps in controls related to money mule detection and 
fraud prevention. Where gaps or non -compliance are detected, RBI issues supervisory letters 
demanding timely corrective actions, with involvement from the banks’ top management and 
audit committees. In cases of violations, the RBI enforces stringent actions, including 
monetary penalties, to uphold regulatory standards and protect the financial ecosystem. 
API Integration with I4C for Faster Cybercrime Response 
3.6.5 RBI apprised the Comm ittee about the integration of Application Programming 
Interfaces (APIs) between banks and the Indian Cybercrime Coordination Centre (I4C) portal 
marks a significant step toward speeding up the response to complaints registered under the 
National Cybercrim e Reporting Portal (NCRP). This initiative, facilitated by RBI, aims to 
streamline communication and data exchange between financial institutions and cybercrime 
authorities to tackle cyber -related fraud and crimes more efficiently. I4C requested RBI to 
advise banks to onboard the NCRP -Centralized Fraud and Citizen Financial Fraud Reporting 
and Management System (CFCFRMS) platform and proceed with API integration. In 
response, RBI has issued advisories mandating banks and financial institutions to join the 
platform and encouraging them to implement API integration as and when approached by 
I4C. This ensures that all involved parties work seamlessly in reporting and investigating 
cybercrime incidents tied to financial fraud. 
3.6.6 RBI informed the Committee that as on July 2025, 19 banks and financial institutions 
have fully completed the API integration process, enabling automated, real -time sharing of 
complaint-related information with I4C. In addition, over 350 banks have been on boarded to 
the CFCFRMS platform, reflecting broad adoption across the financial sector. However, the 
full integration process is still underway for some institutions, with delays primarily 
attributed to dependencies on third-party vendors or technical challenges.  
  

 
 
31 
 
Supervisory Actions and Coordination 
3.6.7 Beyond public advisories, RBI issues confidential instructions to banks and financial 
institutions to combat evolving fraud patterns. These include sharing information on fraud 
“hotspots” requiring enha nced monitoring, identifying money mule accounts linked to 
investment or job frauds and tightening internal controls and staff accountability. Banks must 
respond swiftly to law enforcement  agencies, secure customer data and align anti -fraud 
efforts with anti-money laundering protocols. Compliance deadlines and progress, especially 
regarding technology upgrades like IP -based monitoring, are tracked closely. Banks also 
receive specific instructions on telemarketing compliance, deployment of dedicated staff fo r 
24x7 cybercrime reporting and cooperation with law enforcement, as well as participation in 
national cybercrime mitigation platforms and suspect registries. 
Strengthening Security in Card Usage and Payments 
3.6.8 RBI’s 2016 cybersecurity framework mandat es that banks maintain strong, ongoing 
cyber safeguards; this requirement now extends to cooperative banks as well. Master 
Directions issued in 2021 require regulated digital payment entities to implement robust 
governance and minimum-security standards across all payment platforms, including unified 
authentication for ATMs, mobile and online banking and card transactions. Given the 
growing reliance on outsourcing, the 2023 guidelines outline risk management strategies for 
third-party and cloud -based IT ser vices. Since 2024, non -bank payment system operators 
must also conform to standards for cyber resilience and information security, aiming for a 
robust, secure digital payments ecosystem.  
3.6.9 Further RBI has highlighted the Committee that it has introduced several measures to 
secure card transactions, digital payments and the broader banking system against cyber fraud 
and operational risks. Card tokenisation now protects users by replacing sens itive card data 
with unique tokens, reducing data theft risks. Now, cardholders can manage transaction limits 
or even switch cards on/off and new cards are enabled only for domestic, contact -based 
transactions by default. Only authorised Payment Aggregator s (PAs) are allowed to operate 
and banks must close accounts associated with unauthorised PAs, referring regularly to RBI’s 
updated official list. ATM security has also been enhanced with mandatory video 
surveillance via CCTV and a new convenient and secure way to withdraw cash without using 
physical cards has been introduced where users can withdraw cash using the UPI system. 
Banks are also directed to monitor withdrawals in high -risk locations and review overseas 
card usage. Additionally, the introduction  of a payee name look -up feature in NEFT and 
RTGS systems has helped in verifying account details before fund transfers, thereby reducing 
fraud risk. 
Digital Payment Intelligence Platform (DPIP) 
3.6.10  RBI informed the Committee that the Digital Payment I ntelligence Platform (DPIP) 
is an initiative led by RBI to combat the rising frauds in digital payments by leveraging 
advanced technologies. The RBI constituted a committee to explore the framework for 
establishing DPIP. The platform is designed as a neutr al, industry -wide digital public 
infrastructure (DPI) that enables real -time sharing, analysis and intelligence of payment 
transaction data across banks and non -bank payment system participants. DPIP will use 
Artificial Intelligence (AI) and Machine Learni ng (ML) models developed from historical 
transaction data contributed by banks and payment networks. The platform will provide real -
time risk scores for ongoing transactions. Participating banks can use it to identify suspicious 

 
 
32 
 
transactions and take preve ntive actions effectively. By collecting and sharing data instantly 
across multiple institutions, hence DPIP facilitates centralised threat detection and fraud 
management.  
3.6.11  The Reserve Bank Innovation Hub (RBIH), a wholly owned subsidiary of RBI, i s 
tasked with building a DPIP prototype in collaboration with several banks. The institutional 
framework is being developed with participation from public and private sector banks, 
recognizing fraud as a widespread challenge across the sector. Contextually , the urgency to 
operationalize DPIP arises from a significant surge in digital payment frauds. According to 
RBI's latest Annual report, fraud value related to bank transactions nearly tripled from 
₹12,230 crore in FY24 to ₹36,014 crore in FY25, with a lar ge number of cases occurring in 
digital payment modes such as cards and internet banking.  
Supervision and Coordination 
3.6.12  RBI undertakes thorough supervisory activities, including risk inspections spanning 
KYC, AML/CFT and cybersecurity. Any system gaps identified prompt time-bound remedial 
action, with top management involvement and enforcement action may include penalties. The 
Central Bank maintains continuous engagement with regulated entities to address issues like 
digital frauds and mule accounts, supported by special supervisory cells and frequent training. 
RBI’s coordination extends to law enforcement, regulators and Ministry of Home Affairs, 
facilitating anti -fraud strategies, knowledge sharing through joint workshops and efficient 
cyber fraud response. Initiatives also include collaboration with the telecom and IT sectors to 
tackle phishing scams and consideration of a ‘bank.in’ domain for official bank websites in 
collaboration with MeitY. Efforts are underway to standardize cybercrime complai nt 
handling and accelerate coordinated action via Application Programming Interfaces (APIs) 
connectivity with national fraud and cybercrime management platforms. 
Fostering Innovation and Technological Response 
3.6.13  As technology continues to advance, fr audsters are also becoming more 
sophisticated, constantly finding new ways to exploit vulnerabilities in the financial system. 
RBI informed the Committee that recognizing this ongoing challenge, RBI is committed to 
help banks and financial institutions to stay a step ahead by promoting the adoption of 
advanced technological tools and frameworks. RBI is actively encouraging the use of 
Artificial Intelligence (AI) and Machine Learning (ML) to strengthen fraud detection and 
prevention mechanisms. One notable i nitiative is MuleHunter.ai, a machine learning tool 
developed by the Reserve Bank Innovation Hub (RBIH) for near real -time identification of 
mule accounts. By leveraging advanced pattern -recognition techniques, MuleHunter.ai 
achieves higher accuracy and sp eed than traditional detection systems and is already 
operational at 4 banks (Canara Bank, Punjab National Bank, Bank of Baroda and Bank of 
India) and is being deployed in two others (AU Small Finance Bank and Federal Bank). As of 
now, MuleHunter.ai has flagged over 75,000 potential mule accounts with accuracy of ~90% 
across the four banks, where it has been implemented. 
3.6.14  To encourage further innovation, RBI has introduced a Regulatory Sandbox 
framework that allows banks and fintech companies to pilo t new products and technologies 
under regulatory oversight, particularly those aimed at preventing fraud. In addition, RBI’s 
“HaRBInger Hackathon” annual program brings together fintech professionals from around 
the world to create innovative solutions add ressing immediate challenges such as real -time 
fraud detection and the identification of mule bank accounts. 

 
 
33 
 
3.6.15  RBI is also investing in digital infrastructure to improve sector -wide supervision and 
incident management. The tool known as “DAKSH” , an a dvanced web -based supervisory 
monitoring system, facilitates the automation and streamlining of key processes like cyber 
incident reporting and analysis.  It further ensures that lessons learned from individual 
incidents are translated into actionable intel ligence for the broader financial sector. Looking 
ahead, new platforms like the Digital Payment Intelligence Platform (DPIP) will deploy 
multiple AI models to assess risk levels of transactions, enabling banks to take timely and 
appropriate action to mitig ate potential fraud. The upcoming Central Payment Fraud 
Information Registry (CPFIR) will also serve as a consolidated source for tracking and 
analyzing fraud incidents across the financial sector. 
3.6.16  The RBI has issued guidance encouraging regulated entities (REs) to adopt Artificial 
intelligence (AI) and Machine Learning (ML) for robust transaction monitoring and to 
enhance customer identification processes, including making Video -based Customer 
Identification Process (V -CIP). To ensure ethical and e ffective use of these technologies, an 
Expert Committee has been established to develop frameworks for the responsible 
deployment of AI in the banking and financial sector. RBI’s principle -based regulatory 
approach also calls for regulated entities to dyna mically assess emerging risks from new 
technologies and implement suitable remediation measures.  
Digital Payment Fraud Complaints and Resolution  
3.6.17  RBI informed the Committee that the victims of digital payment fraud can lodge their 
complaints with RBI Ombudsman under the Reserve Bank -Integrated Ombudsman Scheme 
(RB-IOS). Complaints can be filed either physically or digitally ( via email or online portal) 
and in any language, making the process accessible and user -friendly. Over the last three 
financial years, complaints related to unauthorised transactions have been handled diligently 
by RBI Ombudsmen. The average Turnaround Time (TAT) for disposal of complaints related

Complaints are disposed in favour of the complainant, where it is observed that there is a case

Addressing OTP Vulnerabilities and Initiatives 
3.6.20  One-time passwords (OTPs) face several security challenges that impact their 
effectiveness in authenticating digital transactions. Customers are vulnerable to sharing OTPs 
due to social engineering tactics or coercion. Additionally, if a customer’s device is 
compromised through hacking, the OTP can be intercepted and misused. Another significant 
threat is SMS -based OTP interception via SIM swapping, where fraudsters fraudulently 
obtain a genuine customer’s SIM card to gain access to OTPs. RBI informed the Committee 
that in response to these challenges, the RBI has launched multiple public awareness 
initiatives, such as “RBI Kehta Hai” a nd “RBI Says.” These initiatives consistently educate 
customers to never share OTPs, even if requested by individuals claiming to be bank 
officials. Awareness messages are widely disseminated across social media platforms, print 
media and broadcast channels in various languages to maximize reach. 
3.6.21  While the RBI has not mandated a specific authentication factor, the digital payments 
ecosystem predominantly relies on SMS -based OTPs as Additional Factor of Authentication 
(AFA). It was apprised to the Co mmittee that although this method works satisfactorily, 
technological advancements have introduced alternative authentication mechanisms. 
Recognizing this, the RBI issued a draft framework in July 2024 for alternative factors of 
authentication and after re viewing stakeholder feedback, a final circular is expected to be 
released soon. The RBI had discussions with companies like Google to enhance digital 
payment authentication security. Google has developed two of the RBI’s requirement to 
prevent compromise of OTPs through remote access applications and auto -SMS forwarding. 
Further, guidelines have been established to improve transaction transparency and security, 
requiring that alerts and OTP messages received by customers for online transactions display 
the merchant’s name wherever applicable, rather than the payment aggregator’s name, to help 
customers verify transaction authenticity. 
Emerging Quantum Computing Threat 
3.6.22  RBI pointed out to the Committee that quantum computing is poised to significantly 
impact the financial ecosystem by potentially breaking current encryption methods that 
protect sensitive data. Although quantum cryptography offers stronger security, it also 
presents challenges, especially for existing legacy systems that may require wide spread 
upgrades. At present, the threats from quantum computing are in its early stages due to 
uncertainties about how vulnerable current cryptographic controls truly are against future 
quantum computers. In response to concerns raised by the Department of  Economic Affairs 
(DEA) about "quantum proofing the financial sector," RBI agrees that a coordinated, unified 
approach is essential. This collaborative effort, potentially led by the National Security 
Coordination Secretariat (NSCS), would involve Governme nt agencies, regulators, financial 

 
 
35 
 
institutions, academia and private sector players to ensure the smooth and standardised 
transition to Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC).  
3.6.23  Alongside this unified effort, RBI recognises the need for a sector -specific approach 
given the unique challenges faced by Regulated Entities (REs) in the banking and financial 
sector. To address this, RBI has established an Inter -Departmental Group focused on 
formulating proactive measures toward PQC adoption that align with natio nal initiatives. 
Further, REs have been advised to develop a Cryptographic Bill of Material (CBoM), an 
inventory of their existing cryptographic assets to better prepare for a shift to quantum -safe 
technologies. RBI continues to monitor advancements in inf ormation technology and 
cybersecurity closely, fine -tuning regulations and supervisory processes to effectively 
manage emerging cyber threats, including those related to quantum computing. 
Unauthorised Credits to Bank Accounts  
3.6.24  A question was raise d in the meeting of the Committee about the risk that malicious 
actors, such as terrorist organisations, might deposit money into a person’s or business’s bank 
account without their direct involvement or consent. The main worry expressed is that, in the 
current system, anyone can transfer money to any account, possibly creating legal or 
reputational liabilities for the account holder, especially if the source is illicit. In response, it 
is clarified that if money is credited to an account without the accoun t holder’s consent, the 
account holder is not automatically criminally liable for merely receiving that money. Section 
72 of the Indian Contract Act, 1872, explains that if something is paid or delivered by 
mistake or coercion, the recipient must repay or return it to the rightful owner. The response 
further notes that when anyone opens a bank account, there is an implied authorisation for the 
bank to accept and credit funds on their behalf and it would be impractical and contrary to 
standard banking operat ions to require explicit permission for every inward transaction. 
However, this matter may require holistic examination from the Law Enforcement angle. 
Limiting Liability in Unauthorised Electronic Banking Transactions 
3.6.25  RBI has apprised the Committe e that it has put in place specific guidelines to protect 
customers from losses due to un authorised electronic banking transactions. These guidelines 
apply to all scheduled commercial banks as well as co -operative banks and are designed to 
limit the financ ial liability of customers when unauthorised transactions occur. The key 
features of this framework are as follows: 
 Zero Liability: 
Customers bear no loss in two scenarios: (a) if the fault is with the bank; or (b) if the 
unauthorised transaction occurs due to issues elsewhere in the system and the customer 
reports the unauthorised transaction within three working days from receiving the 
communication. This means prompt reporting by the customer ensures full protection. 
 Limited Liability: 
If the loss results from the customer’s own negligence, the customer is responsible for 
all losses up to the time the incident is reported to the bank. If the loss is not due to the 
customer’s or the bank’s fault but is caused by a third -party system i ssue and the customer 
reports the transaction within four to seven working days of receiving communication, the 

 
 
36 
 
maximum liability of the customer ranges from ₹5,000 to ₹25,000, depending on the type of 
account/ instrument. 
 Liability beyond Seven Days: 
If the customer delays in notifying the bank and reports the unauthorised transaction 
after seven working days, the extent of their liability is determined according to the bank’s 
Board approved policy. 
 Burden of Proof: 
The burden of proving customer liability  in case of unauthorised electronic banking 
transactions shall lie on the bank. 
 Timely Resolution and Compensation: 
Banks also have been advised to ensure that a complaint is resolved and liability of 
the customer, if any, established within such time, as may be specified in the bank’s Board 
approved policy, but not exceeding 90 days and the customer is compensated as given above. 
Where banks are unable to resolve the complaint or determine the customer liability, if any, 
within 90 days, the compensation as prescribed is paid to the customer. 
Public Awareness Campaigns  
3.6.26  As per the information provided by the RBI to the Committee, during the financial 
year 2024 -25, a total of 4,13,95,262  participants attended financial literacy programs 
organised by the Financial Inclusion and Development Department (FIDD) of the RBI 
through its Regional Offices (ROs), Centres for Financial Literacy (CFLs) and Financial 
Literacy Centres (FLCs). Continuous efforts are being made to maximize the coverage and 
impact of these initiatives. Special focus has also been laid on the youth, with RBI’s regional 
offices conducting 563 financial literacy programs for school and college students across the 
country. The Offices of the Reserve Bank of India Ombudsmen (ORBIOs) contribute actively 
by conducting townhall and awareness programs each year, including dedicated sessions in 
educational institutions, generally attended by 100 to 500 participants. These programs serv e 
a wide demographic, including students, senior citizens and women.  
3.6.27  In the fiscal years 2023-24 and 2024-25, the Ombudsmen offices conducted a total of 
203 and 243 awareness programs and 46 and 47 townhall events, respectively. The number of 
such programs in educational institutions increased from 76 to 108 over the two years, 
reflecting intensified outreach. Ombudsmen offices further enhance public understanding by 
participating in local fairs and festivals, using hoardings and distributing bookl ets to spread 
information on digital frauds. From 2017 to May 2025, various RBI Regional Offices have 
conducted 2,032 Direct Contact Awareness Programs under the Electronic Banking and 
Awareness Training (e -BAAT) initiative, educating people about electron ic payment 
systems, their advantages and cyber safety concerns. On average, each program was attended 
by around 100 people. To scale these efforts further, RBI is revamping the Direct Contact 
Awareness Programs by engaging third parties to reach more peopl e and increase the 
frequency of events. In addition to these direct programs, awareness campaigns are 
periodically launched through newspapers, television, radio and other media platforms. 

 
 
37 
 
3.6.28  Every year in March, RBI observes Digital Payment Awareness  Week (DPAW), 
during which a variety of activities and competitions are held to promote safe digital 
payments. Other stake holders like banks, Payment System Operators, etc., are also 
encouraged to participate in DPAW with their own initiatives. It is esti mated that the 
initiatives during DPAW 2025 have had about 30 crore impressions. To unify these 
widespread awareness activities, the mission ‘Har Payment Digital’ was launched in 2023 
with the objective of making every citizen aware of safe digital payment practices.  
3.6.29  Public awareness campaigns (PACs) have also been conducted on specific threats, 
such as money mule frauds, delivered across television, print, digital media and SMS 
channels in 2024. RBI has further cautioned public through press relea ses against frauds 
under the pretext of KYC updation. RBI also used to conduct quiz contests, which are aimed 
at creating greater awareness about the Reserve Bank and the financial ecosystem among the 
students. RBI has been encouraging the youth to cultivate responsible financial behaviour and 
develop the habit of safe and secure usage of digital financial products through its public 
awareness campaigns. The quantum of prize for the quiz contest varies from contest to 
contest. 
Recommendation/Observation 
3.6.30  The Committee appreciates the RBI’s comprehensive framework for Customer 
Due Diligence and Fraud Risk Management, which reflects a strong commitment in 
securing the financial system through rigorous KYC practices, proactive monitoring 
and robust supervisor y oversight. The Committee is of the view that increasing 
collaboration and real -time information sharing between financial institutions and law 
enforcement agencies can accelerate response times and improve the effectiveness of 
preventive measures.  
3.6.31   The Committee recommends that banks should regularly train their employees 
and organise awareness programs for customers about the latest methods that 
fraudsters use. The Committee is of the view that by doing so, both staff and customers 
will be bette r prepared to recognise and respond to new types of scams and thereby 
complementing technological solutions with human vigilance. 
3.6.32   The Committee notes the deadline fixed by RBI to all banks to complete the 
migration process to the exclusive Interne t Domain ‘.bank.in’ is 31 st October, 2025 with 
the Institute for Development and Research in Banking Technology (IDRBT) 
designated as the registrar for this process. The Committee believes that till date no 
major banks have migrated to the domain. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
RBI should supervise the entire migration process, ensuring that all banks adhere to the 
schedule and complete the migration process within the prescribed deadline. The 
Committee further recommends that the RBI should cond uct public awareness 
campaigns to educate consumers on identifying genuine ‘.bank.in’ domains, thereby 
reinforcing public trust. The Committee also suggests that to have an audit mechanism 
for verifying the domain usage and security standards, as it would further strengthen 
the preventive impact of this initiative.  
3.6.33    The Committee appreciates the good progress made in connecting banks with 
the Indian Cybercrime Coordination Centre (I4C) portal through Application 
Programming Interface (API) integra tion. This integration is important for improving 

 
 
38 
 
how cybercrime complaints and financial fraud cases are handled. So far, 19 banks 
have fully completed the API integration and over 350 banks have joined the Citizen 
Financial Cyber Fraud Reporting and Management System (CFCFRMS) platform. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends the RBI to extend this API integration to all 
remaining banks, including smaller and cooperative banks, to ensure full coverage and 
a strong nationwide cybersecurity response. The Commi ttee also recommends  that a 
clear deadline should be set for completing this integration. Further, the Committee 
suggests that the RBI may provide support to banks facing technical or vendor -related 
difficulties by offering dedicated technical help or sett ing up a centralized helpdesk to 
resolve issues quickly. The Committee believes that RBI, together with I4C, needs to 
have a regular review process to closely monitor the progress of this integration effort. 
The Committee recommends initiating early collab oration among stakeholders to 
develop and implement quantum -resistant cryptographic solutions, ensuring a smooth 
shift from legacy systems to future-proof technologies.  
3.6.34  The Committee acknowledges the RBI’s proactive and innovative approach 
through the Digital Payment Intelligence Platform (DPIP). It is truly impressive, 
showcasing a strong commitment to safeguarding the digital payments ecosystem. 
However, the Committee r ecommends setting  a timeline for the rollout of the DPIP, 
ensuring its speedy implementation to address the rising digital payment frauds 
effectively.  
3.6.35  The Committee also appreciates the implementation of MuleHunter.ai, as a pilot 
phase, in the fou r banks Canara Bank, Punjab National Bank, Bank of Baroda and 
Bank of India to validate the tool's effectiveness and accuracy in detecting mule 
accounts. Considering the promising results of MuleHunter.ai, the Committee 
recommends that MuleHunter.ai be gra dually expanded to other banks, both public 
and private sector banks, to cover a broader segment of the digital payments ecosystem. 
The Committee further recommends that the upcoming Central Payment Fraud 
Information Registry (CPFIR) be fully leveraged as a centralized, comprehensive fraud 
data repository to facilitate sector -wide analysis, coordination and mitigation of 
emerging fraud threats. 
3.6.36  The Committee notes that the Reserve Bank -Integrated Ombudsman Scheme 
(RB-IOS) provides a mechanism for vi ctims of digital payment fraud to lodge 
complaints, reflecting RBI’s commitment to consumer protection. However, the 
increasing turnaround time (TAT) for resolving unauthorised transaction complaints 
from 36.3 days in FY 2022-23 to 68.3 days in FY 2024-25 indicates a growing backlog or 
complexity in case resolution. The Committee, therefore, recommends the RBI to 
consider enhancing the Ombudsman’s operational capacity by adopting advanced 
digital case management tools, streamlining complaint resolution work flows and 
possibly increasing staffing to reduce delay in resolving the complaints.  
3.6.37  The Committee appreciates the proactive stand taken by RBI in addressing 
quantum computing threats through coordinated efforts and the formation of an Inter -
Departmental Group in this regard is commendable. The Committee suggests that the 
Regulated Entities (REs) need to accelerate the development of their Cryptographic Bill 
of Material (CBoM) to ensure a smoother transition to quantum-safe technologies. 

 
 
39 
 
3.6.38  The Committee notes the concern about illicit deposits into account holders’ 
banks without their knowledge and it is good that existing laws protect innocent account 
holders from criminal liability. However, considering the unauthorised transactions 
including facilitating money laundering or fraud, the Committee recommends that the 
approval of recipient should be necessary for receiving money in their account , actively 
involving customers in their own protection.  The Committee feels that this measure can 
also ensure that money do not get transferred to mule account. 
3.6.39  The Committee advises the integration of digital ID checks like Aadhaar -based 
e-KYC to ensure every person initiating a deposit is authentic, thereby limiting 
anonymity that criminals exploi t. The Committee observed that, despite the 
implementation of KYC measures, mule accounts continue to be opened at banks, 
indicating lapses or negligence on the part of bank personnel. The Committee 
recommends that strict action be taken against those foun d responsible to ensure 
accountability and deter future violations.  The Committee suggests exploring the 
implementation of behavioural biometrics that analyse customer pattern, like typing 
speed or mouse movement of users to spot unusual activity and alert  the bank or 
temporarily block suspicious deposits. 
3.6.40  The Committee appreciates the RBI’s well -structured liability limits to protect 
customers by fairly assigning zero or limited liability based on timely reporting and 
accountability, thereby fostering trust in digital banking. The Committee feels that 
liability limits be made more customer -centric and effective by personalizin g them 
according to individual security behaviour. The Committee suggests RBI to explore the 
possibility of rewarding users who adopt strong digital hygiene and report fraud 
promptly with lower liability.  
3.6.41  The Committee recommends the introduction of affordable micro -insurance 
policies designed to protect customers against losses exceeding standard liability limits, 
thus providing a reliable financial safety. The Committee believes that banks should use 
AI tools to proactively reduce customer liabil ity upon detecting suspicious activity, even 
before formal complaints are made. The Committee recommends exploring the zero 
liability periods and providing personalized assistance for vulnerable groups such as the 
elderly or differently -abled to enhance in clusiveness. The Committee also suggests 
enabling trusted family members/friends to assist in reporting unauthorised 
transactions to support customers who may be unable to act quickly themselves. The 
Committee recommends establishment of a forgiveness program for first-time victims of 
fraud, who maintain good security practices afterward, in order to restore confidence 
and encourage continued digital adoption. 
3.6.42  The Committee appreciates the extensive and sustained efforts of RBI in 
enhancing financia l literacy and digital payment awareness across diverse 
demographics, reflecting a strong commitment to financial inclusion and consumer 
protection. The Committee recommends that the awareness programs should be 
conducted both in Government and private sch ools so that the budding generation 
becomes aware of cybercrimes and is less likely to fall prey to it. The Committee also 
recommends that the awareness programs should reach at the grassroots level through 
active involvement of Panchayat representatives, local community leaders and 
representatives of people of the concerned constituencies. The Committee feels that to 

 
 
40 
 
foster inclusive participation integrate financial literacy sessions into village level 
gatherings, cooperatives, self-help groups and local festivals.  
3.7  Department of Financial Services (DFS) 
3.7.1 The Department of Financial Services (DFS) plays a central and proactive role in 
safeguarding India’s financial ecosystem by coordinating policy, regulating banking 
practices, financial services, insurance and collaborating with cyber -law enforcement 
networks. DFS has convened multiple high-level workshops with stakeholders including RBI, 
banks, NPCI, MeitY, DoT and fintech firms to address emerging online fraud threats and 
cyber risks. D uring such sessions, it was reported that approximately 70 lakhs mobile 
connections linked to suspected financial fraud were deactivated and ₹900 crore in funds 
were prevented from being siphoned.  
Streamlining Incident Response Mechanisms 
3.7.2 DFS has hi ghlighted the Committee that it played a key role in implementing the 
Citizen Financial Cyber Fraud Reporting and Management System (CFCFRMS) in 
coordination with I4C. This platform, launched in 2021, integrates the 1930 toll -free helpline 
with a cyber-fraud mitigation centre. It has helped recover over ₹5,489 crore from more than 
17.8 lakh complaints. DFS regularly facilitates cross -sharing of mule account data and fraud 
indicators with RBI, law enforcement agencies and I4C. In a recent initiative (Operati on 
Chakra-V), the Central Bureau of Investigation ( CBI) arrested individuals and identified 
nearly 8.5 lakh mule accounts used in frauds, based on leads supported by DFS coordination.  
Strengthening Cyber Governance Framework 
3.7.3 DFS works with RBI and f intech firms to periodically review cyber security audits 
and best practices. Regulatory collaboration ensures banks to implement robust real -time 
fraud detection systems, financial fraud risk indicators (FRIs) and compliance with 1930 
helpline protocols. These initiatives significantly reinforce India’s cyber resilience in the 
financial space though sustained public outreach, improved cross -jurisdictional legal 
frameworks and proactive compliance remain central to closing remaining gaps. In a major 
policy move, DFS mandated digitization and simplification of KYC norms across fintech 
segments. This measure is expected to reduce fake onboarding, identity misuse and facilitate 
quicker fraud detection.

Recommendation/Observation 
3.7.4 The Committee observes that financial intermediaries operating in different 
segments such as banks, stock brokers, insurance companies and mutual fund platforms 
have developed distinct user interfaces and operational frameworks, leading to 
inconsistencies in user experience. The Committee furt her observes that this 
fragmentation creates barriers for users to migrate from one platform to another, 
resulting potential security vulnerabilities as users struggle to adapt to varying 
authentication methods, transaction processes and interface designs.  The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the DFS in coordination with relevant regulators including 
RBI, SEBI, IRDAI and PFRDA, should implement standardized guidelines for user 
interface design and customer experience across all financial platforms. The  Committee 
further recommends that the DFS should also mandate the adoption of standardized 
APIs and data portability features that enable users to seamlessly transfer their 
financial profiles including Demat accounts transaction history, and preferences a cross 
platforms, thereby reducing switching costs.  
3.7.5 The Committee notes that despite advanced AI, transaction monitoring and data 
sharing, mule accounts persist in banks. The Committee feels that current technologies 

 
 
42 
 
have not fully stopped fraudsters. The Committee observes that fraudsters often recruit 
vulnerable people via social media, scams and local networks. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends creating a formal network involving local communities, 
Panchayats, local groups, NGOs, etc., to watch and report suspicious mule recruitment. 
The Committee further recommends tha t training these groups and offering small 
rewards to them will motivate timely reporting of suspicious activities. The Committee 
feels that combining this human intelligence with existing technology is necessary to 
build a stronger defence against mule accounts. 
3.8  Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) 
Institutional Mandate and Framework 
3.8.1 The Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) informed the Committee that it 
regulates capital markets and maintains digital resilience through strateg ic policy 
frameworks. In August 2024, SEBI issued the Cybersecurity and Cyber Resilience 
Framework (CSCRF) for all regulated entities, mandating governance practices, cyber threat 
monitoring and continuous risk assessment. The initial compliance deadline w as set for 
January 2025 and was extended to August 2025 to accommodate regulatory readiness across 
entities. SEBI introduced a Cyber Capability Index (CCI) to evaluate regulated entities’ cyber 
maturity and preparedness. It also enabled Small and Medium In termediaries (SMIs) to 
access Security Operation Centers (SOCs) either internally or via market SOCs to meet 
compliance obligations. Further, SEBI -led workshops such as “Niveshak Shivir” in Pune 
(June 2025) supported investor education and digital awareness campaigns. 
Verified UPI Mechanism for Investor Protection and Investor Awareness  
3.8.2 SEBI apprised the Committee that in June 2025, it launched a “Validated UPI 
handles” and “SEBI Check” for secured payments by investors to enhance investor protection 
and combat Fraud  where fraudsters pose as brokers. This mechanism helps investors verif y 
authenticity before transferring funds, significantly reducing fraud risk. SEBI’s collaboration 
with Investor Education & Protection Fund Authority (IEPFA), National Council of Applied 
Economic Research (NCAER) and the Bombay Stock Exchange (BSE)  has res ulted in 
investor workshops focusing on fintech risks and digital literacy. These initiatives help 
empower retail investors with knowledge to identify and avoid digital fraud. 
Enforcement Tools and Market Surveillance 
3.8.3 SEBI’s enhanced cybersecurity monitoring includes: 
 Regular audits and governance checks for high-risk entities. 
 Use of real -time surveillance and algorithm detection to flag anomalous trading 
behaviour. 
 Introduction of “kill switches” to halt suspicious algorithmic trades especially 
relevant as retail algo-trading gains traction. 
 
SEBI is also deploying business continuity and disaster recovery plans and mandates 
penetration testing and phishing simulations for trading platforms. 
  

 
 
43 
 
National Stock Exchange (NSE) 
3.8.4 NSE adheres to SEBI’s Cybersecurity and Cyber Resilience Framework (CSCRF) 
issued in August 2024 and its subsequent clarifications and compliance extensions through 
March 2025. NSE mandates quarterly cyber -incident reporting via its Member Portal for all 
trading members; failure to comply leads to disciplinary action as per circular dated 1 st July, 
2025. NSE informed the Committee that amid intensified geopolitical tensions, NSE 
temporarily blocked access to its websites for overseas users, citing high -risk cyber activity 
from specific IP ranges while ensuring international trading remained unaffected. The 
Exchange operates under enhanced vigilance mode with 24x7 monitoring by its Security 
Operations Center (SOC) and Network Operations Center (NOC), per internal advisories. 
Security Operations Center (SOC) & Audits 
3.8.5 Mandated by SEBI’s Cybersecurity and Cyber Resilience Framework (CSCRF) , NSE 
maintains a Market SOC (M -SOC) to onboard and support smaller regulated entities like 
brokers and depository participants in maintaining cybersecurity standards. NSE enforces 
penetration testing (red teaming), real -time threat monitoring, disaster recovery planning and 
annual cyber drills in line with regulatory expectations. 
Capacity Building & Stakeholder Engagement 
3.8.6 NSE collaborates with agencies including the CERT -In, the Investor Education and 
Protection Fund Authority (IEPFA), the National Council of Applied Economic Research 
(NCAER), and SEBI on investor awareness programs. Workshops like the “Niv eshak Shivir” 
held in Pune in mid -2025 focused on digital literacy and cyber risk education for capital 
market participants. 
Technology Row and Institutional Strategy 
3.8.7 NSE’s majority-owned IT arm, NSEIT Ltd (recently sold to Investcorp), was integral 
to its cloud infrastructure, cybersecurity architecture and digital services platform. This 
strategic restructuring demonstrates NSE’s commitment to robust and scalable cyber 
readiness. NSE also upgraded cybersecurity frameworks in collaboration with third -party 
entities like Aujas (now part of NSEIT/NuSummit) for identity access management, Managed 
Detection and Response (MDR) and threat advisory services. 
Recommendation/Observation 
3.8.8 The Committee notes that cybersecurity incidents in the financial market sector 
can cause large -scale data breaches, operational disruptions, and significant financial 
losses. The Committee also notes that these threats are growing in sophistication and 
increasingly target trading systems, depository operations, and sens itive investor data. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends SEBI to establish a robust, multi -layered 
cybersecurity framework; mandate regular independent audits; implement real -time 
incident-disclosure protocols; and develop collaborative platforms for proa ctive risk 
monitoring. 
3.8.9 The Committee has observed the growing phenomenon of unsolicited and 
unregistered financial influencers on social media platforms who promise exorbitant or 
unrealistic returns to the public, often leading to misinformation, fin ancial losses and 

 
 
44 
 
potential scams. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Significant Social 
Media Intermediary (SSMIs) be directed to allow only SEBI registered financial 
influencers to advice and to do marketing on social media platforms. The Comm ittee 
further recommends SEBI for exploring the possibility of "verified tick" (a verification 
mark indicating that an account or entity has been authenticated and confirmed as 
legitimate by a platform or service) for the SEBI registered investment advisor s on 
social media platforms. The Committee also recommends that such registration should 
be subject to prescribed disclosure norms and conduct guidelines, including the clear 
display of registration numbers, disclaimer and any potential conflicts of intere st on 
social media profiles. The Committee recommends that SEBI, in collaboration with 
MeitY, establish a robust mechanism for identifying unregistered financial influencers 
and swiftly issuing legally binding takedown orders to social media intermediaries  for 
specific content found to violate SEBI regulations. The Committee also recommends 
that SEBI should make suitable mandatory legal provisions for violation of disclosure 
norms and take strict actions against those who disseminate misleading information. 
3.8.10  The Committee is of the view  that SEBI should collaborate with social media 
platforms to develop mechanisms for identifying and curbing unregistered financial 
influencers, including leveraging artificial intelligence tools for content monitoring a nd 
enabling public reporting of suspect financial promotions. The Committee further 
recommends that SEBI should also take strict enforcement actions against those who 
disseminate misleading information.  The Committee is also of the view that SEBI 
should conduct regular awareness campaigns to educate investors on the risks of 
following unverified financial advice. 
3.9  National Payments Corporation of India (NPCI) 
Institutional Mandate 
3.9.1 The National Payments Corporation of India (NPCI), operating under the Reserve 
Bank of India (RBI)  and the Indian Banks' Association (IBA) , manages India’s core retail -
payment systems including UPI, RuPay, Immediate Payment Service (IMPS), Aadhaar -
enabled Payment System (AePS) and Bharat Bill Payment System (BBPS) serves as the 
backbone of digital transactions. NPCI maintains high cybersecurity standards by aligning 
with globally recognised protocols: PCI DSS v4.0, ISO 27001 (Information Security), ISO 
22301 (Business Continuity) and ISO 27701 (Data Privacy). It also follows a Zero -Trust 
Architecture, with continuous security monitoring via its Security Operations Centre and 
integrated threat intelligence systems. 
Advanced Fraud Risk Management (FRM) 
3.9.2 NPCI shared with the Committee that its flagship Fraud Risk Management (FRM) 
solution employs Artificial intelligence (AI) and Machine Learning (ML ) to analyse UPI and 
RuPay transactions in real time, classify anomalous patterns and issue alerts to member banks 
for preventive action. The system also helps detect mule accounts and coordinate timely 
responses across the ecosystem. In June 2025, NPCI signed a MoU with Institute for 
Development and Research in Banking Technology (IDRBT) to jointly develop cyber 
resilience training and launch an NPCI -certified payment security certification, along with 
sharing threat intelligence through systems like Indian Bank Center for Analysis of Risks and 
Threats 3.0 (IBCART 3.0). 

 
 
45 
 
Risk Controls & Consumer Protection Measures 
3.9.3 In a reply to the Committee, NPCI has stated that it employs multiple robust measures 
to secure UPI transactions and prevent fraud. These include device -binding protocols linking 
customers’ mobile numbers to devices, daily transaction limits, two -factor authentication and 
AI-driven anomaly detection that helps banks customise rules against suspicious transactions. 
From 30th June, 2025, a new rule mandates users must verify the actual identity of the UPI 
beneficiary before completing any payment, greatly reducing the risk of misdirected funds 
and fraud. NPCI also provides a real -time fraud monitoring system free of cost to ov er 500 
banks and operates a 24x7 Fraud Management Cell, actively conducting mystery shopping 
exercises to identify and block fraud attempts. 
3.9.4 To build user trust and promote safer payment experiences, UPI apps display 
beneficiary names during transactions and provide clear notifications about debiting amounts, 
preventing social engineering scams. NPCI works closely with banks by sharing data on 
suspicious accounts linked to mule activities. Complementing technical safeguards, NPCI 
conducts extensive training workshops for banks and fintechs and runs multi -language public 
awareness campaigns across TV, radio, digital platforms and print media to educate users 
about phishing, QR-code risks and safe digital practices. Further, NPCI collaborates actively 
with law enforcement and FIU -India, providing critical data and support for cybercrime 
investigations. 
Real-Time Fraud detection and Consumer Protection 
3.9.5 NPCI apprised the Committee that it ensures interoperability between payer and 
payee banks, while t ransaction authentication is handled by the payer’s bank and 
authorisation by the customer  himself. To protect customers from cyber frauds on BHIM, 
UPI and RuPay platforms, NPCI provides banks with a free, Artificial intelligence (AI) and 
Machine Learning (ML) powered real-time fraud monitoring system that scores transactions 
and helps banks set custom rules to block suspicious activity. Once a fraudulent transaction is 
reported on the NPCI portal, further transactions on the affected account are blocked 
immediately. NPCI also has a formal chargeback mechanism through which customer banks 
can initiate refunds based on scheme rules and guidelines. For complaints filed directly via 
the National Cyber Crime Reporting Portal, NPCI collaborates with the Indian Cy ber Crime 
Coordination Center (I4C) by providing a real -time API that enables beneficiary banks to 
track fraudulent funds and take protective actions such as placing liens or freezing accounts. 
This proactive coordination between NPCI, banks, RBI and law e nforcement agencies has 
helped save approximately ₹280 crores between January 2023 and June 2025, demonstrating 
effective consumer protection in cyber fraud cases. 
Security Framework for Strengthening Digital Payment 
3.9.6 On a query related to security standards measures, NPCI informed the Committee that 
it routinely issues detailed security circulars covering essential aspects such as cybersecurity 
hygiene, incident reporting protocols, mobile security best practices and ransomware 
advisories. These circu lars ensure strict compliance across all stakeholders including 
Application Service Providers (ASPs), Technology Service Providers (TSPs), Third Party 
Application Providers (TPAPs) and Payment Service Providers (PSPs), with PSPs being 
regulated entities directly overseen by the regulator.  

 
 
46 
 
3.9.7 NPCI mandates annual security audits carried out by CERT -In empanelled auditors, 
requiring participating entities to submit comprehensive compliance certificates and clean 
audit reports. This process is designed to proactively identify and address security  
vulnerabilities within their systems. Further, NPCI conducts specialized annual functionality 
audits targeting critical TPAPs and system security audits focused on key ASPs that deliver 
payment services to Regional Rural Banks (RRBs) and cooperative banks . If gaps or 
weaknesses are detected through these assessments, NPCI requires the concerned entities to 
promptly develop and submit detailed remediation plans and corrective actions.  
Recommendation/Observation 
3.9.8 The Committee appreciates NPCI’s comprehensive and proactive efforts to 
secure India’s digital payment ecosystem, like consistent issuance of security guidelines, 
strict enforcement of compliance among stakeholders and rigorous annual audits to 
identify promptly and address vulnerabilities. The Committee notes that NPCI provides 
a real -time fraud monitoring system free of cost to over 500 banks. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends NPCI that this valuable service be expanded and made available 
to a greater number of banks, including smalle r regional and cooperative banks, to 
enhance overall security coverage across the entire banking ecosystem. The Committee 
is of the view that widening access will strengthen early detection and prevention of 
fraudulent transactions nationwide, thereby impr oving consumer protection and 
reinforcing trust in digital payment platforms. 
3.10  Law Enforcement Agencies  
3.10.1  In the rapidly digitizing landscape of India, cybercrime has emerged as both a 
formidable internal security challenge and a significant threat to the nation’s economic fabric. 
With the proliferation of digital transactions, growing dependence on information technology 
and increasing connectivity among citizens and businesses, cybercrimes such as online 
financial fraud, phishing attacks, ra nsomware incidents, data breaches and even cyber -
terrorism have grown in frequency and sophistication. The multidimensional nature of these 
threats demands a layered, coordinated and highly specialized response from India’s security 
and law enforcement agencies. 
3.10.2  At the forefront of this response are specialised law enforcement agencies, most 
notably the Central Bureau of Investigation (CBI) and the National Investigation Agency 
(NIA). The CBI plays a critical role in investigating complex and inters tate cybercrimes 
particularly those involving organized criminal networks, significant financial losses or 
national security implications. The NIA, on the other hand, focuses on cybercrimes that 
intersect with terrorism and threats to national security, su ch as cases of cyber -terrorism and 
attacks on critical infrastructure. Complementing these central agencies are the numerous 
State and Union Territory (UT) police forces that serve as the first line of defence in 
addressing public complaints. From cyber fr aud and identity theft to digital harassment and 
online defamation, these local units handle a wide spectrum of cases.  
3.10.3  Central to the effectiveness of these investigations and enforcement actions is the 
Indian Cyber Crime Coordination Centre (I4C) . Unlike traditional law enforcement agencies, 
I4C does not investigate or prosecute cases directly. Rather, it functions as the national nodal 
agency responsible for orchestrating a holistic and unified response to cybercrime across the 
country. The ecosystem is further reinforced by agencies such as the National Crime Records 
Bureau (NCRB), which compiles and analyses crime data, tracks patterns and statistics 

 
 
47 
 
related to cyber incidents and assists with digital forensic support. The Intelligence Bureau 
(IB), additionally, contribute by monitoring cyber threats with implications for internal 
security and advising on policy and national preparedness. 
3.10.4  Each of these institutions, while working within their distinct areas from criminal 
investigation, cy ber-terror counteraction, frontline law enforcement, to central policy 
guidance and coordination collaborate to form a resilient and adaptive cybercrime prevention 
and enforcement framework. The synergy between central investigative agencies, State -level 
enforcement units and specialised coordinating bodies like I4C ensures that India can respond 
to the evolving nature of cybercrime with agility, expertise and nationwide reach. 
Central Bureau of Investigation (CBI) 
3.10.5  The Central Bureau of Investigatio n (CBI), established under the Delhi Special 
Police Establishment Act, 1946, is India’s premier law enforcement agency tasked with 
investigating complex, high -value, and transnational crimes. In the realm of cybercrime, the 
CBI plays a critical role in pro bing offences that have inter -state or international 
ramifications, including cyber -enabled financial frauds, crimes against critical information 
infrastructure, and online exploitation networks. Over the last two decades, the agency has 
systematically dev eloped structural, operational, technical, and forensic capabilities for 
cybercrime investigation. 
3.10.6  To address the growing complexity of cyber threats, the CBI has created a dedicated 
Cyber Crime Investigation Division (CCID), which includes specialized units for crimes such 
as online child sexual exploitative and abuse material (CSEAM) , cyber slavery and 
international fraud operations. This includes the deployment of the Network for Evidence 
Tracing, Research, and Analysis (NETRA) lab that supports activities such as real -time 
packet inspection, forensic imaging, memory analysis, and reverse engineering of malware. 
NETRA provides critical capabilities for network forensics, encrypted data handling, and 
correlation of digital traces across geographies. 
3.10.7  In its presentation to the Committee, the CBI informed that its approach to 
cybercrime is based on a broad strategic framework built around three pillars: financial, 
technical and human. Under the financial pillar, attention is given to breaking the structure 
and utility of mule bank accounts, illegal payment gateways and cyber money laundering 
systems. Technical aspects include the use of phishing toolkits, breach data repositories and 
tools enabling anonymous routing and data exfiltration. Under  the human pillar, the CBI 
targets facilitators such as scam syndicates operating from overseas compounds, human 
traffickers and online exploiters. This framework enables the agency to take a multi -pronged 
operational approach. 
3.10.8  The CBI has led and coordinated several nationwide enforcement efforts, the most 
prominent being Operation Chakra I to V, which focused on dismantling frauds involving 
scam call centres, investment schemes, cryptocurrency money laundering, mule accounts, and 
financial app -driven extortion. These operations were conducted in coordination with 
international law enforcement agencies such as INTERPOL, Europol, the FBI and national 
police forces in Japan, Germany, and Australia. Another major operation, Operation Megh 
Chakra target ed circulation and possession of child sexual exploitative and abuse material 
(CSEAM) across online platforms and cloud storage environments. As per the information 
placed before the Committee, these interventions have led to the seizure of digital assets,  

 
 
48 
 
arrest of key accused and discovery of financial and operational infrastructure behind 
organized cybercrime networks. 
3.10.9  The CBI has also developed strong international cooperation mechanisms. Through 
the INTERPOL Global Communication System (I -24/7), the agency shares and receives near 
real-time information on suspects and criminal activity hosted across borders. It has built 
internal capability for processing Mutual Legal Assistance Treaties (MLATs), INTERPOL 
Red Notices, and fast -tracking intellige nce via the BHARATPOL Portal, a digital gateway 
that connects law enforcement agencies at the central and state levels for issuing and 
responding to INTERPOL references. Several assistance requests related to crimes such as 
crypto fraud, tech impersonation scams, and CSEAM distribution have been handled through 
the portal. 
3.10.10 In terms of jurisdictional challenges, the CBI continues to encounter operational 
constraints owing to the requirement of State consent under Sections 5 and 6 of the Delhi 
Special Police Establishment Act, 1946 (DSPE Act). As per its submission to the Committee, 
eight States have withdrawn general consent, hampering the quality of investigation and 
detrimental to timely and prompt investigation.  This inhibits timely detection and i s 
particularly problematic for coordinated national responses and digital offences with cross -
border implications. The multiplicity of laws and the absence of harmonized procedures 
across states complicate access to search, seizure, and trial jurisdiction.  
3.10.11   To overcome, overload and decentralize procedural capacity, the agency stressed 
the need of establishment of regional digital forensic labs, to reduce reliance on centralized 
infrastructure and enhance turnaround times for evidence extraction a nd analysis. It has also 
undertaken regular upgrades of its internal cyber forensic protocols. This includes use of 
write blockers, calculation and preservation of hash values, maintenance of chain of custody 
logs, and ensuring judicial compatibility of di gital evidence standards. The CBI conducts 
frequent audits of its Malkhanas (evidence stores) and updates its SOPs in compliance with 
emerging decisions and procedural law changes, including under the Bharatiya Sakshya 
Adhiniyam, 2023. 
3.10.12   The forensic capabilities developed by the CBI include disk and memory forensics, 
mobile device examinations, deep packet inspection, blockchain tracing and the use of open -
source intelligence (OSINT) tools for profiling suspects on social media and dark net forums.  
The agency also uses machine learning for data clustering, transaction pattern analysis, and 
scraping large volumes of email, cloud, and device metadata. These tools are operationalized 
under controlled environments by trained cyber forensic specialists a t the headquarters and 
are also demonstrated to law enforcement trainees as part of its outreach programs. 
3.10.13   In addition to enforcement, the CBI highlighted the importance of preventive 
communication and awareness, stressing the need for digital hy giene campaigns and public 
reporting mechanisms. The agency supports the introduction of cyber literacy modules in 
school and university education, as well as intensive engagement with the fintech ecosystem 
for fraud prevention. It recommended national rec ognition schemes for exemplary digital 
safety practices by public and private institutions.
