3.10.14   CBI has positioned itself as India’s apex national investigative agency for large -
scale cybercrime leveraging legal authority, forensic strength, internat ional networks, and 
specialized manpower. However, it continues to face external constraints in operational 
jurisdiction and evidence logistics due to procedural dependencies on States. Addressing 

 
 
49 
 
these challenges is vital for enabling CBI to function as t he cornerstone of India’s unified, 
resilient and responsive cybercrime control ecosystem. 
Recommendation/Observation 
3.10.15  The Committee in CBI’s reply noted that certain States have withdrawn 
general consent required under the Delhi Special Police Establishment Act, 1946 (DSPE 
Act). It creates major hurdle in the seamless investigation and asking specific consent 
again on a case-to-case basis is time consuming, creates stress on resources and 
manpower, can hamper the quality of investigation and is detrimental to timely and 
prompt investigation. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the MHA must 
engage in active consultation with those State Governments that have not given consent 
in order to resolve this issue. Further the Committee is of the view that the Delhi Special 
Police Establishment Act, 1946 may be amended in order to empower the CBI to 
investigate the cybercrime cases in the country, without the consent of the State. 
National Investigation Agency (NIA) 
3.10.16  Created under the NIA Act, 2008, the NIA investigates offences that impact national 
security, including terrorism -related cybercrime and online radicali sation. Its jurisdiction 
includes Section 66F of the IT Act (cyber terrorism).  The National Investigation Agency’s 
(NIA) Anti -Cyber Terrorism Division (ACTD) plays a pivotal role in addressing cyber 
threats with national security implications. The ACTD oversees specialized units focused on 
dark web monitoring to uncover illicit networks and extremist communication channels, 
alongside threat intelligence gathering to identify and assess emerging digital risks. Its digital 
forensics and attribution capabilities enable the precise tracing of hostile cyber activities, 
contributing significantly to the identification of perpetrators and s afeguarding critical 
infrastructure from cyber-terrorist attacks. 
Combating Cyber Terrorism and Online Radicalisation 
3.10.17   In a reply to the Committee, NIA stated that it serves as the central authority in 
India for investigating terrorism -related cases, which explicitly includes cyber terrorism and 
online radicali sation. Historically, radicalisation occurred through physical gatherings, 
meetings and ideological sessions; however, it has increasingly shifted to digital platforms. 
The use of Virtual Private Networks (VPNs), encrypted communication channels and 
anonymous social media accounts allows perpetrators to conceal their identities, exploit 
jurisdictional challenges and disseminate extremist content with broader reach and lower risk 
of detection. 
3.10.18   Since the Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act, 1967 (UAPA) does not explicitly 
address radicalisation, NIA prosecutes such cases under Section 13 (punishment for unlawful 
activities) and Section 18 (punishment for conspiracy and preparatory a cts). In situations 
where, online platforms are utilised for terrorist training or recruitment, Sections 18A 
(organizing terrorist training camps) and 18B (recruitment for terrorist acts) are invoked, 
depending on the nature and extent of the digital activ ities involved. Moreover, the 
amendment to the NIA Act in 2019 formally incorporated cyber terrorism within the 
Agency’s jurisdiction. This empowers the NIA to investigate offences involving the use of 
computer resources or unauthorised access to sensitive  information, data, or computer 
databases that could threaten the sovereignty, security, or interests of India. To address this, 

 
 
50 
 
the NIA has established a dedicated Anti Cyber Terrorism Division (ACTD), specializing in 
probing cyber terrorism cases. 
3.10.19  As India’s premier investigative agency, NIA places significant emphasis on the 
thorough collection and analysis of digital evidence in all cases, especially those 
demonstrating the increasingly complex nexus between terrorism and cyberspace.  
Role in Inter-Agency Coordination 
3.10.20   NIA is entrusted by Central Government with investigation of cases involving cyber 
terrorism and online radicalisation registered by State police in cases, especially when they 
have national or cross -border implications. The Central Government can also order NIA to 
investigate a case suo-moto based on the information available with the Government.   
3.10.21 NIA maintains close coordination with central intelligence agencies such as the 
Intelligence Bureau (IB) and the Research and Analysis Wing (R&AW), as well as with State 
police forces. It contributes to regular intelligence -sharing forums, including Multi Agency 
Centre (MAC) and Special Focus Group (SFG) meetings. Additionally, the NIA convenes the 
annual Anti-Terror Squad (ATS) Conference, which focuses on emerging threats, including 
the use of new-age technologies in terrorism. During investigations, the NIA also undertakes 
joint operations and interrogations, facilitating a more comprehensive and multi -dimensional 
approach to case resolution. 
Encrypted Messaging Applications 
3.10.22  NIA informed the Committee that it maintains a comprehensive database of 
encrypted messaging applications that are developed and exploited by anti -national and 
terrorist elements. To monitor such covert activities, the NIA employs advanced tools 
developed by the Defence Research and Development Organisation (DRDO). In addition, the 
agency actively tracks the usage patterns of these encrypted applications through DRDO's 
technological platforms. Whenever a new application or digital platform emerges that could 
facilitate terrorist activities, the NIA conducts a thorough assessment, which includes 
analysing the IP addresses used to host the application. The agency performs detailed 
metadata analysis on the usage of these apps to extract critical information such as 
communication frequency, geolocation and timestamps. 
3.10.23  Upon identifying a suspicious entity based on these parameters, NIA leverages IP 
Detail Record (IPDR) analysis to de crypt and understand the usage patterns of such 
encrypted applications. Further, the agency utilises the NETRA platform developed by 
Defence Research and Development Organisation (DRDO) to capture and analyse data 
packets from users accessing the destination IPs of these applications. 
3.10.24  One of the major technical challenges faced by the NIA is identifying users who 
access encrypted apps through Virtual Private Networks (VPNs). VPNs route internet traffic 
through multiple intermediate servers, without  leaving any direct trace of user activity and 
complicating efforts to detect access to encrypted apps or dark web sites. In terms of legal 
challenges, the most important one pertains to failure of foreign based encrypted application 
platforms to provide d ata/IP addresses of the end users, due to our lack of jurisdiction over 
the servers hosted in such foreign countries. 

 
 
51 
 
Ensuring Integrity and Protection of Digital Evidence 
3.10.25 NIA highlighted the Committee that the agency ensures the protection and for ensic 
integrity of digital evidence during transnational cyber investigations through a robust 
combination of technical, procedural, legal and diplomatic measures. At the outset of 
investigations, Data Preservation Requests are dispatched via the National Central Bureau 
(NCB) the  CBI’s Nodal Point. The NIA meticulously maintains chain of custody 
documentation in accordance with the newly introduced certification provisions for electronic 
evidence under Section 63(4) of the Bharatiya Sakshya Adhiniyam, 2023 (BSA). This 
includes recording cryptographic hash values, such as SHA -256, generated at the time of 
evidence collection by any law enforcement agency, including foreign agencies acting upon 
NIA’s requests, thereby ensuring the evidence’s integrity during transfer and analysis. 
3.10.26 To secure cross -border transmission, encrypted files are exchanged on physical 
media such as CDs. Electronic storage devices containing digital evidence, accompanied by 
certifications under the Indian Evidence Act/BSA, are typ ically couriered physically under 
seal, particularly when treaty protocols demand such measures. These transmissions are 
coordinated through the Ministry of Home Affairs’ Central Authority under the Letters 
Rogatory (LRs)/Mutual Legal Assistance Treaties f ramework or via Interpol’s National 
Central Bureau for countries without treaty arrangements. 
3.10.27  For international evidence sharing, NIA utilises Mutual Legal Assistance Treaties 
(MLATs) with 42 countries, alongside multilateral conventions such as t he Harare 
Convention and the UN Convention on Transnational Organised Crime (UNTOC). These 
agreements facilitate the exchange of digital records including emails, IP logs, metadata and 
more governed by due process, dual criminality principles, judicial scr utiny and the 
respective domestic laws of the requested country for outgoing requests. 
3.10.28  In countries without MLATs, the NIA proceeds through Letter Rogatory issued by 
courts on the basis of Assurance of Reciprocity. Additionally, Memoranda of Under standing 
(MoUs) with various nations enable preliminary preservation of digital evidence, notification 
pertaining to the existence of data and provisional data sharing while formal MLAT 
responses are prepared.  
Legal and Procedural Gaps 
3.10.29 NIA informed the Committee that India's cybercrime enforcement landscape 
continues to face critical structural and diplomatic barriers. A major concern stems from data 
localisation gaps many prominent social media and messaging platforms store user data in 
foreign jurisdictions, significantly impeding timely access to digital evidence for domestic 
investigations. This delay not only weakens prosecution but also diminishes operational 
agility in fast -evolving cases. Additionally, the sluggish pace of mutual lega l assistance 
mechanisms has prompted urgent calls for fast -track treaty arrangements modeled on 
frameworks like the CLOUD Act. These reforms are seen as essential to securing prompt and 
lawful access to foreign -hosted data, enabling Indian authorities to e ffectively counter 
digitally enabled criminal and terror activities. 
  

 
 
52 
 
Recommendation/Observation 
3.10.30 The Committee notes the legal and procedural gaps identified by the NIA, 
particularly concerning delays in early stage data preserv ation. The Committe e 
recommends MHA for the establishment of standardised protocols and robust 
monitoring mechanisms to ensure the preservation requests and also to issue necessary 
directives mandating State police to initiate data preservation requests within the first 
24 hours of a cybercrime report to mitigate the risk of losing volatile evidence, such as 
IP logs and server files, when NIA assumes investigation from the State police . The 
Committee further recommends that the SSMIs be required to ensure timely provision 
of required data to NIA, regardless of the physical location of the data centers. 
3.11   Indian Cyber Crime Coordination Centre (I4C) 
Comprehensive Cybercrime Management 
3.11.1  I4C was launched by the MHA in 2 020, I4C acts as the central coordination, 
reporting, capacity -building and advisory body for cybercrime response in India. It is the 
national nodal agency for cybercrime coordination, protection and policy support. Its 
mandate is expressly geared towards creating a secure cyber ecosystem by actively 
facilitating prevention, detection and response to cybercrime threats across the country. As a 
multi-functional body, I4C operates through following seven verticals: (1) the National 
Cybercrime Reporting Portal  and Helpline 1930 facilitate citizen -centric complaint 
registration and immediate response; (2) the National Cyber Crime Research and Innovation 
Centre fosters indigenous technology development for cyber forensics and threat mitigation; 
(3) the Cybercrime  Ecosystem Management Unit vertical aims to develop an ecosystem 
conducive for effective neutralization of threats from cyber criminals by bringing together 
academia, industry and Government to combat cybercrime through multi -pronged and multi-
stakeholder efforts; (4) National Cybercrime Forensic Laboratory as a facility for forensic 
analysis and investigation of Cybercrime by use of the latest digital technology to support 
investigations; (5) Platform for Joint Cybercrime Investigation Team enables cross -
jurisdictional data sharing and collaborative investigations; (6) National Cybercrime Training 
Centre (NCTC) capacity building for law enforcement agencies, public prosecutors and 
judges in the field of cybercrime; and (7) National Cybercrime Threat Analyti cs Unit 
functions as a platform for analyzing cybercrime data and generating threat intelligence. 
National Cybercrime Coordination and Reporting 
3.11.2  The I4C serves as a pivotal nexus for aligning national efforts across law 
enforcement agencies, Government institutions, financial regulators, industry and civil 
society to combat cybercrime cohesively. Through its strategic coordination function, I4C 
facilitates the timely exchange of intelligence, technical resources and operational best 
practices, thereby reinforcing India’s overall cyber resilience. In its role as an enabler of 
multi-sectoral engagement, the I4C fosters a dynamic collaboration ecosystem connecting 
academia, industry leaders, enforcement bodies and citizens to drive innovation, ant icipate 
emerging digital threats and strengthen collective cyber defence mechanisms. 
3.11.3  The I4C administers a robust citizen -facing infrastructure to streamline cybercrime 
reporting and accelerate response mechanisms. The National Cybercrime Reporting  Portal 
(cybercrime.gov.in) serves as India’s official digital interface for lodging complaints across a 
spectrum of cyber offences, including financial fraud, online abuse and crimes targeting 

 
 
53 
 
vulnerable groups. Through its rapid complaint routing system,  the portal ensures that 
submissions are promptly directed to the relevant State or Union Territory law enforcement 
units, enabling efficient triage, follow -up and resolution. Complementing this is Helpline 
1930, a dedicated emergency channel for real-time intervention in financial cyber fraud cases 
facilitating immediate transaction blocking and minimizing victim losses through coordinated 
action with banks and enforcement agencies. 
Cyber Fraud Mitigation and Threat Analytics 
3.11.4  The I4C hosts the Cybe r Fraud Mitigation Centre (CFMC), a dedicated unit that 
facilitates rapid, multi -agency intervention against online financial crimes. CFMC brings 
together stakeholders from banking institutions, payment intermediaries, telecom operators 
and law enforcement  bodies to enable real -time coordination, which includes freezing 
suspicious transactions, tracing digital footprints and disrupting fraudulent accounts. In 
addition, I4C operates a nationwide threat dissemination mechanism, issuing regular 
technical alerts, advisories and situational digests on emerging cyber threats such as scams, 
fake applications and critical vulnerabilities. These communications are directed to relevant 
agencies, regulators and industry partners to reinforce India’s cyber fraud early w arning and 
response ecosystem. 
3.11.5  The I4C harnesses data -driven methodologies to elevate strategic foresight and 
operational precision in cybercrime response. Through its National Cybercrime Threat 
Analytics Unit, I4C applies advanced analytical tools  to detect emerging crime trends, 
geographic hotspots and behavioural patterns, thereby enabling targeted protection strategies 
and intelligence -led enforcement actions. Complementing this capability is its Suspect 
Repository, a continually updated public -accessible database that empowers both law 
enforcement and citizens to verify suspicious digital identifiers such as phone numbers, email 
IDs and URLs against documented cybercriminal profiles. This dual -pronged approach 
enhances situational awareness, fac ilitates rapid risk assessment and reinforces public safety 
in the digital ecosystem. 
Capacity Building and Cybersecurity Outreach 
3.11.6  The I4C undertakes targeted skill development and technical reinforcement to bolster 
India’s cybercrime response ecos ystem. Its National Cybercrime Training Centre (NCTC) 
vertical delivers customized programs for law enforcement personnel, prosecutors and 
judicial officers, equipping them to confront sophisticated cyber threats, master digital 
forensic techniques and add ress evolving criminal methodologies. In tandem, the National 
Cybercrime Forensic Laboratory provides comprehensive digital evidence analysis and 
investigative assistance to both State and Central agencies, thereby strengthening legal 
prosecution efforts and advancing deterrence mechanisms across jurisdictions. 
3.11.7  The I4C drives inclusive and proactive engagement to foster a secure digital culture. 
Through its Outreach Campaigns, including flagship efforts like the “Cyber Dost” initiative, 
I4C dissemin ates safe -cyber practices nationwide via social media messaging, educational 
workshops and targeted programs, with special emphasis on protecting vulnerable 
populations. Additionally, the Citizen Volunteers platform encourages community -led 
vigilance by enabling individuals to report illegal or harmful online content, thereby 
augmenting participatory cybersecurity and reinforcing collective responsibility across 
India’s digital ecosystem. 

 
 
54 
 
3.11.8  I4C plays a pivotal role in shaping India’s cybersecurity governance landscape. 
Through its Guideline s and SOPs, I4C formulates technical frameworks, standard operating 
procedures and strategic policy advisories targeted at Government bodies, industry 
stakeholders and academic institutions, ensuring a harmonized and forward -looking approach 
to mitigating present and emerging cyber threats. Additionally, it gives expert 
recommendations to develop best -practice protocols enabling India’s regulatory architecture 
to remain agile and responsive to technological shifts and evolving threat vectors. 
Recommendation/Observation 
3.11.9  The Committee suggests exploring the possibilities of conversion of complaints 
reported on the National Cybercrime Reporting Portal (NCRP) into e -FIRs thereby 
resolving legal issues arising during the refunding of the lien amount to th e victims on 
account of non-registration of the FIRs. 
3.11.10  The Committee is of the view that standardisation of cybercrime units at the 
State level. The Committee, therefore, recommends establishing State Cybercrime 
Coordination Centres (S4C) in all States/UTs, on lines similar to Indian Cyber Crime 
Coordination Centre (I4C), to ensure seamles s coordination between I4C and S tate-
level units for effective intelligence sharing. 
3.12 National Critical Information Infrastructure Protection Centre (NCIIPC) 
3.12.1  The National Critical Information Infrastructure Protection Centre (NCIIPC) is the 
designated national nodal agency for the protection of Critical Information Infrastructure 
(CII) in India. It functions under the National Technical Research Organi sation (NTRO) and 
is notified under Section 70A of the Information Technology Act, 2000. NCIIPC plays a 
central role in safeguarding systems that, if attacked or disabled, could severely impact 
national security, economic stability, public health, or order . CII is defined, as per Section 
70(1) of the IT Act, 2000 as computer resources whose incapacitation may have a debilitating 
impact on national interests. NCIIPC’s primary mandate includes identifying Protected 
Systems (PSs), conducting sectoral cybersecu rity audits, issuing threat advisories, supporting 
incident response and developing sector -specific cyber maturity. It currently oversees 427 
Protected Systems across 114 organis ations spread across seven critical sectors: Banking, 
financial services and i nsurance (BFSI), Power, Telecom, Transport, Health, Government, 
and Strategic/Public Enterprises. An additio nal 50 systems across 19 organis ations have 
already undergone assessments and await official notification. 
3.12.2  To support these institutions, NC IIPC coordinates lifecycle security ranging from 
architecture review and policy guidance to crisis management planning. The Centre assists in 
formulating Information Security Management Systems (ISMS), Service Level Agreements 
(SLAs), audit scopes, and pos t-incident review mechanisms for designated entities. Its 
advisory support also covers vendor policy, monitoring dashboard configurations, and 
periodic vulnerability analytics. 
3.12.3  One of NCIIPC’s most impactful interventions is the Automated Threat 
Dissemination Platform (ATDP), a real -time intelligence feed system for malicious IPs, 
domains, Indicators of Compromise (IoCs), and malware signatures. As of July 2025, 181 
critical infrastructure entities actively integrate these feeds into their cybersecu rity 
environments. In the first six months of 2025, NCIIPC identified and reported 1,172 phishing 
domains targeting CIIs, sharing these findings with MeitY and other enforcement agencies. 

 
 
55 
 
These actions demonstrate NCIIPC’s transition toward proactive and p redictive threat 
detection. Risk assessments carried out by NCIIPC include field visits, Security Operations 
Centre (SOC) evaluations, incident readiness reviews, and cybersecurity capacity scoring. 
Maturity benchmarking is conducted using a five -dimensional risk matrix that includes 
network security, application security, governance, security operations, and risk management. 
These findings inform both internal remediation and executive -level decision-making within 
CII organisations. 
3.12.4  In April 2025, NCIIPC organized its third national -level cybersecurity drill, the 
Critical Information Infrastructure Security Exercise (CII SECEX). Held in hybrid mode 
across four cities, it b rought together over 170 organis ations and more than 1,050 personnel 
for techn ical drills, executive scenario planning, and high -level decision maker briefings. 
Strategically structured across tiers technical teams, executives, and board -level leadership 
the exercise tested response protocols, inter -agency coordination, and foundati onal resilience 
in simulated breach conditions. 
3.12.5  In terms of policy leadership, NCIIPC released the National Cybersecurity Reference 
Framework (NCRF) in November 2024 a consolidated playbook of best practices, sectoral 
benchmarks, and risk control m atrices for CII protection. In parallel, work continues on a 
Cyber Security Capability Maturity Model (CSCMM) in partnership with IIT Kanpur to 
objectively measure cybersecurity maturity across CII verticals and enable long -term 
planning, tailored to Indian sectoral conditions. 
3.12.6  NCIIPC enforces accountability through the creation of Information Security 
Steering Committees (ISSCs) for each Protected System -owning organi sation. These 
committees chaired by the CEO or equivalent and meeting quarterly ar e responsible for post-
incident reflection, change management approvals, audit tracking, and risk resolution. 
NCIIPC is a standing member on these ISSCs and submits non -compliance escalations to 
relevant ministry Secretaries, Chief Secretaries, and the Nat ional Cyber Security Coordinator 
(NCSC) for appropriate action. 
3.12.7  Passive network -wide scans are also routinely conducted by NCIIPC to evaluate 
Internet-facing attack surfaces of CIIs. This includes the identification of insecure ports, 
leaked creden tials, subdomain enumeration, and re -exposed previously breached data. 
Vulnerabilities are reported confidentially to affected stakeholders and closely followed up to 
ensure timely closure. To support operational resilience, NCIIPC coordinates with CERT -In 
on malware reverse engineering, forensic correlation of digital attacks, and advanced 
persistent threat (APT) tracking. This joint model ensures both strategic oversight and 
immediate technical response during high-priority or complex cyber events. 
3.12.8  NCIIPC forms a critical layer of India’s national cyber resilience architecture. 
Through a structured model of pre -emptive threat detection, layered risk assessment, sectoral 
maturity benchmarking, and executive coordination, the Centre ensures that nati onal critical 
infrastructure is not only protected from evolving threats but is also enabled with the 
institutional knowledge and technologies needed to mitigate future risks. 
Recommendation/Observation 
3.12.9  To strengthen national cybersecurity resilience, the Committee recommends 
that NCIIPC should consider Sector -specific Computer Security Incident Response 
Teams(CSIRTs), such as CSIRT in Finance sector (CSIRT -Fin) and CSIRT in Power 

 
 
56 
 
sector (CSIRT -Power) to be expanded to more critical information infrastructure 
sectors such as healthcare, road transport, railways, aviation  and telecommunications, 
where they can address sector -specific threats like ransomware in hospitals, 
cyberattacks on transport infrastruc ture, and network intrusions in telecom systems to 
improve cyber resilience within respective critical sectors. The Committee is of the view 
that a national coordination mechanism should be established to unify sectoral CSIRTs 
through a central hub that facilitates real-time threat intelligence sharing, joint incident 
response protocols and secure communication channels.  
3.13 Ministry of External Affairs (MEA) and International Cooperation 
3.13.1  The Ministry of External Affairs (MEA) plays a strategic role in India’s global 
cybercrime response efforts, with a core focus on multilateral engagement, bilateral 
collaboration and policy harmonization. Through its dedicated Cyber Diplomacy Division, 
MEA leads India’s participation in international forums such  as the United Nations and 
regional cyber dialogues with partners including the US, Japan and the European Union. This 
division also spearheads the negotiation and operationalisation of bilateral Memoranda of 
Understanding (MoUs) that support joint efforts  in combating cyber threats, enabling 
information exchange, enhancing investigative collaboration and facilitating technology 
transfer. Recent MoUs reflect expanded cooperation in strategic policy dialogue and cyber 
law enforcement with countries like the US, Australia, Singapore, France and the UK, 
underscoring MEA’s commitment to strengthening India’s cyber resilience through 
diplomacy and global partnerships. 
3.13.2  Through the MEA, India is part of cross -border cyber incident response frameworks, 
including intelligence sharing, early warnings and joint investigations. MEA serves as India’s 
nodal point for requests made under bilateral treaties for assistance on cybercrime, especially 
when digital evidence or suspects are located abroad. India participat es in Information 
Security Dialogues under MEA stewardship, facilitating sharing of best practices and incident 
data between nations. MEA leads Indian delegations on cyber issues at the UN, including 
working on the development of international cybercrime t reaties and frameworks and plays a 
pivotal role in global cyber lawmaking processes, advocating norms of responsible state 
behaviour in cyberspace, principles of non -interference and protection of critical information 
infrastructure at UN Group of Governme ntal Experts (GGE) and Open -ended Working 
Group (OEWG). 
3.13.3  MEA provides advisory support to other ministries and enforcement agencies on 
mutual legal assistance treaties (MLATs) and legal frameworks for evidence exchange. The 
MEA’s guidance aligns Ind ian regulations with global norms and enables the lawful cross -
border transfer of evidence by channeling best practices and legal standards from 
international partnerships to Indian policymakers, law -enforcement agencies, and the 
judiciary. MEA, in collabo ration with domestic and foreign partners, organizes capacity 
building programs, international workshops and staff exchanges that train Indian officials in 
the latest cybercrime detection, forensics and response practices. 
3.13.4  MEA coordinates cross -border alerts and rapid response in cyber incidents, 
particularly those threatening India’s critical information infrastructure or economy. It 
represents India at multinational platforms like the G20 and BRICS, advocating global cyber 
security measures and frameworks that align with national interests. The MEA’s overarching 
role in cybercrime protection is to internationalize India’s fight against cyber threats, enhance 
cross-border cooperation, help shape robust legal and diplomatic frameworks and build 

 
 
57 
 
India’s global influence in the digital domain. It acts as the bridge between domestic agencies 
(like I4C and CERT -In) and international partners, ensuring that India remains resilient, 
proactive and influential in the evolving global cyber arena. 
Recommendation/Observation 
3.13.5  Recognising the critical need for rapid international cooperation, the Committee 
suggests instituting a dedicated 24x7 fusion desk or contact point staffed with 
multilingual, legally trained personnel, as envisioned under Article 42 of the UN 
Cybercrime Convention (2024). The Committee is of the view that such a unit would 
facilitate swift communication and coordination with foreign agencies on preservation 
and cooperation requests, thereby mitigating the currently prolonged execution  
timelines of MLATs and Letters Rogatory. 
3.13.6  The Committee observes the challenge posed by  the lack of a statutory mandate 
for compelling data disclosure from foreign service providers (cloud/email/VoIP), where 
most critical data is stored outside Ind ia. The Committee is of the view that relying on 
voluntary cooperation and slow Mutual Legal Assistance Treaty (MLAT) processes 
delays access to vital digital evidence, affecting cybercrime investigations. To address 
this, the Committee recommends pursuing  legal and diplomatic measures to enable 
enforceable and timely data disclosure. 
3.14  Centre for Development of Advanced Computing (C-DAC) 
Pivotal Role of C-DAC in Cybersecurity Advancement 
3.14.1  C-DAC, with support from the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology 
(MeitY) has played a vital role in strengthening India’s cybersecurity ecosystem through 
extensive research and development in critical areas such as mobile security, cyber forensics, 
threat analysis, security operations, blockchain,  cryptography and deepfake detection. These 
efforts have resulted in the creation of indigenous products and solutions that are being 
effectively used by citizens, law enforcement agencies, defence organisations, enterprises and 
critical infrastructure sec tors to safeguard against cyber threats and enhance investigative 
capabilities. 
3.14.2  Recognising the shortage of skilled cybersecurity professionals in the country, C -
DAC has also launched various programs focused on skill development, capacity building  
and awareness generation. Specialised training in cyber forensics has been provided for law 
enforcement and judiciary personnel to build a responsive and resilient cybercrime handling 
framework. In alignment with the Government’s vision of a "Secure Digit al India", C -DAC 
remains committed to continual innovation and global best practices.  
Development of Key Indigenous Cybersecurity Tools 
3.14.3  Some of the key cybersecurity tools developed by C -DAC include the Deepfake 
Detection Tool, which is currently in the testing phase with 89% accuracy on global 
benchmarks and shared with law enforcement agencies for evaluation. M-Kavach 2 is an end-
user mobile security application available to all android users via the Google Play Store and 
provides protection against hidden apps, misconfigurations and risky software. Vishleshak is 
another tool used by security analysts for in -depth threat analysis of mobile applications, 
specifically targeting access behaviours such as camera, location and microphone usage. 

 
 
58 
 
Parikshan aids android developers by identifying and reporting vulnerabilities through 
automated static and dynamic code analysis, while Praamaanik, built on the National 
Blockchain Framework, helps verify app authenticity and developer ownership. Integration 
with M -Kavach 2 allows users to easily cross -check and avoid downloading fake 
applications. 
3.14.4  C-DAC's other strategic contributions include CDACSIEM, a security monitoring 
solution that aggregates and analyzes logs across networks to provide holistic vi sibility to 
Security Operations Centre (SOC) analysts. It was notably deployed during the G20 summit. 
Similarly, Rakshak DNS serves as a secure enterprise DNS resolver that identifies and blocks 
malicious web domains. In the IoT domain, C -DAC, in collabora tion with Standardization 
Testing and Quality Certification (STQC) has developed the IoT Security Evaluation Hand 
book to ensure devices used within Government or enterprise settings meet baseline security 
standards. 
Cyber Security Audit and Forensic Analysis 
3.14.5  To address the growing need for cybersecurity assessments, C -DAC plays a major 
role in conducting rigorous cyber audits and forensic analysis across India’s digital 
ecosystem. These audits help identify operational vulnerabilities, enforce regu latory 
compliance and support proactive risk mitigation especially in sectors l ike finance, health 
care, defenc e, telecom and public utilities. Both technical (vulnerability assessments, 
penetration testing) and procedural (policy adherence, access control  reviews) audits are 
conducted in alignment with standards such as CERT -In guidelines, ISO 27001 and various 
domain-specific regulations mandated by bodies like RBI, IRDAI and NPCI. These audits are 
particularly critical for small and medium enterprises an d Government departments that may 
lack internal cybersecurity capacity. 
3.14.6  As part of its forensic support role, C -DAC has developed the CyberCheck Suite, a 
powerful, web-based forensic tool that enables law enforcement officers to efficiently recover 
and analyse data from digital devices. Along with this toolkit, C -DAC extends broader 
support during incident response, including memory forensics, malware reverse engineering 
and assisting in audits following breaches, especially in cases involving ranso mware or 
insider threats. By providing both technological tools and capacity -building initiatives, C -
DAC continues to contribute significantly toward building a resilient and secure digital 
infrastructure across India. 
Information Security Education and Awareness 
3.14.7  The Information Security Education and Awareness (ISEA) program, implemented 
by C-DAC under MeitY, is a key initiative to combat cybercrime in India through education, 
awareness and capacity building. It conducts nationwide digital safety c ampaigns, supports 
higher cybersecurity education via top institutes and trains IT professionals, auditors and law 
enforcement. ISEA also provides specialized courses for police and judiciary on cyber laws, 
digital forensics and online fraud investigations . By bridging academic learning with real -
world cybersecurity practices, ISEA helps build a skilled, future -ready workforce critical to 
safeguarding India’s digital infrastructure against evolving cyber threats. 
  

 
 
59 
 
Recommendation/Observation 
3.15  Indian Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT-In)  
3.15.1  The Indian Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT -In) operates under the 
Ministry of Electronics and In formation Technology (MeitY). It is the national nodal agency 
responsible for addressing cybersecurity threats, coordinating incident responses, issuing 
advisories and building cyber resilience across Government, critical infrastructure and private 
sectors. CERT-In was designated as the national incident response body under Section 70B of 
the Information Technology (Amendment) Act, 2008. Its statutory mandate includes: 
• Collection, analysis and dissemination of cybersecurity threat information. 
• Forecasting and issuing early warnings. 
• Emergency response coordination during cyber incidents. 
• Promoting security awareness and technical assistance. 
3.15.2  CERT-In provides 24x7 incident detection and response support, helping 
organisations mitigate attacks such as phishing, ransomware, DDoS and data breaches. It 
coordinates containment and recovery efforts following major cyber incidents, while ensuring 
communication between affected organi sations, sectoral regulators and law enforcement. It 
regularly publishes bull etins, security advisories and vulnerability notes regarding newly 
discovered threats. Helps institutions identify and patch weaknesses by providing technical 
mitigation steps including zero-day vulnerabilities, malware families, or phishing campaigns. 
In FY 2024–25, CERT-In conducted 9,700+ cybersecurity audits across ministries, PSUs and 
critical infrastructure facilities to evaluate compliance with security controls. 
3.15.3  CERT-In has partnered with BITS Pilani (2025) to launch certification programs o n 
cybersecurity governance and technical operations for public sector professionals. Regional 
and sector -specific cyber awareness campaigns are conducted by CERT -In, alongside 
MeitY’s Information Security Education and Awareness (ISEA) initiative. CERT -In leads 
India’s participation in global cyber exercises like “Synergy,” collaborates with foreign 
CERTs and facilitates international intelligence exchanges through secured frameworks. 
Cyber Swachhta Kendra (CSK) was launched in 2017 and operated by CERT -In in 
partnership with C -DAC, the CSK provides free cleanup tools for citizens to detect and 
remove botnets and malware. These tools include M -Kavach for mobile security and 
AppSamvid for application whitelisting. 
3.15.4  CERT-In has formulated a Cyber Crisis  Management Plan (CCMP) that guides all 
central agencies, regulators and critical institutions in preparing and responding cyber -attacks 
3.14.8  The Committee appreciates the insightful and forward-thinking of C-DAC to 
harness Artificial intelligence (AI) and Machine Learning (ML) technologies for 
predictive cyber threat intelligence, which is crucial for safeguarding critical national 
infrastructure in today’s rapidly evolving cybercrime. The Committee recommends C -
DAC to explore the development of an AI/ML-powered that predicts cyber threats to 
critical sectors by integrating data from existing tools such as Deepfake Detection, 
Vishleshak, CDACSIEM and Rakshak DNS, along with external global threat 
intelligence. The Committee suggests creation of a national training platform for 
cybersecurity that uses virtual reality (VR), thereby offering realistic practice sessions 
and certification program to build a skilled workforce across the law enforcement 
agencies, judiciary and enterprise. 

 
 
60 
 
and cyber-terrorism. CCMP provides strategic framework to coordinate recovery from cyber -
crisis and enhance resilience.  Simulations, workshops and compliance assessments are 
conducted under the CCMP mandate. CERT -In also regularly conducts workshops for 
Government bodies and key organi sations to sensitize them about the cyber security threat 
landscape and enabling them to prepare & implement the CCMP. So far, 205 such CCMP 
workshops have been conducted. 
3.15.5  CERT-In’s Sectoral coordination involves collaborative efforts with sector -specific 
CSIRTs such as CSIRT -FIN and CSIRT -Power, facilitating rapid cross -sectoral respo nse 
mechanisms. It also includes joint publication of Digital Threat Reports, exemplified by 
initiatives like CERT -In’s BFSI Sector Advisory Report 2024. The enforcement and 
regulatory role of Cert -In includes issuing legally binding directions under Secti on 70B on 
matters such as incident reporting timelines (within six hours for specific incidents), 
synchronisation of system clocks using NTP and compliance requirements related to KYC, 
log retention and cloud services as per the April 2022 directive. It al so involves active 
coordination with IT intermediaries, data centers, VPN providers and other digital service 
platforms. 
Recommendation/Observation 
3.15.6  The Committee acknowledges CERT -In’s crucial role in safeguarding India’s 
cybersecurity by effectively managing threat detection, incident response and 
coordination across multiple sectors. To strengthen its capabilities further, the 
Committee recommends the adoption of an AI -driven Cyber Threat Intelligence 
Platform that enables quicker and mor e intelligent identification and handling of cyber 
threats. Further, the Committee encourages CERT-In to build strong partnerships with 
start-ups and academic institutions to foster innovation and develop a skilled 
cybersecurity workforce. The Committee al so recommends conducting regular, flexible 
cybersecurity drills in collaboration with sectoral teams to ensure CERT -In remains 
agile and prepared to tackle emerging cyber challenges promptly. 
3.16  Ministry of Education 
Department of School Education & Literacy (DoSEL)  
3.16.1  The Department of School Education & Literacy (DoSEL), under the Ministry of 
Education, plays a vital role in building a resilient education system and promoting digital 
safety among schoolchildren, teachers and stakeholders across I ndia. DoSEL has 
mainstreamed digital safety, cyber hygiene and responsible use of technology in school 
curricula following the recommendations of the National Education Policy (NEP) 2020. 
These initiatives raise awareness among students and teachers about safe internet practices, 
cyberbullying, phishing and online threats. The department conducts regular in -service and 
online training for teachers on digital safety and cybersecurity, equipping them to identify 
and address issues like cyberbullying, online fraud and digital addiction. 
3.16.2  It was informed to the Committee that campaigns and classroom modules by DoSEL 
promote safe digital behaviour, with a special focus on marginalized groups and gender -
sensitive content, often in partnership with autonomou s bodies and State Governments. 
Samagra Shiksha Abhiyan, a flagship scheme supports the introduction of digital literacy 
programs, safe network protocols and infrastructural safeguards in Government schools. This 
includes filtered access to online resource s and the encouragement of parent involvement in 

 
 
61 
 
digital safety. DoSEL has issued advisories on preventing cyber incidents in educational 
institutions, including protocols for parental engagement and reporting of cybercrimes 
affecting minors. There are sch ool and district -level grievance redressal mechanisms to 
handle reports of cyber offences against children, ensuring timely intervention and support. 
3.16.3  DoSEL contributes significantly to India’s cyber safety ecosystem by promoting 
awareness and responsible digital behaviour among students and communities. Through 
Workshops & Collaborations, DoSEL actively partners with entities such as NCERT, 
National Commission for Protection of Child Rights (NCPCR) and State police to conduct 
educational sessions focusing on cyber safety, online privacy and the risks associated with 
social media usage. To ensure broad accessibility, DoSEL also leads efforts in Promoting 
Responsible Internet Use by developing digital safety resources in multiple Indian languages 
and formats tailored for diverse audiences, including rural and underserved populations. 
These initiatives foster inclusive participation and empower citizens with  the knowledge 
needed to navigate the digital world safely. 
Department of Higher Education (DoHE)  
3.16.4  The Department of Higher Education (DHE) is responsible for driving technological 
advancement, digital literacy and cyber safety awareness at the ter tiary level of education 
throughout India’s universities and colleges. The Digital Learning Platforms administered by 
the Department of Higher Education such as SWAYAM, the National Digital Library and 
Virtual Labs promote inclusive and accessible educatio n. These platforms incorporate 
operational guidelines and, in some cases, training modules that encourage responsible use of 
digital resources. In line with ICT -Enabled Education, DoHE policies emphasize cyber 
ethics, data privacy and cybersecurity as inte gral components of “21st Century Skills,” 
embedding cyber protection content across both technical and general curricula. To further 
promote hands -on engagement, the department facilitates Academic Collaborations, 
including workshops and hackathons (e.g., Smart India Hackathon) that address cybersecurity 
challenges and spur digital innovation among students and faculty. 
3.16.5  DoHE apprised the Committee that it plays a guiding role in embedding 
cybersecurity and digital protection across India's higher ed ucation landscape. Through UGC 
Guidelines, the University Grants Commission under DoHE issues advisories to universities 
and colleges on cybersecurity measures, safeguarding research data and preventing 
cybercrime within campus environments. Additionally, the National Mission on Education 
through ICT (NMEICT) supports the rollout of secure digital platforms, disseminates cyber 
awareness resources and ensures both students and faculty can access relevant cyber safety 
learning tools. DoHE also prioritizes Res earch and Faculty Training, offering specialized 
modules in cyber forensics, digital ethics and online intellectual property to faculty members 
and research scholars across academic institutions, thereby enhancing institutional capacity 
and knowledge resilience. 
3.16.6  The DoHE collaborates with I4C, MeitY, and law-enforcement agencies to spearhead 
nationwide awareness initiatives, empowering student bodies as cyber -safety ambassadors to 
cultivate a secure and digitally resilient academic ecosystem on campuses and in surrounding 
communities. 
  

 
 
62 
 
Recommendation/Observation 
3.16.7  The Committee recommends that to effectively build a resilient digital 
generation, cybercrime prevention and cyber hygiene education should be integrated 
into the school curric ulum starting from early grades to senior secondary level.  The 
Committee further recommends that this curriculum inclusion be mandated for all 
schools irrespective whether it governed by Central board or State board to ensure no 
child is left behind.  The C ommittee also recommends for introduction of mandatory 
cyber education subject in non -technical courses across colleges and universities, 
integrating modules on cyber awareness, digital safety, responsible online conduct and 
basics of data privacy. 
3.17  Ministry of Corporate Affairs 
Safeguarding India’s Corporate Digital Infrastructure 
3.17.1  The Ministry of Corporate Affairs (MCA) operates the MCA21 portal, a key digital 
platform that enables regulatory compliance and corporate governance for companies and 
LLPs (Limited Liability Partnership) registered in India. It facilitates statutory filings under 
the Companies Act and LLP Act, 2008 forming a foundational part of India’s corporate 
regulatory ecosystem. As digital governance deepens, the cyber threat landscape has grown 
more complex, making the security and resilience of MCA21 critical not only to protect 
sensitive stakeholder data but also to uphold the integrity of regulatory processes and support 
national cybersecurity objectives. 
3.17.2  To address these evolving challenges, MCA has implemented a robust, multi -layered 
cybersecurity strategy in alignment with guidelines issued by the Ministry of Electronics and 
Information Technology (MeitY). This includes conducting comprehensive security audits 
covering all applications and infrastructure and performing regular Vulnerability Assessment 
and Penetration Testing (VAPT) through CERT -IN empanelled vendors. Threat intelligence 
is actively utilised through the blocking of malicious indicators such as IP ad dresses, URLs 
and IOCs, based on advisories from CERT -IN and the National Critical Information 
Infrastructure Protection Centre (NCIIPC), with real -time threat reporting mechanisms in 
place. 
3.17.3  Additionally, MCA follows a structured monthly cycle for patch management and 
maintains secure configuration baselines to ensure systems are up to date and resilient. The 
guidelines from MeitY on information security and cybersecurity are fully adopted across the 
platform. Cybersecurity practices and advisories applicable to Government employees are 
also incorporated into MCA21 operations to promote cyber hygiene and reduce human -
related risks. Collectively, these measures demonstrate MCA’s commitment to maintaining a 
secure digital infrastructure that underpins the compliance and governance of India’s 
corporate sector. 
Preventive Measures in MCA21v3 
3.17.4  MCA21v3 employs a multi-layered, advanced cybersecurity architecture aligned with 
MeitY, CERT -IN, NCIIPC and industry standards to address the growing threat landscape 
surrounding digital regulatory platforms. At the perimeter and application layer, MCA21v3 
leverages a CDN -based Web Application Firewall (WAF) from Akamai to defend against 
sophisticated attacks such as zero -day exploits, DDoS, SQL injection and XSS (Cross -Site 

 
 
63 
 
Scripting). This is supported by an anti -DDoS system that monitors and mitigates malicious 
inbound traffic in real time and a Network Intrusion Prevention System (NIPS) that 
continuously scans for and blocks, harmful network activities. 
3.17.5  Within the endpoint, server and infrastructure layers, the platform incorporates an in -
house WAF, combining with external firewalls to secure the borders between internal and 
external networks, while internal firewalls based on Zero Trust model segmen t resources to 
contain potential breaches. Antivirus protections are regularly updated to detect malware and 
abnormal behaviours across all servers and endpoints. Secure VPN tunnels are mandatory for 
server-to-server and endpoint communication encrypting t raffic and safeguarding against 
interception such as Man -in-the-Middle attacks. To secure email and user access, MCA21v3 
restricts user internet access via a web proxy and permits only pre -approved domains. Email 
security is further enhanced with gateway -levels and boxing to isolate and examine 
suspicious attachments and links, effectively blocking phishing and malware delivery to end 
users.  
3.17.6  Strategically, the architecture employs micro -segmentation techniques to reduce the 
attack surface, isolating critical assets and limiting lateral movement in case of compromise. 
The network is also organized into well -defined zones (DMZs, MZs), each governed by 
stringent firewall policies to rigorously separate trusted and untrusted resources. This 
architectural approach ensures that the MCA21v3 platform remains resilient, preserving data 
confidentiality and integrity, ensuring service availability and supporting compliance with 
India’s national and sectoral cybersecurity requirements. 
Security Monitoring and Compliance Framework 
3.17.7  MCA21 has established a comprehensive real -time security monitoring and response 
system to safeguard its digital ecosystem. At its core is a Security Information and Event 
Management (SIEM) tool that collects, correlates and anal yzes security event data across the 
platform, enabling efficient detection and response to cyber threats. This is supported by a 
24x7 Security Operations Centre (SOC) that ensures constant surveillance and immediate 
resolution of any anomalies. To enhance resilience further, MCA21 employs a structured 
Vulnerability Management program, continuously identifying and mitigating security risks. 
Tools like Tenable VA support infrastructure vulnerability scanning, while SAST and DAST 
help address application -level weaknesses during both development and deployment stages. 
A rigorous patch management process ensures timely updates across all software layers, 
minimizing exposure to known vulnerabilities through a well -defined patching cycle. 
Systems are maintained at n-1 version levels, ensuring they are secure and current. 
3.17.8  In addition to these internal measures, third -party penetration tests are conducted 
regularly by CERT -IN empanelled agencies. These simulations reveal hidden security flaws 
in MCA21 web appl ications, ensuring independent validation and strengthening the 
platform’s overall defence. The entire cybersecurity architecture is anchored in strict 
compliance with national and international standards. Internet access is highly restricted and 
zero-trust principles govern internal system interactions, ensuring access is always verified. 
MCA21 integrates ISO 27001 best practices for structured risk management and regularly 
undergoes internal and STQC -conducted security audits. Compliance with guidelines f rom 
CERT-IN and NCIIPC further reinforces its cyber defenc e posture, making MCA21 a secure 
and resilient platform for corporate governance in India.  
  

 
 
64 
 
Recommendation/Observation 
3.17.9  The Committee appreciates the comprehensive and robust cybersecurity 
measures already implemented in MCA21 portal to protect India’s corporate 
regulatory ecosystem. However, recognizing the increasing sophistication of cyber 
threats, especially those exploiting shell companies and fraudulent corporate entities, 
the Committee recommends the Ministry of Corporate Affairs the adoption of an AI -
powered, blockchain -based verification and continuous monitoring system integrated 
into MCA21. The Committee is of the view that this framework should enable seamless 
and automated collab oration with law enforcement and regulatory agencies for rapid 
investigation and action of any fraudulent activities.

Part IV: Prevention Strategies 
4.1 Legal framework available to prevent cybercrimes 
4.1.1  The legal mechanism available to prevent cybercrimes in India is a comprehensive 
and evolving framework combining legislation, rules, institutional setups and technological 
initiatives to address the growing cyber threat landscape effectively. Cyber Crimes in India 
are primarily addressed through various legislations as mentioned below: 
● Information Technology Act, 2000 
● Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023 
● Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023 
● Bharatiya Nagrik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023 
● Bharatiya Sakshya Adhiniyam, 2023 
● The Indecent Representation of Women (Prohibition Act), 1986 
● The Young Persons (Harmful Publication) Act, 1956 
● Protection of Children from Sexual Offences Act, 2012 
4.1.2  MeitY looks after the “Matters relating to Cyber Laws, administration of the 
Information Techn ology Act, 2000 (IT Act) and other IT related laws” according to 
Government of India (Allocation of Business) Rules, 1961 (AoBR). The enactment of IT Act, 
2000 designed initially to facilitate e -commerce and electronic governance aligned with the 
Model Law  on Electronic Commerce adopted by the United Nations Commission on 
International Trade Law. Over time, it has become the core statute addressing cyber offences 
such as hacking, unauthorised data access, identity theft, cyber terrorism and publication of 
obscene material online. Several rules under the IT Act bolster this, like the Information 
Technology (Procedure and Safeguards for Blocking Access of Information by Public) Rules, 
2009, the Information Technology (Reasonable security practices and procedur es and 
sensitive personal data or information) Rules, 2011 and the Information Technology 
(Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021.

4.1.4  Commonly used legal provisions for preventing cybercrimes 
i. Section 79(3)(b) of the IT Act establishes the liability framework for IT 
intermediaries such as social media platforms and internet service providers regarding 
the third-party content they host. These intermediaries enjoy “safe harbour” protection 
from li ability for such content only if they exercise due diligence and comply with 
government or court orders to take down unlawful material. According to Section 
79(3)(b), when an intermediary receives actual knowledge such as a court order or 
official notification that certain content on their platform is being used to commit an 
unlawful act, they must promptly remove or disable access to it, or else they risk 
losing their immunity and face legal consequences. 
The Information Technology (Intermediary Guidelines  and Digital Media Ethics 
Code) Rules, 2021, particularly Rule 3(1)(d), further clarify these responsibilities, 
requiring intermediaries to act swiftly, within 36 hours, upon receiving a valid order 
related to unlawful content which can include material th reatening national security, 
public order, decency, defamation, or incitement to offences. 
As of November 30, 2024, 16 States namely - Bihar, Haryana, Karnataka, Kerala, 
Nagaland, Odisha, Punjab, Rajasthan, Sikkim, Tamil Nadu, Uttar Pradesh, 
Uttarakhand, We st Bengal, Tripura, Manipur, Goa, have notified the nodal 
officer/authorize agency under section 79(3)(b) of the IT Act, 2000 for the takedown 
of unlawful content. Further, the Ministry of Home Affairs has designated the Indian 
Cyber Crime Coordination Cen tre (I4C) as the nodal agency to perform the functions 
mandated under this provision.  
To streamline the process and ensure faster, coordinated action, I4C developed the 
SAHYOG portal, a centralized platform where authorised agencies, ministries and IT 
intermediaries, including prominent platforms like Josh, Quora, Telegram, Amazon, 
Apple, Google and Sharechat, collaborate to report and remove or disable unlawful 
online content. Since its inception, the portal has facilitated the takedown of over 
83,149 unlawful content. 
This coordinated effort has led to targeted actions such as blocking 3,255 fraudulent 
Skype IDs that impersonated government bodies and were involved in extortion -
related financial fraud; removing 2,745 Instagram accounts spreading fraudulent stock 
market advertisements and scams; shutting down 82 Telegram groups trading mule 
bank accounts used for illegal loan apps and fake stock trading; and taking down 120 
fraudulent investment and loan apps as well as related digital entities impersonatin g 
legitimate financial advisors. Further, more than 76,599 WhatsApp accounts 
associated with organized cybercrime syndicates from Southeast Asia have been 
blocked for activities including digital extortion and illegal stock trading scams.  
ii. Section 69A of the IT Act empowers the Central Government or its authorised officers 
to block public access to any information available on a computer resource when 
deemed necessary in the interests of sovereignty and integ rity of India, defenc e of 
India, security of the state, friendly relations with foreign states, public order or to 
prevent incitement to the commission of any cognizable offence related to these 

 
 
68 
 
grounds. The blocking process is governed by the Information Technology (Procedure 
and Safeguards for Blocking for Access of Information by Public) Rules, 2009, which 
set out a structured procedure ensuring safeguards and due process. Under this 
procedure, a Nodal Officer first initiates the blocking request if content is considered 
harmful to national interests. This request is examined by a Designated Officer and 
then referred to an Inter -Departmental Committee comprising members from various 
Ministries. The Committee reviews the request and may allow the intermediary or the 
originator to present their case, exce pt in emergency situations where immediate 
blocking can occur. Upon the Committee’s recommendation, the Secretary of the 
Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology (MeitY) approves the order, 
which the Designated Officer then issues to the intermediary for compliance.  
In urgent cases, interim blocking orders can be issued with prior approval, subject to 
review within 48 hours. Intermediaries who fail to comply with the blocking orders 
under Section 69A face stringent consequences, including impriso nment of up to 
seven years and fines. Since 2020 to November 2024, approximately 3,265 URLs, 
online accounts, websites and 625 malicious apps have been blocked under Section 
69A to protect India's digital ecosystem. 
iii. Section 69B of the IT Act, grants the Ce ntral Government the power to authorise any 
Government agency to monitor and collect traffic data or information generated, 
transmitted, received, or stored in any computer resource, with the purpose of 
enhancing cybersecurity and preventing cyber intrusio ns or the spread of harmful 
computer contaminants. This traffic data includes details such as the origin, 
destination, route, time and other relevant information related to communication. 
Intermediaries or persons responsible for computer resources are obl igated to provide 
technical assistance and facilitate online access to such data when requested by the 
authorised Government agency. Failure to comply with these directions can result in 
penalties, including imprisonment for up to three years and fines. Th e operation of 
Section 69B is complemented by the Information Technology (Procedure and 
Safeguards for Monitoring and Collecting Traffic Data or Information) Rules, 2009, 
which prescribe procedures and safeguards for the monitoring and collection of traffi c 
data or information for cybersecurity purposes. 
 
iv. Section 69 of the IT Act, 2000 grants the Central and State Governments the authority 
to intercept, monitor, or decrypt information transmitted, received, or stored in any 
computer resource. This provision is invoked when it is necessary in the interest of the 
sovereignty or integrity of India, defence of India, security of the State, friendly 
relations with foreign States or public order or for preventing incitement to the 
commission of any cognizable offe nce relating to above or for investigation of any 
offence. Intermediaries, including ISPs, social media platforms and telecom service 
providers, are required to extend technical assistance and comply with lawful 
directions issued under this section. Failure to comply with such directions may result 
in penalties, including imprisonment of up to seven years and fines. To prevent misuse 
of powers under Section 69, the Information Technology (Procedure and Safeguards 
for Interception, Monitoring and Decryption of Information) Rules, 2009 establish a 
detailed framework. These rules ensure that interception orders are issued only by 
competent authorities with prior approval from a Secretary -level officer and are 
subject to review by a high-level committee. 
 

 
 
69 
 
v. Section 70B of the IT Act establishes the Indian Computer Emergency Response 
Team (CERT -In) as the national agency responsible for cybersecurity incident 
response, with a focus on prevention and proactive measures. CERT -In’s 
responsibilities include collecting, analysing and disseminating information on cyber 
incidents, issuing forecasts and alerts and coordinating responses to cybersecurity 
threats. The agency also plays a crucial role in issuing guidelines, advisories, 
vulnerability notes and white papers aimed  at preventing cyber incidents and 
promoting best practices in information security. By offering recommendations on 
procedures for cybersecurity, response actions and reporting mechanisms, CERT -In 
seeks to prevent cyber threats before they escalate. The In dian Computer Emergency 
Response Team (CERT -In) Rules, 2013 (copy attached) establish the operational 
procedures for CERT -In’s mandate under Section 70B, focusing on prevention, 
coordination and response to cyber threats while ensuring compliance and 
accountability across sectors. 
 
vi. The Information Technology (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics 
Code) Rules, 2021, prescribe due diligence to be observed by intermediaries, 
including Social Media Intermediaries (SMIs) and Significant Social Media 
Intermediaries (SSMIs). 
(a) Rule 3(1)(d) of the IT Rules 2021 mandates that intermediaries, such as social 
media platforms and other digital services, must ensure compliance with the 
laws of India and refrain from publishing or transmitting content that 
promotes, encourages, or incites illegal activities, including a ny offensive 
material related to national security, public order, or religious harmony.  
(b) Rule 3(1)(g)  mandates preservation of information and records by the 
intermediaries for 180 days for investigative purposes or for a longer period if 
so, required by the court or an authorised government agency.  
(c) Rule 3(1)(h) requires the intermediary to retain user registration information 
for 180 days after any cancellation or withdrawal of registration. 
(d) Rule 3(1)(j) speaks directly to the requirements of law enforcem ent agencies 
and mandates that an intermediary shall ‘as soon as possible’ but not later 
than 72 hours of receipt of a written order, provide information/assistance to a 
lawfully authorised government agency for investigative, protective or cyber 
security activities, for verification of identity or for prevention, detection, 
investigation or prosecution of offences.  
(e) Rule 3(2)(a)(ii)  provides that the Grievance Officer of an intermediary is 
obligated to receive and acknowledge any order/notice/direction iss ued by an 
appropriate government/competent authority or competent court.  
(f) Rule 4(1)(a), (b) and (c)  requires an SSMI must appoint a Chief Compliance 
Officer, a nodal contact person  and a Resident Grievance Officer. 
(g) Rule 7 provides that where an intermedia ry fails to observe the IT Rules, 
2021, the exemption available under Section 79(1) of the IT Act, 2000 shall 
not be available to such intermediary and consequently the intermediary shall 
be liable for punishment under any law for the time being in force 

 
 
70 
 
vii. Section 12AA of the Prevention of Money Laundering Act, 2002 (PMLA)  
provides conditions for re -verification and disallowing of specified transactions in 
certain cases which involves cash withdrawals and deposits and transactions where 
there is a risk of mon ey laundering and terror financing.   For such transactions banks 
are expected to; 
(a) Verify the identity of the transacting parties,  
(b)Take additional prescribed steps to examine ownership, source of funds and 
financial position. 
(c) Record the purpose  behind the transactions and the intended nature of 
relationship.  
Failing which, the specified transaction should be disallowed.  
Additionally, in cases where transaction or series of specified transactions undertaken 
by a client is considered suspicious or likely to involve proceeds of crime, the 
reporting entity shall also increase the future monitoring of the business relationship 
with the client. This provision is key to identification of money mules and preventing 
money laundering from going through. 
viii. RBI Master Directions on KYC 2016 Updated 2024. 
RBI’s Master directions prescribe the procedure of customer onboarding by spelling 
out requirements for Customer Due Diligence (CDD), Officially Verifiable 
Documents (OVDs) required, risk -based classification  of accounts, verification of 
existence of customers among others. However, due to the laxity on the part of Banks 
and FIs and lack of enforcement of these directions, fraudsters have been abusing the 
Banking ecosystem.   
RBI Guidelines on Regulation of Payment Aggregators and Payment Gateways It 
prescribes CDD requirements on part of the banks for onboarding of Payment 
Aggregators (PAs). 
RBI Advisory dated 10th August 2022. It states that banks are advised to put in place 
a comprehensive mechanism duly app roved by the Board/Audit Committee of the 
Board to identify and monitor the suspected money mule accounts. 
RBI Advisory dated 19th November 2024 It advises banks to further strengthen their 
policies, procedures, systems and controls for effectively thwarti ng cyber -enabled 
frauds being perpetrated through banking channels and to guard against the use of 
money mule accounts for such fraudulent transactions. This advisory further improves 
upon the comprehensive mechanism put in place by banks pursuant to the Advisory 
dated August 10, 2022  and lays down detailed instructions for ‘Identification of 
Suspected Money Mules and Action thereof’. 
ix. The Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023 (BNS) :  The BNS modernizes and toughens 
India’s criminal law provisions for cybercrime by categorizing organized cybercrime 
as a distinct and severely punishable offence, expanding punishment for fraud and 
misinformation via electronic means and incorporating offences related to digital 
expressions affecting sovereignty, public order, religious harmony  and morality. 
Some of the sections applicable to cyber offences are mentioned below:- 

 
 
71 
 
 Section 77  covers the act of watching, capturing, or sharing images of a 
woman engaged in a private act without her consent, which constitutes 
"voyeurism." If these images are published or transmitted by electronic means, 
such conduct also falls within the purview of cybercrime. 
 Section 78 addresses the offence of stalking, encompassing both physical and 
online stalking.  
 Section 111 stands out as a key provision targeting continuing unlawful 
activities, including cyber crimes, committed ei ther individually or as part of 
an organized crime syndicate. The punishment is minimum of 5 years (non -
bailable) which may extend to imprisonment for life and fine not less than ₹ 5 
lakh and fine not less than ₹10 lakh in case death of any is caused.  
 Section 152 provides punishment for any act that excites or attempts to excite, 
secession or armed rebellion or subversive activities, or encourages feelings of 
separatist activities or endangers sovereignty or unity and integrity of India 
purposely or knowingly, by words, either spoken or written, or by signs, or by 
visible representation, or by electronic communication or by use of financial 
mean, or otherwise.  
 Section 196  Promotes or attempts enmity between different groups on 
grounds of religion, race, p lace of birth, residence, language, etc. and doing 
acts prejudicial to maintenance of harmony by words, either spoken or written, 
or by signs or by visible representations or through electronic communication 
or otherwise. 
 Section 197 Publishing any imputat ions, assertions prejudicial to national 
integration by words either spoken or written or by signs or by visible 
representations or through electronic communication or otherwise. 
 Section 199 Deliberate and malicious intention of outraging the religious 
feelings of any class of citizens of India, by words, either spoken or written or 
by signs or by visible representations or through electronic means or 
otherwise, 
 Section 294 Regulating obscene and inflammatory content published or 
transmitted via electronic means, with increased penalties for repeat offences 
or offences committed in sensitive religious places. 
 Section 303 primarily related to the offence of theft. Its scope can be extended 
to theft not only to physical movable property, but also t o theft of mobile 
phones, electronic data and computer hardware or software. This means theft 
of such items can also falls within the purview of cybercrime. 
 Section 353 addresses statements and rumours circulated electronically to 
incite public mischief. 
 Section 356 addresses the offence of defamation, including the transmission of 
defamatory content through electronic means thereby falling within the scope 
of cybercrime. 

 
 
72 
 
These sections reflect an integrated approach to addressing cybercrime that includes 
not only digital fraud and un authorised access but also cyber -enabled offenc es 
affecting public order, national integration and decency. This renewed statutory 
regime complements the Information Technology Act, 2000, by focusing on the 
organized and continuing nature of cyber offences, thus enhancing India’s capacity to 
prosecute complex cybercrime networks effectively. 
Gaps in the Legal Framework for Preventing Cybercrime 
4.1.5 Cybercriminals are agile and constantly adapt their techniques and methodology 
leveraging the new technologies for committing cybercrimes and swiftly deploying measures 
to counter law enforcement efforts. To effectively address these challenges, the legal 
framework needs to constantly evolve. There are four major type of infrastructu res used by 
the cybercriminals; Telecommunication, Information Technology, Banking and FinTech  and 
Human. Gaps in regulation and enforcement of the existing regulations lead to their 
exploitation.  
a) Virtual Private Networks (VPNs): VPNs are frequently exploited by cybercriminals to 
evade detection. While CERT -In has issued a circular mandating data preservation, 
there are many VPNs service which are non -compliant. In the meeting of the 
Committee, several queries were raised by the Member with respect to the 
vulnerability in the usage of VPNs. 
b) There is a need for robust mechanism for holding the IT Intermediaries for non -
compliance of various legal provisions laid down in IT Act & its rules. Often, there 
have been instances when IT Intermediaries have failed t o comply with the legitimate 
request made by the Law Enforcement Agencies for data request and removal of 
online unlawful content. In light of the above, existing provisions under Section 79 of 
the IT Act, 2000 pertaining to safe harbour clause, need to be  revisited to ensure 
accountability of IT intermediaries whose platforms are being misused.  
c) The Information Technology (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics 
Code) Rules, 2021, under Rule 3(2) provides the Intermediary to prominently publish 
on its website, mobile based application or both the name of the grievance officer and 
its contact details as well as the mechanisms by which a user and victim may make 
complaint against violations of vario us provisions of IT Intermediary Rules, 2021. 
Further, Rule 4  provides for appointment of Chief Compliance Officer and Nodal 
Contact Person for 24x7 coordination with Law Enforcement Agencies and Resident 
Grievance Officer in respect of Significant Social Media Intermediaries (SSMIs). 
Thus, there are no designated nodal officers for raising requests in respect of 
Intermediaries who are not SSMIs, which complicates communication and adversely 
affect data request raised by LEAs. Many IT intermediaries are for eign-based and 
there is no registration mechanism for IT intermediaries within the country. In many 
cases, grievance officers are based abroad and do not respond to emails. Hence, there 
is a need for a registration mechanism for IT Intermediaries operating  within the 
country. 
d) Even where SSMIs have appointed nodal officers the number is grossly inadequate. 
For e.g. Facebook, WhatsApp, Telegram etc. have only one nodal officer for the entire 
country to coordinate with the LEAs. There is a need to appoint regi onal nodal 
officers.  

 
 
73 
 
e) Both the IT Act and the BNSS address extraterritorial offences, providing India with 
legal tools to deal with crimes committed outside its borders. Both laws empower law 
enforcement agencies to take action against cybercrimes and othe r offences, 
regardless of where the crime occurs, as long as the impact or the perpetrator involves 
India. Despite this legal framework, practical challenges emerge due to issues like 
jurisdictional conflicts, lack of international cooperation and the comp lexity of 
enforcement across borders. 
4.1.6  Some of the important offences under the IT Act, 2000 viz., unauthorised access to 
computer system, tampering with computer source documents, hacking with computer 
system, dishonestly receiving stolen computer resources, identity theft, cheating by 
personation using computer resource, violation of privacy (capturing or publishing images 
without consent) etc., are bailable offences and the punishment prescribed are not stringent 
enough to deter the cybercriminals  from committing such crimes. As a result, cybercriminals 
can easily get bail and further continue to commit such offences. Many of these offences are 
in the nature of transnational and organised crimes wherein multiple criminal networks are 
operating together to execute such crimes. The main source for committing cybercrimes is 
data breaches. Further, unauthorised access to computer systems and demand for huge 
ransomware and other cyber -attacks have exponentially increased and have become highly 
sophisticated as a result of new and emerging technologies, requiring rethinking of the penal 
provisions for such crimes. 
Recommendation/Observation 
4.1.7  The Committee appreciates that the Government has put in place strong legal 
frameworks to effectively address and prevent cybercrime. However, the Committee 
observes persistent and evolving challenges driven by technological advances and agile 
cybercriminal tactics. The Committee recommends that while enforcing the CERT -In 
mandates with respect to VPN logging to a id in prevention of cybercrime, a balanced 
approach must be adopted to protect citizens’ right to privacy. The Committee suggests 
that mandatory data retention and reporting obligations for VPNs be accompanied by 
strict safeguards, including limiting data access solely to authorised investigations 
subject to judicial oversight, along with conducting periodic compliance audits to 
maintain transparency. The Committee encourages the use of privacy -preserving 
technologies, to enable lawful access verification w hile minimizing exposure of sensitive 
data. Further, the Committee also recommends conducting public awareness 
programmes on responsible VPN use and cybersecurity hygiene. The Committee notes 
that some IT Intermediaries have failed to comply with the legit imate request made by 
the Law Enforcement Agencies for data request and removal of online unlawful 
content. Hence, the Committee recommends that the existing provisions of Section 79 of 
the IT Act, 2000 pertaining to safe harbour needs to be revisited to e nsure 
accountability of IT intermediaries whose platforms are being misused. 
4.1.8  The Committee observes that the current rules require grievance and nodal 
officers only for Significant Social Media Intermediaries (SSMIs), leaving other IT 
intermediaries without designated contacts, which hampers effective communication 
and timely data access for law enforcement agencies. Further, many intermediaries are 
foreign-based without any mandatory registration or local presence, causing delays and 
non-responsiveness. The Committee, therefore , recommends establishing a mandatory 
national registration system for all IT intermediaries operating in India, regardless of 

 
 
74 
 
size or origin, requiring them to appoint local grievance officers and nodal contact
