persons with cl ear timelines for responses. Further, for SSMIs, (Facebook, WhatsApp, 
Telegram etc), the Committee suggests increasing the number of nodal officers by 
appointing regional representatives to facilitate faster and region specific coordination 
with law enforcement agencies.  
4.1.9  The Committee appreciates that the IT Act and BNSS empower law 
enforcement agencies to take action against cybercrimes and other offences, regardless 
of where the crime occurs, as long as the impact or the perpetrator involves India. 
However, the Committee observes that significant practical challenges in cross -border 
enforcement due to jurisdictional conflicts and limited international cooperation, which 
hinder effective action against cybercriminals operating globally. The Com mittee 
recommends exploring the possibility for the establishment of a dedicated International 
Cybercrime Liaison Unit with specialized legal and technical experts to resolve the 
problem. 
4.1.10  The Committee notes that many provisions in the IT Act, 2000  are bailable and 
attract low penalties. The accused can get bail easily and the punishments or fines for 
these crimes are relatively low. The Committee, therefore, recommends reviewing and 
amending the IT Act to impose harsher penalties on offenders. 
4.1.11  It has been brought to the notice of the Committee that there is no provision in 
the IT Act, 2000 that holds IT intermediaries liable for compensating victims for 
financial losses, psychological harm, or reputational damage caused due to their 
inaction even after being alerted by G overnment agencies about unlawful or malicious 
content. The Committee, therefore, recommends amending the IT Act, 2000 to 
introduce provisions in the IT Act, making IT intermediaries to compensate victims for 
such acts. 
4.1.12  The Committee notes that Section 78 of the IT Act, 2000 mandates that any 
offence under the Act be investigated by a police officer not below the rank of Inspector. 
The Committee recommen ds that for more effective hand ling of cybercrime cases, 
investigations should be assigned only to police officers senior to the rank of Inspector. 
The Committee also recommends that those officers must be well-versed in all aspects of 
cybercrime, including digital forensics, cyber laws, investigative techniques, cyber 
hygiene and incident response as this will help to resolve cybercrimes efficiently and 
quickly. 
4.1.13   In its meeting the Committee noted that, in view of the rapidly evolving nature 
of cyber threats and the increasing complexity of technology -enabled crimes, it is 
essential that India’s cybercrime legal framework undergoes regular updates to remain 
effective and future -ready. The Committee recommends that the Ministry of Home 
Affairs, in collaboration with the Ministry of Electronics & Information Tech nology, 
Ministry of Law & Justice and relevant regulators, institutionalize a structured, 
multidisciplinary review mechanism to assess cybercrime laws periodically. Adopting 
such a systematic and periodic legislative review will foster a proactive, resilie nt and 
future-ready legislative ecosystem allowing India to remain adaptive to emerging 
threats and to safeguard national security, digital innovation, and citizen rights in the 
face of a fast changing cyber world. 

 
 
75 
 
4.1.14   The Committee notes that ambigui ty in the suspension of suspicious 
transactions has caused inconsistent responses from financial institutions, weakening 
efforts against cybercrime and money laundering. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that financial institutions may add provisions to  incorporate immediate 
suspension of suspicious transactions upon alerts from monitoring systems or law 
enforcement agencies. The Committee specifies that those provisions should clarify that 
such preventive actions comply with all regulatory obligations a nd include provisions 
for disciplinary measures against officials negligent or complicit in facilitating illicit 
activities.  
4.1.15   The Committee recommends that a certified foundational course in 
cybersecurity be made a prerequisite for entry -level police recruitment. The Committee 
observes that such recruited personnel would be in a position to better handle modern 
digital threats from the outset of their policing careers. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Government may consider integratin g this requirement into the 
recruitment and training framework. 
4.1.16   The Committee recommends that the capacity of Law Enforcement Agencies 
(LEAs) be enhanced through regular specialized training for police officers, prosecutors 
and judicial officials in areas such as cyber forensics, investigative techniques, cyber 
hygiene and cyber laws. The Committee is of the view that States and Union Territories 
should be supported in establishing dedicated cybercrime training and research centers 
to ensure a consistent and high standard of skill development among LEA personnel 
nationwide. The Committe e further recommends investing in advanced forensic 
infrastructure, including state -of-the-art labs that cover Memory Forensics, Network 
Forensics, Image Enhancement, Malware Analysis, Cryptocurrency Forensics and 
Mobile Forensics, making these facilities accessible to LEAs across the country.  
4.1.17   The Committee observes that at present, regulation of online gaming in our 
country is overseen by various Ministries and various State Governments resulting in a 
fragmented and at times, inconsistent approac h to enforcement and consumer 
protection. Recognizing these challenges, the Committee recommends the Central 
Government for the establishment of a dedicated ecosystem for Online Gaming in the 
country through a comprehensive consultative process. The Commit tee is of the view 
that this system should not only oversee the sector to address concerns related to real 
money/betting apps but also foster the development of a vibrant 
multimedia/animation/gaming ecosystem within the country. 
4.1.18  The Committee notes  that cybercrime laws in India are currently scattered 
across multiple statutes, creating enforcement and judicial challenges. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that there is an urgent need for comprehensive, unified 
cybercrime legislation. This new legislation should clearly define cyber offences, address 
emerging technologies and provide strong, adaptable penal provisions. The Committee 
further recommends the establishment of a specialized, dedicated Integrated 
Cybercrime Task Force, exclusively focuse d on investigating cybercrime. The 
Committee suggests that this Task Force should operate nationally with the mandate 
and capacity to tackle complex, multifaceted and transnational cyber offences. 
  

 
 
76 
 
4.2  Tools and Technologies for Cyber Protection 
4.2.1  India’s cyber protection landscape leverages advanced tools and institutional 
technologies d eveloped and deployed by organis ations such as C -DAC (Centre for 
Development of Advanced Computing), Department of Telecommunications (DoT), Reserve 
Bank of India (RBI) and the Indian Cyber Crime Coordination Centre (I4C).  
• AppSamvid: It is an application whitelisting software for Microsoft Windows based 
operating systems. Whitelisting allows only pre-approved files to execute on operating 
system. This is in contrast  to the traditional signature based antivirus software 
approach of blacklisting the virus files. Whitelisting has the advantage over 
blacklisting as it does not require frequent virus definition updates. AppSamvid can 
protect operating system against computer malware (such as Viruses and Trojans). 
• M-Kavach 2: C -DAC has developed M -Kavach 2, a comprehensive mobile device 
security solution that addresses emerging threats related to android -based mobile 
devices. The major emphasis is on advising the users agai nst security 
misconfigurations, detection of hidden/ banned apps and scanning the device for 
potential malicious apps installed on the user's mobile device. 
• USB Pratirodh: It controls the usage of removable storage media like pen drives, 
external hard drives, cell phones and other supported USB mass storage devices. Only 
authenticated users can access the removable storage media. 
• CyberCheckLite: C-DAC has developed a portable forensic computer seizure tool. It 
has built-in Previewing, Hashing and Imaging of  computer systems in both offline and 
online conditions. CyberCheckLite comes with a bootable forensic Windows 
Operating System, which write -blocks the suspect system's storage media. In online 
mode, it's capable of imaging RAM as well as hard disk. Withou t a separate write -
blocker, computers can be seized using CyberCheckLite USB drive. 
• DARPAN Network Management System, Browser JSGuard, Disk/Mobile/Network 
Forensics tools, SIMXtractor: Suite of forensic and investigative technology 
extensively deployed in police and enforcement labs across India. 
• Training and Lab Support: CDAC has helped establish over 25 Cyber Forensics Labs 
and trained more than 3,000 officers on digital forensics. C-DAC also provides various 
training programs and lab support to foster high-quality manpower for the R&D and IT 
industry. These programs are delivered through their Advanced Computing Training 
School (ACTS) with 11 locations and a network of 15 Authorised Training Centers 
(ATCs). 
• Telecom Cyber Security Rules (2024 –25): DoT has mandated a multilayered 
compliance and technology regime for telecom service providers. 
• Security Certification: All telecom equipment must undergo mandatory security testing 
as per Indian Telecom Security Assurance Requirements (ITSARs). 
• The Mobile Number Validation (MNV) Platform is a centralized system proposed by 
the Department of Telecommunications to verify the authenticity of mobile numbers 
used in digital transactions. It aims to prevent impersonation, SIM -related fraud and 

 
 
77 
 
unauthorised access by ena bling real -time backend validation of mobile identifiers 
across telecom and non-telecom platforms. 
• India’s national IMEI database, managed through the CEIR  (Central Equipment 
Identity Register) platform, tracks lost, stolen and tampered mobile devices. Under 
telecom cybersecurity rules, second -hand resellers will be legally required to validate 
device IMEI status before sale to prevent fraud and ensure compliance. 
• Security Operations Centres (SOC): Telecom operators required to establish SOCs for 
ongoing monitoring, detection and response. 
• Financial Fraud Risk Indicator (FRI): It is a risk -based metric that classifies a mobile 
number to have been associated with Medium, High, or Very High risk of financial 
fraud. It empowers stakeholders -especially banks, NBFCs and UPI service providers - 
to prioritize enforcement and take additional customer protection measures in case a 
mobile number has high risk. 
• Cybersecurity Operation Centres (C -SOC): Banks must operationalize SOCs for 24×7 
network monitoring and incident detection. 
• Risk-Based Authentication & Secure Payment Gateways: Implementation of strong 
customer authentication, whitelisting internet -facing systems and deep packet 
inspection. 
• Incident Reporting and Crisis Management: Frameworks for prompt cyber ri sk 
reporting, incident disclosure, post -breach analysis and internal redressal are 
compulsory. 
• Standards and Audits: Regular compliance audits and third -party penetration testing 
are mandated by RBI circulars. Major digital payments institutions also emplo y real-
time fraud detection (Fraud Risk Indicators, MuleHunter™). 
• National Cybercrime Reporting Portal (NCRP): All -India digital portal for reporting 
and triaging cyber incidents. 
• Cyber Fraud Mitigation Centre (CFMC): Platform for immediate freezing of sus pected 
fraudulent transactions, operating as a real -time bridge between banks, payment 
intermediaries and law enforcement agencies. 
• Samanvay Platform: A Joint Cybercrime Investigation system for data sharing, crime 
mapping, analytics and multi-agency coordination. 
• Cyber Suspect Registry: A dynamic repository based on NCRP and financial sector 
partners, enabling pattern detection and proactive interdiction. 
• Broadcast and Alert Systems: Rapid dissemination of advisories, technical alerts and 
trend reports to all State/UT agencies. 
4.3  Cyber Hygiene and Institutional Safeguards 
4.3.1  Cyber hygiene and institutional safeguards form the backbone of India’s strategy to 
counter the rising tide of cybercrime. The Government’s multi -layered approach spans policy 

 
 
78 
 
guidelines, awareness campaigns, technical interventions and operational systems deployed 
across ministries and regulatory agencies.  
Awareness Campaigns and Education Initiatives 
4.3.2  The Ministry of Home Affairs, through the Indian Cyber Crime Coordina tion Centre 
(I4C), leads nationwide cyber awareness campaigns such as ‘Cyber Dost’ and ‘STOP -
THINK-ACT’. These initiatives disseminate cyber hygiene advisories via social media, 
television, radio (AIR, Doordarshan) and grassroots outreach, promoting safe digital practices 
across diverse communities. The Ministry of Electronics & IT (MeitY) runs the Cyber 
Swachhta Kendra (Botnet Cleaning and Malware Analysis Centre), providing free tools to the 
public and organisations for malware removal, system clean -up  and safe online practices. It 
supports downloadable solutions such as M -Kavach and AppSamvid and publishes booklets 
and hand books on digital safety for women and vulnerable groups. CERT -In publishes 
regular “Guidelines on Information Security Practices,” checklists and security awareness 
materials for government employees and the public. These guidelines serve as foundational 
tools for government entities and auditors to assess cyber hygiene, ensure compliance and 
safeguard against emerging digital threats. 
School and Higher Education Outreach 
4.3.3  The Department of School Education and Literacy (DoSEL) integrates cyber safety 
modules into school curricula and teacher training programs, supported by initiatives like 
DIKSHA and Cyber Safety Weeks coordinat ed with state and district authorities. The 
Department of Higher Education (DHE) and University Grants Commission (UGC) promote 
ICT-enabled learning and digital hygiene advisories in colleges and universities, in 
collaboration with technical agencies such as C-DAC and NIC. 
Institutional Safeguards 
Indian Cyber Crime Coordination Centre (I4C) 
4.3.4  The Indian Cyber Crime Coordination Centre (I4C) serves as the nodal agency for 
disseminating cyber hygiene best practices, incl uding manuals, ‘Do’s and Don’ts  guides and 
incident response protocols for government departments and agencies. It coordinates user 
education through platforms like the National Cyber Crime Reporting Portal (NCRP) and the 
Financial Fraud Management System, which enable instant alerts and rapid response to 
reported fraud. The Cyber Crime Volunteers Programme engages informed citizens in online 
surveillance and timely reporting of unlawful content. 
CERT-In and Cyber Swachhta Kendra 
4.3.5  CERT-In, as the national incident response organisati on under MeitY, provides 
advisories, daily bulletins, vulnerability analyses and rapid alerts to the stakeholders 
including government, industry and citizens. The Cyber Swachhta Kendra helps organisations 
maintain endpoint and network hygiene via regular toolkits and malware scan utilities. 
CERT-In’s guidelines are regularly updated to incorporate newly emerging threats such as 
ransomware, phishing and social engineering. They are a statutory requirement for all central 
government ministries/departments. 
  

 
 
79 
 
Department of Telecommunications (DoT) 
4.3.6  DoT enforces Telecom Cyber Security Rules (2024) mandating telecom operators to 
establish Security Operations Centres (TSOC), implement network vulnerability testing and 
adopt incident response workfl ows. DoT initiatives like Sanchar Saathi empower citizens to 
validate SIM ownership, block lost/stolen phones and mitigate telecom -related fraud. The 
Digital Intelligence Platform (DIP) coordinates stakeholder action to prevent large -scale 
telecom misuse and fraud. 
Reserve Bank of India (RBI) and Financial Institutions 
4.3.7  RBI’s cyber security framework for banks/NBFCs mandates Security Operation 
Centres, incident reporting, fraud risk analytics (MuleHunter™) and layered authentication 
for critical financial assets and payment systems. RBI and NPCI enforce rapid fraud detection 
and response, share advisories on secure banking practices and collaborate with I4C for 
citizen-facing fraud prevention through helpline 1930. 
Sectoral Safeguards and Regulatory Mandates 
4.3.8  All ministries, critical infrastructure operators and financial regulators are required to 
regularly train their workforce in safe IT practices, run phishing simulation drills and adhere 
to CERT -In, DoT and RBI advisories. The Data Protection  framework (DPDP Act 2023) 
prescribes mandatory breach notifications, data minimi sation and secure system deployment 
across the public and private sectors. 
4.4  Infrastructure Readiness in Education and Public Sector 
4.4.1  Infrastructure readiness is a co rnerstone for India’s cybercrime protection strategy, 
especially across the education and public sectors where digital adoption has surged. The 
government, through various ministries and agencies, is investing in remediation, digital 
resilience and systematic safeguards to address gaps identified in recent audits. 
School Education Infrastructure 
4.4.2  ICT Integration and Digital Infrastructure: The Ministry of Education under the 
Samagra Shiksha Abhiyan has introduced large -scale ICT labs, smart classrooms  and access 
devices (tablets, notebooks) in government schools, supported by internet connectivity and 
digital learning content. Special emphasis is given to teacher training for cyber safety, secure 
learning management systems and parent-student engagement for safe digital practices. 
4.4.3  Cyber Hygiene and Awareness: The Department of School Education & Literacy 
mandates digital safety modules in curricula, regular “Cyber Safety Weeks,” and teacher 
workshops on safe internet usage, delivered in partnership with agencies like NCERT, CBSE 
and MeitY’s Information Security Education & Awareness (ISEA) project. 
4.4.4  Schools are progressively being connected to digital redressal mechanisms for 
reporting cyber incidents, supported by evolving guidelin es on grievance escalation, parental 
engagement and early detection of cyber risks. Advisory frameworks from the Ministry of 
Education and MeitY promote structured protocols, community awareness campaigns and 
capacity-building efforts to strengthen institu tional response across the school education 
system. 

 
 
80 
 
Higher Education Infrastructure 
4.4.5  Campus Networks and Safeguards: Universities and colleges have adopted dedicated 
firewalls, secure Wi -Fi, two -factor authentication and endpoint protection for IT 
infrastructure. Major institutions participate in faculty skill -building workshops with 
organisations like CDAC and NIC. 
4.4.6  Cybersecurity Curriculum and Labs: The Department of Higher Education has 
promoted cyber forensics laboratories, faculty upskilling and sponsored ICT-enabled research 
on cyber -physical systems. Initiatives include UGC advisories on safe online academic 
practices, cyber etiquette and incident response planning in higher education. 
4.4.7  Professional Training: In 2025, MeitY and CER T-In tied up with BITS Pilani for a 
formal cybersecurity certification program aimed at government officials, public sector units  
and higher education professionals, addressing both technology and management aspects of 
cyber preparedness. 
Critical Infrastructure and Government Systems 
4.4.8  Security Audits and Monitoring: CERT -In and the National Critical Information 
Infrastructure Protection Centre (NCIIPC) conducted over 9,700 security audits in FY 2024 –
25 for critical sectors such as power, transport  and BFSI (Banking, Financial Services  and 
Insurance). Sectoral Computer Security Incident Response Teams (CSIRTs) like CSIRT -Fin 
(finance) and CSIRT-Power (energy) facilitate incident response, while sector -specific crisis 
management frameworks guide organisations through recovery and resilience planning. 
4.4.9  Cyber Crisis Management: All public sector bodies must implement the Cyber Crisis 
Management Plan (CCMP), with 205+ workshops held nationwide to ensure the public sector 
is trained in real -time thr eat response and recovery. Institutional templates, guidelines and 
readiness checklists were published for this purpose. 
4.4.10  Infrastructure Moderni sation: State and central ministries have been directed to 
deploy endpoint security, SIEM (Security Infor mation and Event Management), firewalls  
and automated threat monitoring into public IT infrastructure. DoT’s Sanchar Saathi digital 
platform, for example, allows for proactive SIM/IMEI validation and fraud detection in 
telecom infrastructure. 
Indigenous Tools and Capacity Building 
4.4.11  India’s digital infrastructure in education and public institutions benefits significantly 
from indigenous development efforts. The Centre for Development of Advanced Computing 
(CDAC) contributes by provisioning secured platforms and back -end lab integration (e.g., 
forensics labs, university campuses and digital classrooms), in support of national 
cybersecurity objectives. Details of CDAC’s specific cyber tools and forensic technologies 
such as AppSamvid, USB Pratirodh  a nd M -Kavach are already in operational use across 
educational and public sector systems. Additional capacity building and collaboration with 
CDAC are being leveraged through its partnerships with academic institutions and 
government departments to enhance readiness and reduce dependency on foreign security 
platforms. 
  

 
 
81 
 
Challenges and Ongoing Initiatives 
4.4.12  There is a continued shortfall in skilled cyber professionals emphasizing the need for 
more structured investments in infrastructure and training. M any institutions still lag in 
compliance, especially at the state level, due to lack of regular audits or up -to-date 
technological safeguards. The government is expanding indigenous tooling and enforcing 
audit regimes to bridge these gaps. The government’s  future strategy includes quantum 
encryption migration, cloud -based cyber protections, cross -sector digital twins for training 
and expanding secure digital services to rural areas. 
4.5  Global Dimensions of Cybercrime and Preventive Measures 
4.5.1  Cybercrime, in the context of today’s digital world, is transnational, transcending 
traditional geographical boundaries and giving myriad complexities to law enforcement and 
regulatory efforts. With the proliferation of digital connectivity, criminal actor s exploit 
differences in jurisdictions, legal frameworks and enforcement capabilities to engage in illicit 
activities such as hacking, financial fraud, data breaches and misinformation campaigns. The 
multifaceted nature of cybercrime’s international reach can be explored through the following 
various aspects. 
(i) Cross-Border Operations and Regulatory Gaps 
One of the defining features of cybercrime is its cross -border character, which allows 
perpetrators to exploit the patchwork of differing regulatory and enfo rcement 
environments between countries. Cybercriminals commonly coordinate attacks using 
digital resources scattered across several jurisdictions for instance, deploying malware 
from servers situated in one country while targeting victims in another and ro uting 
illicit profits through a third nation. This practice effectively frustrates law 
enforcement, as no single country’s authorities possess comprehensive oversight or 
jurisdiction. The result is an environment in which criminals can evade detection and 
prosecution by capitalizing on legal loopholes and delays inherent in international 
cooperation. 
(ii) Convergence of Criminal Activities 
The convergence of various forms of illicit activities significantly increases the threat 
posed by transnational cybercrime.  Organized crime groups, particularly in regions 
such as Southeast Asia, have begun to blend and integrate diverse criminal enterprises 
such as cyber -enabled fraud, underground banking and sophisticated money 
laundering techniques into unified, highly adap tive operations. Recent reports by the 
United Nations Office on Drugs and Crime (UNODC) show that these criminal 
syndicates routinely leverage underground financial channels and unregulated virtual 
asset service providers to launder the proceeds of cyber fraud. 
(iii) Jurisdictional Challenges and Legal Complexities 
Variations in legal definitions, extradition treaties and enforcement capabilities across 
nations create significant hurdles in tracking and prosecuting cybercriminals. This has 
led to safe havens where offenders can avoid prosecution due to the lack of legal 
harmonisation.  

 
 
82 
 
(iv)  Emergence of New Threats and Criminal Service Models 
Emerging trends such as the adoption of cryptocurrencies for money laundering, the 
proliferation of dark web marketplaces a nd the rise of crime -as-a-service (CaaS) 
offerings have further broadened the international scope of cybercrime. Regions like 
Southeast Asia have become prominent hubs for testing and supporting new forms of 
illicit digital services ranging from ready-to-use phishing kits and ransomware tools to 
deepfake generators and Money Laundering -as-a-Service platforms. These 
developments have enabled cybercrime to scale efficiently and offered transnational 
criminal groups a list of options of services to buy or rent, thus accelerating the spread 
and impact of global cyber threats. 
Cybercrime landscape in Southeast Asia 
4.5.2  The transnational cybercrime landscape in Southeast Asia has evolved into a 
significant and sophisticated threat, with scam compounds emerging as major hubs for 
organized crime that transcends borders. According to the United Nations Office on Drugs 
and Crime (UNODC), cyber -enabled fraud has continued to intensify, resulting in estimated 
financial losses between US $18 billion and $37 billion fro m scams targeting victims in East 
and southeast Asia in 2023, much of which can be attributed to organized syndicates 
operating within Southeast Asia. Key criminal hubs in Cambodia, Lao PDR and Myanmar, 
have become entrenched bases for a range of illicit a ctivities, from illegal online gambling 
and human trafficking to large -scale cyber fraud. These operations have expanded at an 
alarming rate, as evidenced by satellite imagery and extensive financial inflows to scam 
wallets between 2021 and 2024. These com pounds, often fortified with high walls, barbed 
wire, armed guards and strict surveillance, function as nearly autonomous zones, posing 
significant challenges to law enforcement and regulatory authorities.  
4.5.3  The convergence of cyber fraud with underg round banking and cryptocurrency has 
established a resilient infrastructure that enables transnational criminal groups to operate with 
impunity. To address this escalating threat, it is imperative to intensify efforts that target the 
underlying infrastruct ure supporting these networks. Enhanced cross -border cooperation, 
strengthened regulatory frameworks and bolstered enforcement mechanisms are essential to 
mitigating the impact of these highly organized criminal operations. 
4.5.4  Organised gangs recruit u nemployed youth worldwide, including from India, by 
offering fake overseas jobs. Once they land in places like Cambodia, Vietnam, etc., they are 
asked to work in scam centers. Recruits pay high fees for visas and get trapped and are forced 
to scam people w hile facing abuse and passport confiscation. Scammers use WhatsApp to 
contact and “mule accounts” to hide stolen money, laundering it via crypto currency, gold 
and cash withdrawals. The Indian Government has rescued over 2,000 victims from these 
scam operations in Cambodia, Laos, Myanmar and Thailand. 
4.5.5  Scam compounds in Southeast Asia use various methods to defraud people in India. 
These include fake investment and trading schemes promising high returns, task -based scams 
which involve activities such as “like and subscribe”, part -time jobs, fake reviews etc., that 
waste victims’ time or money and digital arrest scams where fraudsters pretend to be law 
enforcement to extort money through threats. They also run dating or romance scams to build 
emotional connection to gain victims’ trust and steal money. The  stolen funds are laundered 
via complicated networks involving mule accounts, ATM withdrawals, gold purchases, 
cryptocurrencies and prepaid wallets. 

 
 
83 
 
International Agencies involved in Prevention of Cybercrime 
4.5.6  Cybercrime is a transnational threat tha t requires a coordinated global response. Key 
international agencies like INTERPOL, Europol and the United Nations Office on Drugs and 
Crime (UNODC) play vital roles by enhancing collaboration, intelligence sharing and 
capacity building. They work together  to strengthen legal frameworks, assist member states 
in combating cyber threats and safeguard the global digital environment. Their combined 
efforts highlight the importance of a unified, comprehensive approach to effectively address 
the evolving challenges of cybercrime. 
International Criminal Police Organisation (INTERPOL) 
4.5.7  INTERPOL, the inter -governmental organi sation with 196 member countries is 
facilitating global police cooperation by enabling data sharing on crimes and criminals, as 
well as pr oviding technical and operational support. Each member country hosts an 
INTERPOL National Central Bureau (NCB), run by national police officials and serving as 
the contact point for the General Secretariat and other NCBs. The INTERPOL Cybercrime 
Directorate focuses on reducing the global impact of cybercrime and enhancing community 
safety. The following are some of the key initiatives of INTERPOL to combat evolving 
cybercrime:- 
Operation Serengeti (Sep -Oct 2023), a joint effort with AFRIPOL across 19 Africa n 
countries, led to over 1,000 arrests, dismantling of more than 134,000 malicious 
infrastructures  and identified 35,000 victims tied to nearly $193 million in financial 
losses. 
Operation Synergia (Sep-Nov 2023) coordinated 60 law enforcement agencies from over 
50 countries and private partners to target phishing, malware  and ransomware attacks, 
resulting in over 30 arrests, identification of 70 suspects  and takedown of around 70% 
of more than 1,300 malicious servers. 
Operation Storm Makers II (Oct 2023)  across 27 countries in Asia and beyond targeted 
human trafficking and migrant smuggling, arresting 281 individuals and rescuing 149 
victims destined for cyber scam centers. 
The Global Rapid Intervention of Payments (I -GRIP) initiative, launched with the 
Financial Action Task Force in 2022, helps police worldwide track, freeze and seize 
criminal assets. Since its inception, over $200 million linked to cyber -enabled fraud has 
been intercepted. 
United Nations Office on Drugs and Crime (UNODC) 
4.5.8  The mission of UNODC is to contribute to global peace and security, human rights 
and development by making the world safer from drugs, crime, corruption and terrorism, 
through working for and with Member States to promote justice and the rule of law and build 
resilient societies. The mandate of UNODC encompasses thematic areas related to preventing 
and countering transnational organized crime, corruption, terrorism and the world drug 
problem as well as enhancing and strengthening criminal justice systems. Cybercrim e and 
Anti Money Laundering Section (CMLS) under Organized Crime and Illicit Trafficking 
Branch (OCB) in UNODC focus on the issue of cybercrime. The following are some of the 
efforts of UNODC to fight cybercrime:  

 
 
84 
 
 (i) 39 victims and 35 alleged suspects of online child sexual exploitation identified as a 
direct result of UNODC’s intervention.  
 (ii) 02 guides developed on digital evidence and cryptocurrencies for law enforcement 
officers and criminal justice authorities.  
 (iii)  Reports published on cybercrime:-  
(a) Casinos, cyber fraud and trafficking in persons for forced criminality in 
Southeast Asia – September 2023.  
(b) Casinos, Money Laundering, Underground Banking & Transnational 
Organized Crime in SE Asia – January 2024.  
(c)Transnational Organized C rime in the Pacific: Expansion, Challenges and 
Impact – October 2024.  
Draft United Nations Convention against Cybercrime 
4.5.9  On 9 th August 2024, the UN General Assembly's Ad Hoc Committee finalized the 
draft United Nations Conven tion against Cybercrime. This land mark treaty seeks to 
strengthen international cooperation by standardizing definitions, procedural powers and 
data-sharing mechanisms to support cross-border investigations and capacity building against 
cybercrime. It aims to promote a  unified global response to cyber threats while protecting 
fundamental rights and freedoms. The convention was adopted by the UN General Assembly 
and will be opened for signature in Hanoi in 2025. India has actively contributed to shaping 
this convention, reflecting its commitment to influencing international cyber norms that 
respect national sovereignty and align with domestic legal priorities.  
European Cybercrime Centre (EC3), Europol 
4.5.10  The European Cybercrime Centre (EC3), established in January 2 013 by Europol, 
serves to strengthen the law enforcement response to cybercrime within the European Union. 
Its primary mission is to protect European citizens, businesses and governments from online 
crime by coordinating cross-border investigations and providing technical expertise. EC3 also 
hosts the Joint Cybercrime Action Taskforce (J -CAT) which was launched in September 
2014. Its mission is to drive intelligence -led, coordinated action against key cybercrime 
threats through cross -border investigations a nd operations by its partners. The taskforce was 
involved in 33 high-profile operations in 2022. 
The following are some of the efforts of EC3 to fight cybercrime: 
(a) The European Money Mule Action (EMMA) is an annual international law 
enforcement initiative coordinated by Europol, aimed at identifying and 
apprehending individuals involved in money mule schemes, which facilitate 
money laundering activities. In its ninth  edition, EMMA 9, LEAs from 26 
countries, in collaboration with Europol, Eurojust, INTERPOL and several private 
industry partners, have identified 474 recruiters/herders, 10,759 money mules, 
4,659 investigations initiated, 1,013 arrests, €32 million (₹284. 19 Cr) prevented 
losses.  

 
 
85 
 
(b) Coordination of a joint operation, including private -sector partners to target a 
botnet, ‘Ramnit’ that had infected millions of computers around the world. 
(c) Coordination with Eurojust in an operation targeting large -scale malware attacks 
that originated in Ukraine, an operation that led to tens of arrests. 
National Cyber Crime Unit (NCCU) 
4.5.11  The National Cyber Crime Unit (NCCU) is part of the National Crime Agency 
(NCA) and leads the United Kingdom’s fight against cybercrime. NCCU’s role is to protect 
the UK public from serious and organised crime. NCCU leads, coordinates and supports the 
national law enforcement response to Cybercrime. NCCU supports organisations that fall 
victim to Cybercrime and take action against those res ponsible. The Triage, Incident 
Coordination & Tasking (TICAT) team of NCCU is available 24x7 and can help businesses 
identify, remove and remediate the initial access/compromise. Where possible, NCCU 
pursues criminal justice outcomes for cybercriminals, bu t its work also includes the use of 
alternative disruptions – sanctions, takedowns and the infiltration of criminal enterprises. 
4.6  Practices adopted by countries to prevent Cybercrime 
Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3) – USA 
4.6.1  The Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3) was set up by the FBI in May 2000 under 
its Cyber Division to receive complaints on a wide range of internet -enabled crimes, 
including intellectual property violations, hacking, economic espionage, online extortion, 
money laundering and identity theft. Its mission is to offer the public and its partners with a 
reliable way to report suspected cybercrimes and to foster strong partnerships with law 
enforcement and industry to help those who report. Information received is analysed and 
disseminated for investigative and intelligence purposes for law enforcement and public 
awareness. The individual IC3 complaints are combined with other data, which allows the 
FBI to connect complaints, investigate reported crimes, track trends and threats  and in some 
cases, even freeze stolen funds. 
4.6.2  In the year 2023, the IC3 received 880,418 complaints, with reported financial losses 
exceeding $12.5 billion. Over the past five years, the IC3 has averaged around 758,000 
complaints each year, These co mplaints address a wide array of Internet scams affecting 
individuals across the globe. The IC3’s Recovery Asset Team (RAT) was established in 
February 2018 to streamline communication with financial institutions and assist FBI field 
offices with the freez ing of funds for those who made transfers to domestic accounts under 
fraudulent pretences. The RAT functions as a liaison between law enforcement and financial 
institutions supporting statistical and investigative analysis. Since its establishment (till 
2023), the RAT has achieved a 71% success rate, addressing 3,008 incidents, freezing 
$538.39 million (₹4544.9 Cr) of $758.05 million (₹6398.8 Cr) in reported losses  and 
significantly reducing the impact of financial fraud. 
4.6.3  The Federal Bureau of Inves tigation (FBI), in its strategic endeavor to combat 
ransomware, has adopted a decentralized approach by empowering its regional field offices 
to target specific cybercriminal groups. This initiative demonstrates an advanced operational 
framework that leverages specialized expertise to dismantle organized cybercrime effectively. 
For instance, the Cleveland Field Office has disrupted the “Radar/Dispossessor” ransomware 
group, while the Tampa Field Office successfully neutralized the Hive group and provided 

 
 
86 
 
decryption keys to victims, while Newark Office made significant progress against the 
LockBit ransomware variant. The Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI), in its strategic 
endeavor to combat ransomware, has adopted a decentralized approach by empowering it s 
regional field offices to target specific cybercriminal groups. This initiative demonstrates an 
advanced operational framework that leverages specialized expertise to dismantle organized 
cybercrime effectively. 
Anti-Scam Command (ASCom) – Singapore 
4.6.4  The Anti -Scam Command (ASCom) was operationalized on 22 March 2022 to 
achieve greater synergy between various scam -fighting units within the Singapore Police 
Force (SPF), by integrating scam investigation, incident response, intervention, enforcement  
and sense-making capabilities under a single umbrella. The command comprises the Anti -
Scam Centre, three Anti -Scam Investigation Branches and oversees the Scam Strike Teams 
situated within each of the seven Police Land Divisions. The following are some of the 
efforts of ASCom to Fight Scams and Cybercrimes:  
i. Co-location of staff from 06 banks and Government Technology Agency (GovTech) 
within the ASCom. Carousell and Shopee (e-commerce) staff also collocated.  
ii. Since Jan 2023 to Jun 2024, the ASCom froze more tha n 29,900 bank accounts based 
on reports referred to the Anti -Scam Centre (ASC) and recovered more than $154 
million (₹967.21 Cr).  
iii. In the first half of 2024, the ASCom, together with the Scam Strike Teams in the seven 
Police Land Divisions, conducted 13 island -wide anti -scam enforcement operations, 
leading to the investigation of more than 4,000 money mules and scammers. Police 
have a lso stepped up our enforcement with more than 300 money mules charged in 
Court.  
iv. ASCom also focused on upstream interventions to identify and alert victims by 
leveraging Robotic Process Automation (RPA) technology to identify job, investment 
and other scam victims and alerting them. Through the A.S.T.R.O. Project (Automation 
of Scam -fighting Tactics & Reaching Out), the ASCom works with banks in 
automating information-sharing, information-processing and mass distribution of SMS 
alerts to scam victims to pre vent further financial losses. In the first half of 2024, more 
than 46,400 SMSs were sent to alert more than 33,600 victims averting over $204 
million (₹1281.24 Cr) of potential losses.  
v. In the first half of 2024, the close collaboration between SPF and ov erseas law 
enforcement agencies resulted in the successful takedown of 09 transnational scam 
syndicates comprising three fake friend call syndicates, four suspected money 
laundering cells, one phishing scam syndicate and one investment scam syndicate. 
More than 100 persons based overseas who were responsible for more than 320 
transnational scam cases, were arrested.  
vi. ScamShield Suite: It is a joint effort by the Ministry of Home Affairs, the Singapore 
Police Force, Open Government Products and the National Crime Prevention Council to 
inform citizens on scams. The ScamShield mobile app leverages advanced artificial 
intelligence technology to effectively filter scam messages and block calls from 
numbers linked to fraudulent activities. Complementing this, a 24 x7 helpline at 1799 
has been established to provide immediate assistance and verification for suspicious 
communications. 

 
 
87 
 
Joint Policing Cybercrime Coordination Centre (JPC3) - Australia 
4.6.5  In March 2022, the Australian Federal Police (AFP) led Joint Po licing Cybercrime 
Coordination Centre (JPC3) was launched to enable state and territory law enforcement, 
Commonwealth agencies and the private sector to coordinate Australian policing responses to 
ensure maximum impact on high -harm, high -volume cybercrime,  by utilising the powers, 
experience, investigative and intelligence capabilities of all Australian policing jurisdictions. 
4.6.6  The role of JPC3 is to enhance intelligence -sharing and target development across 
police and industry; coordinate joint task forces to counter priority cybercrime threats; 
coordinate capability improvements through cross -skilling, joint training and collaborative 
tool development; and communicate prevention, awareness raising and media activities. The 
following are some of the efforts of JPC3 to fight cybercrime:  
i. JPC3 uses AFP’s cross-jurisdictional partnerships with key international stakeholders’ 
presence in 33 countries. Through various operations JPC3 was able to disrupt the 
cybercrime networks and centres in and outside Aus tralia, such as in October 2024 in 
Philippines, where more than 250 suspects were arrested from various scam centres. 
ii. In August 2024, the JPC3 commenced Operation FIRESTORM, as a proactive 
initiative and a holistic approach to combating Cyber Enabled Fraud (CEF), bolstering 
the ongoing efforts of Australian law enforcement, the National Anti -Scam Centre 
(NASC) and key industry stakeholders. These JPC3 operations also focused on 
disruption of mule account networks used in cybercrime  
Internet Content Provider (ICP) Number – China 
4.6.7  The Ministry of Industry and Information Technology (MIIT) of the People’s 
Republic of China issues three different types of ICP numbers, which are managed at the 
provincial level:  
i. ICP license  for commercial websites, which cover any website offering goods or 
services to customers.  
ii. ICP filing for non -commercial websites which are purely informational and are not 
involved in direct sales.  
4.6.8  Mobile APP ICP filing for all mobile apps. Starting from September 1, 2023 , all new 
apps must undergo the ICP Filing process before they can be published on app stores in 
China, while the existing apps should complete the filing before March 31, 2024. This rule 
applies to Apple App stores, as well as all android app stores and m ini-program app (quick 
app) stores in China.  
4.6.9  The ICP filing is compulsory for all websites based in mainland China, including 
those offering advertising services such as banner ads and sponsored content. This regulation 
is integral to ensuring comp liance with national standards governing digital platforms.  The 
process for obtaining an ICP filing requires submission through platforms such as the Alibaba 
Cloud/AWS/ Huawei ICP Filing System. Applicants must provide detailed information, 
including the company name, address and phone number, along with a Chinese verification 
document. Additionally, the application must include the legal representative's name, phone 
number, email address and proof of identity. The person responsible for the website must also 

 
 
88 
 
provide their name, phone number, email address and proof of identity. Other required details 
include the website's emergency contact phone number, a photograph of the person 
responsible for the website, the IP address, ISP, server location and a list of domains where 
the website will be accessible. This detailed and structured process ensures adherence to 
regulatory requirements and enhances oversight over websites/web apps/mobile apps 
operating in mainland China. 
National Press and Publication Administration (NPPA) – China  
4.6.10  The National Press and Publication Administration (NPPA) is the designated 
authority in the People's Republic of China responsible for regulating the gaming industry 
and approving or rej ecting games for release within the country. The approval process 
enforced by NPPA includes several mandatory requirements: games must comply with 
Chinese laws and regulations, secure copyright registration from the National Copyright 
Administration (NCAC)  and obtain an Internet Content Provider (ICP) certificate. 
Additionally, an anti -addiction system must be embedded to regulate user engagement. 
Developers are required to implement mechanisms for user registration with real names and 
ID verification. Furt hermore, the NPPA mandates the monitoring of gaming time and in -
game purchases to ensure compliance with national policies on responsible gaming.  
4.6.11  NPPA imposes strict restrictions on game content to uphold societal and cultural 
values. Games are prohibited from depicting obscenity, pornography, nudity, homosexuality, 
polygamy, adultery, or any form of sexual content. Depictions of violence, terror and cruelty 
are strictly barred, as are scenes, characters, or images considered excessively frighteni ng. 
Content that insults or slanders others, endangers social morality, or undermines national 
cultural traditions is not permitted. Additionally, the promotion of law violations, drug use, 
drug trafficking, or any form of criminal behaviour is strictly for bidden. In 2024, the NPPA 
has approved an average of 107 games per month, reflecting its robust operational framework 
and commitment to regulating the gaming industry in alignment with national interests and 
ethical standards. 
European Cybersecurity Competence Centre (ECCC) 
4.6.12  The European Cybersecurity Competence Centre (ECCC) plays a critical role in 
enhancing the cybersecurity resilience of the European Union by strategically guiding 
investments in research, innovation and the industrial sector. The ECCC aligns with the goals 
set out in the EU Cybersecurity Strategy for the Digital Decade, focusing on the coordination 
and implementation of cybersecurity measures through both the Digital Europe Programme 
(DEP) and Horizon Europe (HE). A key aspect of the ECCC’s mandate is its collaboration 
with the Network of National Coordination Centres (NCCs) to develop and deploy Security 
Operations Centres (SOCs) across sectors, as well as strengthen the capabilities of national 
CSIRTs (27 NCCs have been assigned) . These efforts contribute to improving the Union’s 
ability to prevent, detect and respond to cyber incidents while fostering synergies between 
civilian and defence cybersecurity initiatives. In doing so, the ECCC supports the 
development of a trusted and secure digital environment within the Union, taking into 
account the requirements of key legislative frameworks such as the NIS2 Directive and the 
Cyber Resilience Act. Furthermore, the ECCC is responsible for guiding the implementation 
of coordinated vuln erability disclosures and facilitating cross -border information sharing, 
which ensures a harmonized response to cybersecurity threats across Member States.  

 
 
89 
 
4.6.13  ECCC aims to strengthen the Union's cybersecurity capabilities by investing in the 
education, certification and upskilling of cybersecurity professionals. Through initiatives 
such as the European Cybersecurity Skills Framework (ECSF), the ECCC promotes 
standardised curricula, cross -border education opportunities and the certification of 
competencies, which collectively ensure that the EU has a skilled workforce capable of 
meeting emerging cybersecurity challenges. Additionally, Small and medium -sized 
enterprises (SMEs) often face increased risks due to limited resources and expertise. To 
address this, the ECCC provides targeted funding opportunities through National 
Coordination Centres (NCCs) and supports initiatives such as cybersecurity maturity 
assessments, development of risk modelling tools and vulnerability management solutions 
aimed specifically at SMEs. The ECCC also fosters collaboration among academia, industry 
and public authorities to address common cybersecurity goals, with an emphasis on 
strengthening coordination through Information Sharing and Analysis Centres (ISACs). 
These collective efforts not only bolster the cybersecurity maturity of the Union but also 
serve as key measures to prevent cybercriminal activities and safeguard critical infrastructure, 
ensuring a secure and trustworthy digital ecosystem for all stakeholders. 
Recommendation/Observation 
4.6.14  The Committee observes that it is imperative to strengthen bilateral and 
multilateral agreements with international law enforcement agencies. To combat 
cybercrime, the Committee recommends effective Mutual Legal Assistance Treaties and 
Memoranda of Understanding are necessary and efforts may be taken to achieve this 
goal. The Committee notes that difference in law enforcement expertise hinder effective 
investigations and therefore recommends regular joint training sessio ns focused on 
digital forensics, blockchain analysis and virtual asset tracing, involving both domestic 
and international partners.  
4.6.15  The Committee recommends the development of secure, interoperable platforms 
for real -time cyber threat intelligence  sharing between countries and releva nt 
organisations. The Committee further recommends establishing a multilingual, user -
friendly online platform through which citizens can easily report cybercrimes, 
improving response times and data collection. Recognizi ng the vital role of public -
private collaboration, the Committee further recommends closer partnerships with 
global technology, financial and telecommunications sectors, as this would facilitate 
better monitoring, detection and coordinated takedown of malicious infrastructure.  
4.7  Innovative measures adopted by States to deal Cybercrime 
4.7.1  Police and public order are subjects in the State List. Many States and Union 
Territories (UTs) have strengthened their ability to address cyber threats by implementing 
innovative initiatives such as specialized cyber units, advanced police training programs and 
robust public awareness campaigns. All these efforts not only have improved the preventive 
and investigative capabilities but also set benchmarks for  others, contributing to a 
coordinated and stronger national response to cybercrime. The following segment will 
highlight certain exemplary measures adopted by different States and UTs. 
Telangana State Cyber Security Bureau (TGCSB) 
4.7.2  The Telangana Sta te Cyber Security Bureau (TGCSB), was established in 2023 in 
Hyderabad, is a specialized bureau set up by the Government of Telangana to tackle 

 
 
90 
 
cybercrime and strengthen cybersecurity throughout Telangana. With state -wide jurisdiction 
and support from all departments, TGCSB aims to protect citizens, institutions and 
infrastructure from cyber threats while fostering a secure digital environment. TGCSB’s core 
responsibilities include registering, investigating and prosecuting cybercrimes, setting up 
mechanisms to detect and counter threats and enhancing cyber safety awareness in 
partnership with various stakeholders. The Director of TGCSB also serves as the Chief 
Information Security Officer (CISO) for the Police Department and other organisations as 
directed by the Government. It serves also serves as State Cyber Crime Coordination Centre 
and nodal agency to coordinate with I4C, national agencies, IT industry and law enforcement 
agencies of other States, Intelligence Department etc. It also commissions studies  and 
research to understand various cybercrimes, provides training, conducts special operations 
against cyber offenders and submits recommendations for necessary policy changes.  
4.7.3  TGCSB is led by its Director and divided into four main branches: the Cybercrime 
Branch, Cyber Security Branch, Administration and Logistics Branch and Auxiliary Units. 
The Cybercrime Branch is headed by Superintendent of Police (SP) and has dedicated cyber 
police stations and forensic units, while the Cyber Security Branch is also headed by SP and 
focuses on threat intelligence, training, audits and research. The Auxiliary Units consist of 
cybercrime police stations in six Commissionerates and 21 district -level coordination cells. 
With a sanctioned strength of 501, TGCSB com prises officers, technical experts, legal 
advisors and other professionals. The Bureau has established 7 new cybercrime police 
stations, booked 472 cases since April 2024 and arrested over 160 cyber offenders. 
Financially, TGCSB safeguarded over ₹283 crore s from fraud, including ₹179.38 crores in 
the current year and has conducted more than 30,511 public awareness programs. Arrested 27 
offenders in the first inter -state operation conducted in Rajasthan, blocked thousands of 
fraudulent SIMs, IMEIs and URLs/a pps and detected 817 accused connected to 134,218 
cases nationwide, with 16,925 cases in Telangana alone.  
Cyber Command and Control Centre - Maharashtra 
4.7.4 The Maharashtra Cyber Security Project is a landmark initiative aimed at elevating 
cybersecurity and digital policing across Maharashtra, with its state -of-the-art Cyber 
Headquarters located in Mahape, Navi Mumbai. The centre is equipped with a platform that 
integrates over 50 global forensic technologies, 17 threat intelligence tools and 13 
cybersecurity tools leveraging AI and blockchain technologies. It also consists of a highly -
skilled team of over 150 experts in cyber forensics, investigation and technology. It also 
houses the Nodal Cyber Police Station and 50 district cyber police stations will use the 
Centre’s resources to investigate citizen and enterprise complaints. The Centre serves as an 
integrated grievance redressal hub accessible via helpline, web portal and mobile app, 
strengthening the State’s ability to tackle a wide range of cyber complaints. The project 
operates across six key domains:- 
(i) The Nodal Cyber Police Station supports operational needs of all 50 district labs in 
resolving complex cyber cases and upholds digital law and order; 
(ii) The Command  & Control Centre manages helpline 1930 and the newly developed 
helpline 14407, providing a unified response point for cyber fraud, online 
harassment and similar threats; 

 
 
91 
 
(iii) Technology Assisted Investigation (TAI) utilises the latest forensic tools and will 
assist in investigations of digital devices like PCs, mobiles  and hard drives, as well 
as conduct malware analysis, thus enabling faster resolution of cybercrime cases; 
(iv) Computer Emergency Response Team - Maharashtra (CERT-MH) safeguards the 
State’s critical infrastructure and Government resources, shielding it from cyber -
attacks; 
(v) Centre of Excellence (COE) focuses on training 5,000 police officers over five years 
and raising public awareness about cyber safety and emerging threats; 
(vi) Security Operations Centre (SOC) protects Maharashtra’s cyber infrastructure and 
its 50 district labs from unauthorised access and digital breaches. 
Cyberdome – Kerala 
4.7.5  Cyberdome, an initiative by Kerala Police, serves as an advanced technological 
research and development centre aimed at becoming a centre of excellence in cybersecurity 
and technology augmentation for effective policing. Established as a high -tech public-private 
partnership, Cyberdome integ rates resources from Government agencies, academia, private 
industry, ethical hackers and civil society to build a resilient cyber threat mitigation 
ecosystem. Its mission focuses on protecting citizens from evolving cyber threats through 
collaboration and innovation. 
4.7.6  The operational framework of Cyberdome includes key divisions such as Cyber 
Intelligence, Cyber Security, Incident Response, Cyber Forensics, Research and 
Development and Training & Awareness. Its network spans 20 companies, 6 academic 
institutions, 4 NGOs, 5 government agencies, 3 international bodies and all RBI -regulated 
banks. Over 2,500 experts and a Mobile Technicians Group of 250 statewide members 
contribute their technical skills and knowledge. 
4.7.7  Cyberdome has implemented va rious strategic initiatives to strengthen Kerala’s 
cybersecurity landscape and modernize law enforcement through advanced technology. 
These include Vulnerability Assessment and Penetration Testing (VAPT) on critical 
Government websites, specialized cybercr ime investigation training in partnership with 
regulatory bodies, such as RBI and “Kid Glove” workshops to raise cyber awareness among 
students, educators and parents. It also operates a financial fraud monitoring cell addressing 
online banking frauds and employs AI and machine learning in smart policing tools like 
facial recognition and traffic violation detection. Additional projects include the 
establishment of a Cyber -Security Operations Centre (C -SOC), a Robotics and Automation 
Research unit for law en forcement technology needs and extensive capacity -building 
programs to enhance Government employees’ cybersecurity skills. Cyberdome has also 
established a presence in Metaverse to actively counter threats in virtual spaces, showcasing 
its forward-looking approach. 
4.7.8  To further public safety and policing efficiency, Cyberdome has developed multiple 
applications. The BSafe app to protect against fraudulent calls, OTP and UPI scams; the e -
Curfew Pass System to facilitate emergency travel during lockdowns ; the Shops App to 
enable home delivery of essentials; POLKART for digitizing police personnel shopping at 
Central Police Canteens; and the BSafe Quarantine Tracker for real -time monitoring of 

 
 
92 
 
COVID-19 quarantined individuals. Further, Grapnel, a Darkweb m onitoring tool, supports 
tracking and reporting illicit online activities. 
Operation P-Hunt – Kerala 
4.7.9  Operation P -Hunt is an initiative led by the Counter Child Sexual Exploitation 
(CCSE) Unit of Kerala Police aimed at combating online child sexual a buse and the 
distribution of Child Sexual Exploitation and Abuse Material (CSEAM). The operation 
targets individuals involved in disseminating CSEAM, utilising data from sources such as 
Cyber Tipline Reports, the Internet Crimes against Children Child Onli ne Protection System 
(ICACCOPS), social media and anonymous complaints. The CCSE processes this 
information to identify suspects’ critical details including IP addresses, emails, phone 
numbers and device types, which are then organized for focused action. 
4.7.10  The operation is conducted on a date designated by the Director General of Police 
and the CCSE shares suspect data with all District Police Chiefs. Specialized teams, 
including cyber experts, locate suspects prior to coordinated raids conducted sim ultaneously 
across all districts. Devices found containing CSEAM content are seized, arrests are made 
and cases registered under the IT Act and POCSO Act. The CCSE Centre centrally 
coordinates these operations to maintain uniformity. Since inception, 18 st atewide P -Hunt 
operations have been conducted, resulting in 6,146 searches, 1,758 cases filed, 351 
individuals arrested and seizure of 3,305 devices. Additionally, 1,994 reports have been 
submitted to courts under relevant legal provisions. This ongoing, s ystematic effort 
demonstrates Kerala Police’s strong commitment to addressing and reducing online child 
sexual exploitation in the State. 
Cyber Helpline 1930 Call Centre – Haryana 
4.7.11  The Cyber Helpline 1930 Call Centre in Haryana operates 24x7 and is fully 
integrated with the 112 emergency response service, ensuring continuous, immediate support 
to cybercrime victims and improving public accessibility and trust. It is staffed by 70 trained 
personnel skilled in victim support and cybercrime management, enabling fast and effective 
complaint resolution.  
The helpline has established a formal partnership with banks/financial institutions, with 15 
nodal officers from ten major banks deployed at the helpline centre to provide real -time 
coordination for freezing fraudulent transactions. This collaboration has played a crucial role 
in reducing financial losses for cybercrime victims and strengthening the overall response 
mechanism. Due to these improvements, the recovery rate of defrauded funds has increased 
significantly from 6% in September 2023 to 27% in September 2024, reflecting the enhanced 
efficiency and effectiveness of Haryana’s cybercrime response system. 
4.7.12  Operation on Suspect locations projected on PRATIBIMB 
(i) Operation Anti-Virus – Rajasthan 
Operation Anti -Virus, launched by Rajasthan Police in the Mewat region, 
represents a proactive shift in combating cybercrime through stringent legal 
actions, including filing FIRs under session trial sections and arresting offenders. 
A comprehensive Criminal Di rectory is maintained to track arrested and wanted 
cybercriminals, classified into categories like fake SIM providers, fake bank 

 
 
93 
 
account creators, mule account operators and perpetrators of scams such as honey-
trapping and phishing. 
The operation is suppor ted by a dedicated "War Room" at the range office with a 
cyber-technical team that sets daily targets for the Range Special Team (RST) in 
collaboration with District Special Team (DST) and District Deeg Police to 
conduct coordinated enforcement. Daily stra tegic meetings with the 
Superintendent of Police, Deeg to oversee the planning and monitor crime trends 
via the Pratibimb portal. Villages are categorized geographically into A, B, C 
based on criminal activity, allowing targeted cordon - and -search operati ons by 
RST, DST and district police to dismantle cybercriminal networks 
comprehensively. 
Over the past nine months in 2024, the Rajasthan police have registered 314 suo-
moto cases and arrested 1,138 cybercriminals, reducing the district’s share of 
national cybercrime suspects from 20% to 4%. Legal and administrative measures 
include seizing criminal assets (vehicles and properties) and opposing bail 
applications using strong evidence and arguments. Preventive measures like strict 
legal actions are taken aga inst individuals attacking police officers and lawful 
measures to demolish illegal constructions owned by cybercriminals to undermine 
their operational bases.  
(ii) Jharkhand  Police 
The Jharkhand Police have shown notable diligence and innovation in tackling 
cybercrime, focusing especially on high -incidence districts like Jamtara and 
Deoghar. They have also taken a proactive approach to emerging cybercrime 
threats in newly emerging identified hotspots including Giridih, Dhanbad, Bokaro, 
Hazaribagh and Koderma. Through sustained and coordinated efforts, the police 
have successfully disrupted cybercriminal networks, leading to the arrest and 
prosecution of numerous offenders, thereby strengthening public security in these 
vulnerable areas. 
The police's key strateg y encompassed the identification of cybercrime hotspots 
via comprehensive data analysis and intelligence gathering, pinpointing Jamtara 
and Deoghar while recognizing the emergent threat landscape in other districts. 
The deployment of the 'Pratibimb' crime mapping module facilitated real -time 
location tracking and the precise pinpointing of cybercriminal activities, thereby 
expediting response measures. Coordinated intelligence sharing across districts 
through 'Pratibimb' platform led to targeted operations that effectively disrupted 
organized cybercriminal activities and culminated in the apprehension of a 
substantial number of cyber fraudsters. 
Centre for Cyber Operations and Security (CenCOPS) - Chandigarh 
4.7.13  The Centre for Cyber Operations and Security (CenCOPS) is a strategic initiative by 
Chandigarh Police in partnership with the Defence Research and Development Organisation 
(DRDO) aimed at strengthening cybersecurity infrastructure and operational capacity across 
northern India. It has four core functions data analytics and predictive policing, evidence 
collection with advanced forensics, cybersecurity and cybercrime investigation and Open 
Source Intelligence  (OSINT) and social media monitoring. It serves mu ltiple States/UTs 

 
 
94 
 
including Jammu & Kashmir, Punjab, Haryana, Uttarakhand, Himachal Pradesh and 
Chandigarh, providing a unified platform for digital forensics, cybercrime prevention and 
advanced analytical competencies, thereby boosting regional cyber resi lience and 
investigation effectiveness. 
4.7.14  CenCOPS is equipped with sophisticated tools for cybercrime investigation and 
forensic analysis, such as malware examination, forensic data recovery and real -time event 
monitoring. It also facilitates sentiment analysis to support predictive policing and enhance 
public safety. The data and command centre of CenCOPS integrates data from varied sources 
including the Crime and Criminal Tracking Network and Systems (CCTNS) and city -wide 
CCTV systems, enabling proactive crime prevention and detection. 
4.7.15  Additionally, CenCOPS incorporates citizen engagement initiatives and leverages 
advanced technologies like facial recognition to improve public safety. Its core functions 
encompass data analytics and pre dictive policing, advanced evidence collection and 
forensics, cybersecurity and cybercrime investigation, as well as Open Source Intelligence 
(OSINT) and social media monitoring making it a comprehensive hub for regional cyber 
operations and security enhancement. 
Recommendation/Observation 
4.7.16  The Committee appreciates and acknowledges the commendable efforts 
undertaken by certain State Governments like Telangana, Maharastra, Kerala, etc., in 
implementing innovative and effective measures to prevent cyb ercrime and safeguard 
the people. Considering these successful models adopted by those State Governments, 
the Committee recommends the Ministry of Home Affairs to coordinate with other 
States to adopt these proven best practices and support them in setting  up effective 
cybercrime prevention mechanisms as it will not only enhance their capacities to 
combat cyber threats but will also fortify the nation’s overall cyber resilience.
