95 
 
OBSERVATIONS/RECOMMENDATIONS AT A GLANCE 
 
The Ministry of Electronics & Information Technology (MeitY) 
The Committee appreciates the comprehensive legislative framework provided 
by the Information Technology Act, 2000 (IT Act) and its associated rules, which 
collectively serve as the cornerstone for regulating digital platforms and safeguarding 
Indian cyberspace.  
(para 3.2.12) 
 
The Committee observes that currently the existing law does not differentiate 
between synthetically generated and user -created content. Considering the increasing 
misuse of deepfakes and AI tools for generating harmful or misleading content, the 
Committee recommends that the legal framework be further strengthened to include 
express provisions in the legislation for handling content generated using Artificial 
Intelligence (AI) and similar emerging technologies. The Committee further 
recommends that to address issues of deepfake or obscene content being uploaded in the 
social media, MeitY should consider developing an innovative technological framework 
mandating all photos, videos and similar content shared on digital platforms to have a 
watermark as it would help to prove the origin of the content and make it more difficult 
to fake or edit/manipulate. The Committee is of the view that to make this initiative 
functional, MeitY should set up uniform technical standards for media provenance, 
while CERT -In would act as the coordinator for monitoring and issuing detection 
alerts.    
(para 3.2.13) 
 
The Committee is of the view that the grievance redressal system should be made 
more user -friendly and transparent to enhance public trust and ensure effective 
resolution of complaints. The Committee, therefore, recommends establishing a uniform 
format for filing complaints, setting clearly defined timelines for acknowledgment and 
resolution, and ensuring public disclosure of grievance statistics, including the number 
of complaints received, resolved and pending. The Committee further recommends that 
integrating the grievance redressal mechanism system with emerging data protection 
frameworks would help to create a more holistic regulatory environment that addresses 
concerns related to privacy, content moderation and cybersecurity in a cohesive and 
coordinated manner. 
(para 3.2.14) 
 
Recognising the challenges posed by technologies such as the metaverse, 
blockchain and generative AI, the Committee recommends the development of forward-
looking, flexible regulatory guidelines. The Committee is of the view that the safe 
harbour protection s available to intermediaries under the IT Act, 2000 should be 
reviewed periodically to strike a balance between their liability immunity and the need 
for greater accountability, especially where platforms fail to act on unlawful content. 
The Committee als o recommends a periodic review of enforcement mechanisms and 
penalty provisions under the IT Act, 2000 to ensure their deterrent effect remains 
robust.  
(para 3.2.15) 

 
 
96 
 
The Committee has observed that certain social media intermediaries at times 
fail to coop erate with law enforcement agencies and legal provisions in promptly 
removing unlawful content, including morphed videos, fake profiles, misinformation 
and content promoting violence based on religion or caste, thereby potentially abetting 
criminal activit ies and undermining public order. To address this critical gap, the 
Committee recommends that the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology, 
in consultation with the Ministry of Home Affairs and Ministry of Law and Justice, 
should consider amendin g the Information Technology Act, 2000, to introduce specific 
provisions that hold social media intermediaries legally accountable as when they fail to 
comply with lawful takedown orders within prescribed timelines. Such amendments 
should include graded pe nalties, including monetary fines and potential suspension of 
operations for persistent non -compliance, while ensuring that due process and appeal 
mechanisms are preserved to maintain the balance between content regulation and 
freedom of expression. 
(para 3.2.16) 
 
The Committee notes that the OTT platforms have emerged as a primary source 
of entertainment in India, with a reach that surpasses traditional cinema, including a 
substantial audience of minors. The Committee believes that unlike films, which requ ire 
mandatory pre -certification under the Cinematograph Act, OTT content currently 
operates under a self -classification system with post -release grievance redressal as per 
the IT (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021. The 
Committee further believes that although the rules require age ratings, parental 
controls and a grievance mechanism, the lack of pre -release checks and weak age 
verification still leave minors exposed to harmful content. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Government may consider establishment of a recognized panel of 
experts comprising child development specialists, educators, legal experts, social 
scientists and community representatives who will monitor the new OTT releases. The 
Committee is of the  view that the Post Release Review Panel will watch the flagged or 
trending content post release based on user complaints submitted through a 
standardised grievance mechanism. The Committee recommends that the Panel shall 
devise suitable norms and guidelines keeping in view the cultural ethos and sensitivity of 
the country. The Committee further recommends that the panel may also workout 
suitable penalties in case of infringement of these norms/guidelines. The Committee also 
recommends that OTT platforms mu st be required to adopt more strong technology 
enabled age verification systems and effective parental control mechanisms, going 
beyond simple self -declaration, to better restrict minors' access to age -inappropriate 
material. The Committee is of the view t hat regional language content classification and 
parental guidance notices should be mandated to offer better protection for rural and 
semi-urban viewers. 
 (para 3.2.17) 
 
  

 
 
97 
 
The Committee is of the view that enhanced coordination between Governmental 
agencies, regulators, technology platforms and civil society would be beneficial to 
strengthen the country’s ability to respond to cyber challenges. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends the establishment of formal ways for these groups to work 
together for  knowledge sharing, joint decision -making and capacity building in the 
cyberspace domain. 
(para 3.2.18) 
 
The Committee recommends that all apps should comply with a strict data 
protection and security standards as prescribed in applicable laws and regulations. The 
Committee also suggests setting compliance requirements for app stores, including 
regular audits to prevent malicious or harmful apps from being distributed. The 
Committee also recommends for development of an indigenous app store to facil itate 
Indian startups. 
(para 3.2.19) 
 
 
The Ministry of Information and Broadcasting (MIB) 
 
The Committee is of the view to introduce a strong verification system for 
offshore advertisers. The Committee recommends that the offshore advertisers must 
undergo a thorough digital verification process, like uploading official documents, 
completing a live video identification, etc., to prove their identity and must use zero -
trust frameworks during verification, where every access request from offshore 
advertisers (including changes to ads, payment details, or identity information) must be 
continuously authenticated and monitored to curb fraudulent and deceptive advertising 
targeting Indian users. 
(para 3.3.4) 
 
Department of Telecommunications (DoT) 
 
The Committee commends the DoT for its significant initiatives to tackle 
cybercrime using telecom resources, highlighting the successful Sanchar Saathi portal 
that engages citizens in protecting digital identities. The Committee notes the strong 
impact of technologies l ike the Centralized International Out Roamer (CIOR) system 
blocking over 1.35 crore spoofed calls in a day and reducing such incidents by 98% 
along with the use of AI, facial recognition and the Financial Fraud Risk Indicator 
(FRI) for innovative preventio n. The Committee also acknowledges the Digital 
Intelligence Platform (DIP) for fostering real -time collaboration among key sectors to 
fight cybercrime. To keep pace with evolving fraud techniques, the Committee 
recommends deeper integration and automation of data flow between existing systems 
such as the Sanchar Saathi portal, Digital Intelligence Platform and law enforcement 
databases. 
(para 3.4.8) 
 
 

 
 
98 
 
Considering the importance of public awareness in cybercrime prevention, the 
Committee observes that sustai ned multilingual and cross -platform campaigns should 
be intensified particularly in regional and rural areas so that more citizens know how to 
report fraud and reclaim control over their mobile credentials. The Committee 
recommends that it would be valuabl e to take the Mobile Number Validation Service 
(MNVS) beyond the current stage and implement it nationwide in collaboration with 
banks, NBFCs and fintech platforms to limit the use of mobile numbers in fraudulent or 
mule accounts. 
(para 3.4.9) 
The Committee notes that the regulatory frameworks governing the use of 
telecom channels such as automatic calls, bulk SMSs and Voice over Internet  Protocol 
(VoIP) to be strengthened in order to prevent misuse of these services for phishing 
campaigns. The Comm ittee recommends continued investment in indigenous AI 
technologies like Artificial Intelligence and Facial Recognition powered Solution for 
Telecom SIM Subscriber Verification (ASTR) to ensure that SIM card issuance with a 
focus on preventing identity fraud at the point of issue.  
(para 3.4.10) 
 
The DoT informed the Committee that about 570 banks and financial 
institutions (FIs) use the Digital Intelligence Platform (DIP) to access Mobile Number 
Revocation List (MNRL) and financial fraud indicators for act ing on suspicious bank 
accounts, leading to action on 20.89 lakh accounts. The Committee commends these 
efforts to tackle telecom-linked financial fraud and recommends that efforts should also 
be made to ensure swift sharing of risk alerts and revocations from all these institutions, 
thus minimizing manual delays in addressing fraud. 
(para 3.4.11) 
 
Telecom Regulatory Authority of India (TRAI) 
 
The Committee appreciates TRAI’s commendable efforts in organizing over 650 
consumer awareness initiatives and driving proactive public outreach to promote 
telecom cybersecurity awareness. The collaborative efforts involving Telecom Service 
Providers (TSPs), NGOs and educational institutions reflect a strong commitment to 
inclusive digital l iteracy. Since TRAI has not yet directly collaborated with MeitY's 
Information Security Education and Awareness (ISEA) project, the Committee 
recommends that TRAI should be encouraged to partner with MeitY’s  ISEA project to 
expand digital safety outreach through role -based learni ng modules and online self -
learning platforms. Further, the Committee recommends that in order to strengthen its 
capacity in dealing with emerging regulatory and technological issues, the Ministry of 
Communications may be approached to expedite the proposa l to enhance TRAI’s staff 
strength. 
(para 3.5.16) 
 
 

 
 
99 
 
With respect to the awareness programmes conducted by TRAI, the Committee 
is of the view that TRAI can also work with universities and research groups to create a 
system to check how effective its awareness programs are working, especially for people 
in rural areas and other vulnerable groups. The Committee feels that a dedicated 
budget may also be considered for cyber literacy pilot projects in schools and 
communities, with regular reviews jointly conducted by DoT, MeitY and TRAI. 
Further, the Committee suggests that the Government may consider the creation of new 
expert verticals within TRAI to address cyber threats, quantum -safe networks and the 
economic challenges of fast -developing telecom techn ologies, enabling a secure digital 
transition for the country. 
(para 3.5.17) 
 
 Considering the steady increase in complaints related to Unregistered 
Telemarketers (UTMs), the Committee is of the view that it is essential to focus on 
targeted actions to address this challenge more effectively. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that measures should be taken to enhance real -time detection mechanisms 
to identify and block UTMs proactively. The Committee suggests that a centralized 
blacklist database shoul d be maintained and it needs to be shared across all telecom 
service providers to ensure swift disconnection of offending numbers. 
(para 3.5.18) 
 
Reserve Bank of India 
 
The Committee appreciates the RBI’s comprehensive framework for Customer 
Due Diligence and Fraud Risk Management, which reflects a strong commitment in 
securing the financial system through rigorous KYC practices, proactive monitoring 
and robust supervisor y oversight. The Committee is of the view that increasing 
collaboration and real -time information sharing between financial institutions and law 
enforcement agencies can accelerate response times and improve the effectiveness of 
preventive measures.  
(para 3.6.30) 
The Committee recommends that banks should regularly train their employees 
and organise awareness programs for customers about the latest methods that 
fraudsters use. The Committee is of the view that by doing so, both staff and customers 
will be better prepared to recognise and respond to new types of scams and thereby 
complementing technological solutions with human vigilance. 
(para 3.6.31) 
The Committee notes the deadline fixed by RBI to all banks to complete the 
migration process to the exclusi ve Internet Domain ‘.bank.in’ is 31 st October, 2025 with 
the Institute for Development and Research in Banking Technology (IDRBT) 
designated as the registrar for this process. The Committee believes that till date no 
major banks have migrated to the domain. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
RBI should supervise the entire migration process, ensuring that all banks adhere to the 
schedule and complete the migration process within the prescribed deadline. The 
Committee further recommends that the RBI s hould conduct public awareness 

 
 
100 
 
campaigns to educate consumers on identifying genuine ‘.bank.in’ domains, thereby 
reinforcing public trust. The Committee also suggests that to have an audit mechanism 
for verifying the domain usage and security standards, as  it would further strengthen 
the preventive impact of this initiative.  
(para 3.6.32) 
 The Committee appreciates the good progress made in connecting banks with 
the Indian Cybercrime Coordination Centre (I4C) portal through Application 
Programming Interface (API) integration. This integration is important for improving 
how cybercrime complai nts and financial fraud cases are handled. So far, 19 banks 
have fully completed the API integration and over 350 banks have joined the Citizen 
Financial Cyber Fraud Reporting and Management System (CFCFRMS) platform. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends t he RBI to extend this API integration to all 
remaining banks, including smaller and cooperative banks, to ensure full coverage and 
a strong nationwide cybersecurity response. The Committee also recommends that a 
clear deadline should be set for completing this integration. Further, the Committee 
suggests that the RBI may provide support to banks facing technical or vendor -related 
difficulties by offering dedicated technical help or setting up a centralized helpdesk to 
resolve issues quickly. The Committee b elieves that RBI, together with I4C, needs to 
have a regular review process to closely monitor the progress of this integration effort. 
The Committee recommends initiating early collaboration among stakeholders to 
develop and implement quantum -resistant cryptographic solutions, ensuring a smooth 
shift from legacy systems to future-proof technologies.  
 (para 3.6.33) 
 The Committee acknowledges the RBI’s proactive and innovative approach  
through the Digital Payment Intelligence Platform (DPIP). It is truly i mpressive, 
showcasing a strong commitment to safeguarding the digital payments ecosystem. 
However, the Committee recommends setting  a timeline for the rollout of the DPIP, 
ensuring its speedy implementation to address the rising digital payment frauds 
effectively.  
(para 3.6.34) 
The Committee also appreciates the implementation of MuleHunter.ai, as a pilot 
phase, in the four banks Canara Bank, Punjab National Bank, Bank of Baroda and 
Bank of India to validate the tool's effectiveness and accuracy in detecting mule 
accounts. Considering the promising results of MuleHunter.ai, the Committee 
recommends that MuleHunter.ai be gradually expanded to other banks, both public 
and private sector banks, to cover a broader segment of the digital payments ecosyste m. 
The Committee further recommends that the upcoming Central Payment Fraud 
Information Registry (CPFIR) be fully leveraged as a centralized, comprehensive fraud 
data repository to facilitate sector -wide analysis, coordination and mitigation of 
emerging fraud threats. 
(para 3.6.35) 
  

 
 
101 
 
The Committee notes that the Reserve Bank -Integrated Ombudsman Scheme 
(RB-IOS) provides a mechanism for victims of digital payment fraud to lodge 
complaints, reflecting RBI’s commitment to consumer protection. However, the 
increasing turnaround time (TAT) for resolving unauthorised transaction complaints 
from 36.3 days in FY 2022-23 to 68.3 days in FY 2024-25 indicates a growing backlog or 
complexity in case resolution. The Committee, therefore, recommends the RBI to 
consider enhancing the Ombudsman’s opera tional capacity by adopting advanced 
digital case management tools, streamlining complaint resolution workflows and 
possibly increasing staffing to reduce delay in resolving the complaints.  
(para 3.6.36) 
 The Committee appreciates the proactive stand take n by RBI in addressing 
quantum computing threats through coordinated efforts and the formation of an Inter -
Departmental Group in this regard is commendable. The Committee suggests that the 
Regulated Entities (REs) need to accelerate the development of thei r Cryptographic Bill 
of Material (CBoM) to ensure a smoother transition to quantum-safe technologies. 
(para 3.6.37) 
 The Committee notes the concern about illicit deposits into account holders’ 
banks without their knowledge and it is good that existing laws protect innocent account 
holders from criminal liability. However, considering the unauthorised transactions 
including facilitating money laundering or fraud, the Committee recommends that the 
approval of recipient should be necessary for receiving money  in their account, actively 
involving customers in their own protection. The Committee feels that this measure can 
also ensure that money do not get transferred to mule account. 
(para 3.6.38) 
The Committee advises the integration of digital ID checks like Aadhaar-based 
e-KYC to ensure every person initiating a deposit is authentic, thereby limiting 
anonymity that criminals exploit. The Committee observed that, despite the 
implementation of KYC measures, mule accounts continue to be opened at banks, 
indicating lapses or negligence on the part of bank personnel. The Committee 
recommends that strict action be taken against those found responsible to ensure 
accountability and deter future violations. The Committee suggests exploring the 
implementation of behavio ural biometrics that analyse customer pattern, like typing 
speed or mouse movement of users to spot unusual activity and alert the bank or 
temporarily block suspicious deposits. 
(para 3.6.39) 
 The Committee appreciates the RBI’s well -structured liability limits to protect 
customers by fairly assigning zero or limited liability based on timely reporting and 
accountability, thereby fostering trust in digital banking. The Committee feels that 
liability limits be made more customer -centric and effective by personalizing them 
according to individual security behaviour. The Committee suggests RBI to explore the 
possibility of rewarding users who adopt strong digital hygiene and report fraud 
promptly with lower liability.  
(para 3.6.40) 

 
 
102 
 
The Committee recommends the introduction of affordable micro -insurance 
policies designed to protect customers against losses exceeding standard liability limits, 
thus providing a reliable financial safety. The Committee believes that banks should use 
AI tools to proactively reduce customer liability upon detecting suspicious activity, even 
before formal complaints are made. The Committee recommends exploring the zero 
liability periods and providing personalized assistance for vulnerable groups such as the 
elderly or differently -abled to enhance inclusiveness. The Committee also suggests 
enabling trusted family members/friends to assist in reporting unauthorised 
transactions to support customers who may be unable to act qui ckly themselves. The 
Committee recommends establishment of a forgiveness program for first-time victims of 
fraud, who maintain good security practices afterward, in order to restore confidence 
and encourage continued digital adoption. 
(para 3.6.41) 
 The Committee appreciates the extensive and sustained efforts of RBI in 
enhancing financial literacy and digital payment awareness across diverse 
demographics, reflecting a strong commitment to financial inclusion and consumer 
protection. The Committee rec ommends that the awareness programs should be 
conducted both in Government and private schools so that the budding generation 
becomes aware of cybercrimes and is less likely to fall prey to it. The Committee also 
recommends that the awareness programs shou ld reach at the grassroots level through 
active involvement of Panchayat representatives, local community leaders and 
representatives of people of the concerned constituencies. The Committee feels that to 
foster inclusive participation integrate financial literacy sessions into village level 
gatherings, cooperatives, self-help groups and local festivals.  
(para 3.6.42) 
Department of Financial Services (DFS) 
The Committee observes that financial intermediaries operating in different 
segments such as banks, stock brokers, insurance companies and mutual fund platforms 
have developed distinct user interfaces and operational frameworks, leading to 
inconsistencies in user experience. The Committee further observes that this 
fragmentation creates barriers for users to m igrate from one platform to another, 
resulting potential security vulnerabilities as users struggle to adapt to varying 
authentication methods, transaction processes and interface designs. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the DFS in coordination w ith relevant regulators including 
RBI, SEBI, IRDAI and PFRDA, should implement standardized guidelines for user 
interface design and customer experience across all financial platforms. The Committee 
further recommends that the DFS should also mandate the a doption of standardized 
APIs and data portability features that enable users to seamlessly transfer their 
financial profiles including Demat accounts transaction history, and preferences across 
platforms, thereby reducing switching costs.  
(para 3.7.4) 
The Committee notes that despite advanced AI, transaction monitoring and data 
sharing, mule accounts persist in banks. The Committee feels that current technologies 
have not fully stopped fraudsters. The Committee observes that fraudsters often recruit 
vulnerable people via social media, scams and local networks. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends creating a formal network involving local communities, 
Panchayats, local groups, NGOs, etc., to watch and report suspicious mule recruitment. 

 
 
103 
 
The Committee further  recommends that training these groups and offering small 
rewards to them will motivate timely reporting of suspicious activities. The Committee 
feels that combining this human intelligence with existing technology is necessary to 
build a stronger defence against mule accounts. 
(para 3.7.5) 
Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) 
The Committee notes that cybersecurity incidents in the financial market sector 
can cause large -scale data breaches, operational disruptions, and significant financial 
losses. The Committee also notes that these threats are growing in sophistication and 
increasingly target trading systems, depository operations, and sensitive investor data. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends SEBI to establish a robust, multi -layered 
cybersecurity framework; mandate regular independent audits; implement real -time 
incident-disclosure protocols; and develop collaborative platforms for proactive risk 
monitoring. 
(para 3.8.8) 
The Committee has observed the growing phenomenon of unsolicited a nd 
unregistered financial influencers on social media platforms who promise exorbitant or 
unrealistic returns to the public, often leading to misinformation, financial losses and 
potential scams. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Significant So cial 
Media Intermediary (SSMIs) be directed to allow only SEBI registered financial 
influencers to advice and to do marketing on social media platforms. The Committee 
further recommends SEBI for exploring the possibility of "verified tick" (a verification 
mark indicating that an account or entity has been authenticated and confirmed as 
legitimate by a platform or service) for the SEBI registered investment advisors on 
social media platforms. The Committee also recommends that such registration should 
be sub ject to prescribed disclosure norms and conduct guidelines, including the clear 
display of registration numbers, disclaimer and any potential conflicts of interest on 
social media profiles. The Committee recommends that SEBI, in collaboration with 
MeitY, establish a robust mechanism for identifying unregistered financial influencers 
and swiftly issuing legally binding takedown orders to social media intermediaries for 
specific content found to violate SEBI regulations. The Committee also recommends 
that SEBI should make suitable mandatory legal provisions for violation of disclosure 
norms and take strict actions against those who disseminate misleading information. 
(para 3.8.9) 
  The Committee is of the view that SEBI should collaborate with social media 
platforms to develop mechanisms for identifying and curbing unregistered financial 
influencers, including leveraging artificial intelligence tools for content monitoring and 
enabling public reporting of suspect financial promotions. The Committee further 
recommends that SEBI should also take strict enforcement actions against those who 
disseminate misleading information.  The Committee is also of the view that SEBI 
should conduct re gular awareness campaigns to educate investors on the risks of 
following unverified financial advice. 
 (para 3.8.10) 
 

 
 
104 
 
National Payments Corporation of India (NPCI) 
The Committee appreciates NPCI’s comprehensive and proactive efforts to 
secure India’s digital payment ecosystem, like consistent issuance of security guidelines, 
strict enforcement of compliance among stakeholders and rigorous annual audits to 
identify promptly and address vulnerabilities. The Committee notes that NPCI provides 
a real -time fraud monitoring system free of cost to over 500 banks. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends NPCI that this valuable service be expanded and made available 
to a greater number of banks, including smaller regional and cooperative banks, to 
enhance overall security coverage across the entire banking ecosystem. The Committee 
is of the view that widening access will strengthen early detection and prevention of 
fraudulent transactions nationwide, thereby improving consumer protection and 
reinforcing trust in digital payment platforms. 
(para 3.9.8) 
 
Central Bureau of Investigation (CBI) 
The Committee in CBI’s reply noted that certain States have withdrawn general 
consent required under the Delhi Special Police Establishment Act, 1946 (DSPE Act). It 
creates major hurdle in the seamless investigation and asking specific consent again on 
a case-to-case basis is time consuming, creates stress on resources and manpower, can 
hamper the quality of investigation and is detrimental to timely and prompt 
investigation. The Co mmittee, therefore, recommends that the MHA must engage in 
active consultation with those State Governments that have not given consent in order 
to resolve this issue. Further the Committee is of the view that the Delhi Special Police 
Establishment Act, 19 46 may be amended in order to empower the CBI to investigate 
the cybercrime cases in the country, without the consent of the State. 
 (para 3.10.15) 
National Investigation Agency (NIA) 
The Committee notes the legal and procedural gaps identified by the NIA,  
particularly concerning delays in early stage data preservation. The Committee 
recommends MHA for the establishment of standardised protocols and robust 
monitoring mechanisms to ensure the preservation requests and also to issue necessary 
directives mandating State police to initiate data preservation requests within the first 
24 hours of a cybercrime report to mitigate the risk of losing volatile evidence, such as 
IP logs and server files, when NIA assumes investigation from the State police. The 
Committee further recommends that the SSMIs be required to ensure timely provision 
of required data to NIA, regardless of the physical location of the data centers. 
(para 3.10.30) 
Indian Cyber Crime Coordination Centre (I4C) 
The Committee suggests exploring the possibilities of conversion of complaints 
reported on the National Cybercrime Reporting Portal (NCRP) into e -FIRs thereby 
resolving legal issues arising during the refunding of the lien amount to the victims on 
account of non-registration of the FIRs. 
(para 3.11.9) 

 
 
105 
 
The Committee is of the view that standardisation of cybercrime units at the 
State level. The Committee, therefore, recommends  establishing State Cybercrime 
Coordination Centres (S4C) in all States/UTs, on lines similar to Indian Cyber Crime 
Coordination Centre (I4C), to ensure seamles s coordination between I4C and S tate-
level units for effective intelligence sharing. 
(para 3.11.10) 
National Critical Information Infrastructure Protection Centre (NCIIPC) 
To strengthen national cybersecurity resilience, the Committee recommends that 
NCIIPC should consider Sector -specific Computer Security Incident Response 
Teams(CSIRTs), such as CSIRT in Finance sector (CSIRT -Fin) and CSIRT in Power 
sector (CSIRT -Power) to be expanded to more critical information infrastructure 
sectors such as healthcare, road transport, railways, aviation and telecommunications, 
where they can address sector -specific threats like ransomware in hospitals, 
cyberattacks on transport infrastruc ture, and network intrusions in telecom systems to 
improve cyber resilience within respective critical sectors. The Committee is of the view 
that a national coordination mechanism should be established to unify sectoral CSIRTs 
through a central hub that facilitates real-time threat intelligence sharing, joint incident 
response protocols and secure communication channels.  
(para 3.12.9) 
MEA and International Cooperation 
Recognising the critical need for rapid international cooperation, the Committee 
suggests instituting a dedicated 24x7 fusion desk or contact point staffed with 
multilingual, legally trained personnel, as envisioned under Article 42 of the UN 
Cybercrime Convention (2024). The Committee is of the view that such a unit would 
facilitate swift com munication and coordination with foreign agencies on preservation 
and cooperation requests, thereby mitigating the currently prolonged execution 
timelines of MLATs and Letters Rogatory. 
(para 3.13.5) 
The Committee observes the challenge posed by the l ack of a statutory mandate 
for compelling data disclosure from foreign service providers (cloud/email/VoIP), where 
most critical data is stored outside India. The Committee is of the view that relying on 
voluntary cooperation and slow Mutual Legal Assistance Treaty (MLAT) processes 
delays access to vital digital evidence, affecting cybercrime investigations. To address 
this, the Committee recommends pursuing legal and diplomatic measures to enable 
enforceable and timely data disclosure. 
(para 3.13.6) 
Centre for Development of Advanced Computing (C-DAC) 
The Committee appreciates the insightful and forward -thinking of C -DAC to 
harness Artificial intelligence (AI) and Machine Learning (ML) technologies for 
predictive cyber threat intelligence, which is crucial f or safeguarding critical national 
infrastructure in today’s rapidly evolving cybercrime. The Committee recommends C -

 
 
106 
 
DAC to explore the development of an AI/ML -powered that predicts cyber threats to 
critical sectors by integrating data from existing tools s uch as Deepfake Detection, 
Vishleshak, CDACSIEM and Rakshak DNS, along with external global threat 
intelligence. The Committee suggests creation of a national training platform for 
cybersecurity that uses virtual reality (VR), thereby offering realistic pr actice sessions 
and certification program to build a skilled workforce across the law enforcement 
agencies, judiciary and enterprise. 
(para 3.14.8) 
Indian Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT-In)  
The Committee acknowledges CERT -In’s crucial role in safe guarding India’s 
cybersecurity by effectively managing threat detection, incident response and 
coordination across multiple sectors. To strengthen its capabilities further, the 
Committee recommends the adoption of an AI -driven Cyber Threat Intelligence 
Platform that enables quicker and more intelligent identification and handling of cyber 
threats. Further, the Committee encourages CERT-In to build strong partnerships with 
start-ups and academic institutions to foster innovation and develop a skilled 
cybersecurity workforce. The Committee also recommends conducting regular, flexible 
cybersecurity drills in collaboration with sectoral teams to ensure CERT -In remains 
agile and prepared to tackle emerging cyber challenges promptly. 
(para 3.15.6) 
Ministry of Education 
The Committee recommends that to effectively build a resilient digital 
generation, cybercrime prevention and cyber hygiene education should be integrated 
into the school curriculum starting from early grades to senior secondary level. The 
Committee f urther recommends that this curriculum inclusion be mandated for all 
schools irrespective whether it governed by Central board or State board to ensure no 
child is left behind. The Committee also recommends for introduction of mandatory 
cyber education sub ject in non -technical courses across colleges and universities, 
integrating modules on cyber awareness, digital safety, responsible online conduct and 
basics of data privacy. 
(para 3.16.7) 
Ministry of Corporate Affairs 
 The Committee appreciates the comprehensive and robust cybersecurity 
measures already implemented in MCA21 portal to protect India’s corporate 
regulatory ecosystem. However, recognizing the increasing sophistication of cyber 
threats, especially those exploiting shell companies and frau dulent corporate entities, 
the Committee recommends the Ministry of Corporate Affairs the adoption of an AI -
powered, blockchain -based verification and continuous monitoring system integrated 
into MCA21. The Committee is of the view that this framework shou ld enable seamless 
and automated collaboration with law enforcement and regulatory agencies for rapid 
investigation and action of any fraudulent activities. 
(para 3.17.9) 

 
 
107 
 
Legal framework available to prevent cybercrimes 
 The Committee appreciates that the Government has put in place strong legal 
frameworks to effectively address and prevent cybercrime. However, the Committee 
observes persistent and evolving challenges driven by technological advances and agile 
cybercriminal tactics. The Committee recommends  that while enforcing the CERT -In 
mandates with respect to VPN logging to aid in prevention of cybercrime, a balanced 
approach must be adopted to protect citizens’ right to privacy. The Committee suggests 
that mandatory data retention and reporting obligat ions for VPNs be accompanied by 
strict safeguards, including limiting data access solely to authorised investigations 
subject to judicial oversight, along with conducting periodic compliance audits to 
maintain transparency. The Committee encourages the use  of privacy -preserving 
technologies, to enable lawful access verification while minimizing exposure of sensitive 
data. Further, the Committee also recommends conducting public awareness 
programmes on responsible VPN use and cybersecurity hygiene.  The Committee notes 
that some IT Intermediaries have failed to comply with the legitimate request made by 
the Law Enforcement Agencies for data request and removal of online unlawful 
content. Hence, the Committee recommends that the existing provisions of Sect ion 79 of 
the IT Act, 2000 pertaining to safe harbour needs to be revisited to ensure 
accountability of IT intermediaries whose platforms are being misused. 
(para 4.1.7) 
 The Committee observes that the current rules require grievance and nodal 
officers on ly for Significant Social Media Intermediaries (SSMIs), leaving other IT 
intermediaries without designated contacts, which hampers effective communication 
and timely data access for law enforcement agencies. Further, many intermediaries are 
foreign-based without any mandatory registration or local presence, causing delays and 
non-responsiveness. The Committee, therefore recommends establishing a mandatory 
national registration system for all IT intermediaries operating in India, regardless of 
size or origin , requiring them to appoint local grievance officers and nodal contact 
persons with clear timelines for responses. Further, for SSMIs, (Facebook, WhatsApp, 
Telegram etc), the Committee suggests increasing the number of nodal officers by 
appointing regional representatives to facilitate faster and region specific coordination 
with law enforcement agencies.  
(para 4.1.8) 
The Committee appreciates that the IT Act and BNSS empower law enforcement 
agencies to take action against cybercrimes and other offences, r egardless of where the 
crime occurs, as long as the impact or the perpetrator involves India. However, the 
Committee observes that significant practical challenges in cross -border enforcement 
due to jurisdictional conflicts and limited international cooper ation, which hinder 
effective action against cybercriminals operating globally. The Committee recommends 
exploring the possibility for the establishment of a dedicated International Cybercrime 
Liaison Unit with specialized legal and technical experts to resolve the problem. 
(para 4.1.9) 
 The Committee notes that many provisions in the IT Act, 2000 are bailable and 
attract low penalties. The accused can get bail easily and the punishments or fines for 
these crimes are relatively low. The Committee, therefore , recommends reviewing and 
amending the IT Act to impose harsher penalties on offenders. 
(para 4.1.10) 

 
 
108 
 
 It has been brought to the notice of the Committee that there is no provision in 
the IT Act, 2000 that holds IT intermediaries liable for compensating v ictims for 
financial losses, psychological harm, or reputational damage caused due to their 
inaction even after being alerted by G overnment agencies about unlawful or malicious 
content. The Committee, therefore, recommends amending the IT Act, 2000 to 
introduce provisions in the IT Act, making IT intermediaries to compensate victims for 
such acts. 
(para 4.1.11) 
 The Committee notes that Section 78 of the IT Act, 2000 mandates that any 
offence under the Act be investigated by a police officer not below the rank of Inspector. 
The Committee recommen ds that for more effective hand ling of cybercrime cases, 
investigations should be assigned only to police officers senior to the rank of Inspector. 
The Committee also recommends that those officers must be well-versed in all aspects of 
cybercrime, including digital forensics, cyber laws, investigative techniques, cyber 
hygiene and incident response as this will help to resolve cybercrimes efficiently and 
quickly. 
(para 4.1.12) 
In its meeting the Committee noted that, in view of the rapidly evolving nature of 
cyber threats and the increasing complexity of technology -enabled crimes, it is essential 
that India’s cybercrime legal framework undergoes regular updates to remain effective 
and future -ready. The Committee recomm ends that the Ministry of Home Affairs, in 
collaboration with the Ministry of Electronics & Information Technology, Ministry of 
Law & Justice and relevant regulators, institutionalize a structured, multidisciplinary 
review mechanism to assess cybercrime la ws periodically. Adopting such a systematic 
and periodic legislative review will foster a proactive, resilient and future -ready 
legislative ecosystem allowing India to remain adaptive to emerging threats and to 
safeguard national security, digital innovati on, and citizen rights in the face of a fast 
changing cyber world.  
(para 4.1.13) 
The Committee notes that ambiguity in the suspension of suspicious transactions 
has caused inconsistent responses from financial institutions, weakening efforts against 
cybercrime and money laundering. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
financial institutions may add provisions to incorporate immediate suspension of 
suspicious transactions upon alerts from monitoring systems or law enforcement  
agencies. The Committ ee specifies that those provisions should clarify that such 
preventive actions comply with all regulatory obligations and include provisions for 
disciplinary measures against officials negligent or complicit in facilitating illicit 
activities. 
(para 4.1.14) 
The Committee recommends that a certified foundational course in  
cybersecurity be made a prerequisite for entry-level police recruitment. The Committee 
observes that such recruited personnel would be in a position to better  handle modern 
digital threats from the outset of their policing careers. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the G overnment may consider integrating this requirement into the 
recruitment and training framework. 
(para 4.1.15) 

 
 
109 
 
 
 The Committee recommends that the capacity of Law Enforcement Agencies 
(LEAs) be enhanced through regular specialized training for police officers, prosecutors 
and judicial officials in areas such as cyber forensics, investigative techniques, cyber 
hygiene and cyber laws.  The Committee is of the view that States and Union Territories 
should be supported in establishing dedicated cybercrime training and research centers 
to ensure a consistent and high standard of skill development among LEA personnel 
nationwide. The Comm ittee further recommends investing in advanced forensic 
infrastructure, including state -of-the-art labs that cover Memory Forensics, Network 
Forensics, Image Enhancement, Malware Analysis, Cryptocurrency Forensics and 
Mobile Forensics, making these facilities accessible to LEAs across the country.  
(para 4.1.16) 
 The Committee observes that at present, regulation of online gaming in our 
country is overseen by various Ministries and various State Governments resulting in a 
fragmented and at times, inconsiste nt approach to enforcement and consumer 
protection. Recognizing these challenges, the Committee recommends the Central 
Government for the establishment of a dedicated ecosystem for Online Gaming in the 
country through a comprehensive consultative process. The Committee is of the view 
that this system should not only oversee the sector to address concerns related to real 
money/betting apps but also foster the development of a vibrant multimedia/animation/  
gaming ecosystem within the country. 
(para 4.1.17) 
 The Committee notes that cybercrime laws in India are currently scattered 
across multiple statutes, creating enforcement and judicial challenges. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that there is an urgent need for comprehensive, unified 
cybercrime legislation. This new legislation should clearly define cyber offences, address 
emerging technologies and provide strong, adaptable penal provisions. The Committee 
further recommends the establishment of a specialized, dedicated Integrated 
Cybercrime Task Force,  exclusively focused on investigating cybercrime. The 
Committee suggests that this Task Force should operate nationally with the mandate 
and capacity to tackle complex, multifaceted and transnational cyber offences. 
(para 4.1.18) 
Practices adopted by countries to prevent Cybercrime 
 The Committee observes that it is imperative to strengthen bilateral and 
multilateral agreements with international law enforcement agencies. To combat 
cybercrime, the Committee recommends effective Mutual Legal Assistance Treaties and 
Memoranda of Understanding are necessary and efforts may be taken to achieve this 
goal. The Committee notes that difference in law enforcement expertise hinder effective 
investigations and therefore  recommends regular joint training sessions focused on 
digital forensics, blockchain analysis and virtual asset tracing, involving both domestic 
and international partners.  
(para 4.6.14) 
 

 
 
110 
 
The Committee recommends the development of secure, interoperable p latforms 
for real -time cyber threat intelligence sharing between countries and  relevant 
organisations. The Committee further recommends establishing a multilingual, user -
friendly online platform through which citizens can easily report cybercrimes, 
improving response times and data collection. Recognizing the vital role of public -
private collaboration, the Committee further recommends closer partnerships with 
global technology, financial and telecommunications sectors, as this would facilitate 
better monitoring, detection and coordinated takedown of malicious infrastructure.  
(para 4.6.15) 
Innovative measures adopted by States to deal Cybercrime 
The Committee appreciates and acknowledges the commendable efforts 
undertaken by certain State Governments like Telangana, Maharastra, Kerala, etc., in 
implementing innovative and effective measures to prevent cybercrime and safeguard 
the people. Considering these successful models adopted by those State Governments, 
the Committee recommends the Ministry of Home Aff airs to coordinate with other 
States to adopt these proven best practices and support them in setting up effective 
cybercrime prevention mechanisms as it will not only enhance their capacities to 
combat cyber threats but will also fortify the nation’s overall cyber resilience. 
(para 4.7.16)

AJAY MAKAN 1
FINAL DISSENT NOTE OF Sh. AJAY MAKAN
To,
The Honourable Chairman,
Department-Related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Home Affairs,
Parliament of India, New Delhi
Subject: Dissent Note and Proposed Amendments to the Recommendations in the Draft
Two Hundred Fifty-Fourth Report on ‘Cyber Crime – Ramifications, Protection and
Prevention’
Respected Chairman,
I extend my sincere appreciation for the Committee's diligent and comprehensive work in
producing Report No. 254. The report meticulously details the grave and escalating threat of
cybercrime, which poses a significant danger to our citizens' financial security, psychological
well-being, and national stability. I completely agree with the report's core objective: to create
a robust legal and institutional framework to protect the people of India from cybercriminals,
financial fraudsters, and malicious actors who exploit our burgeoning digital ecosystem.
However, while I fully support the intent to strengthen our defences against crime, I am
compelled to register my profound concerns regarding several key recommendations. In their
current form, these proposals risk creating a legal architecture that, while intended to enhance
security, could be used to curtail fundamental rights guaranteed by our Constitution
disproportionately. Specifically, I am concerned about the potential for these measures to
stifle freedom of expression, undermine the operational viability of neutral and independent
digital news media, and erode the privacy of our citizens. The liberal voices of civil society,
which are the bedrock of a healthy democracy, could be inadvertently silenced.
Our challenge is to fight crime without sacrificing liberty. To this end, I wish to propose the
following specific, point-wise amendments to the Committee's recommendations. These
suggestions are offered in a constructive spirit and aim to achieve a more judicious balance
that protects our security and our democratic values.
Several draft recommendations could be weaponised against neutral digital news and civil
society—especially the proposals to (a) “review” safe-harbour and (b) addgraded penalties
up to suspensionof platforms for takedown delays, and (c)mandatory pre-certification of
OTTcontent by CBFC. These need tight constitutional guardrails to avoid prior restraint,
coercive censorship, or licensing-by-stealth.

AJAY MAKAN 2
FINAL DISSENT NOTE OF Sh. AJAY MAKAN
Proposed Amendments to the Recommendations of the Committee
1. Regarding the Review of "Safe Harbour" Protections (Reference: Para 3.2.15 &
4.1.7)
• Existing Recommendation:The Committee recommends a periodic review of "safe
harbour" protections under Section 79 of the IT Act, 2000, to strike a "balance"
between intermediary immunity and accountability.
• Concern:The vague call to "review" and "rebalance" the safe harbour principle
threatens to dilute the crucial legal standard set by the Supreme Court inShreya
Singhal v. Union of India. This could compel platforms to engage in pre-emptive
censorship of legitimate content—including critical journalism and civil society
discourse—to avoid legal liability.
• Proposed Amendment:I propose that the recommendation be amended to uphold
and reinforce the core principle of intermediary immunity explicitly as mandated by
the Apex court in the Shreya Singhal case. Instead of a general review, the focus
should be on strengthening procedural safeguards. The amended recommendation
should read:
"The Committee recommends that the principle of 'safe harbour' under Section 79 of the IT
Act, 2000, be preserved. T o enhance accountability, the process for content takedown must be
strengthened. It should be explicitly codified that an intermediary's obligation to take down
content is triggeredonly upon receiving a formal, legally vetted order from a competent
court of law or a duly constituted independent quasi-judicial body.This will prevent the
arbitrary removal of content and ensure that any restriction on speech is subject to judicial
oversight. Nothing in this review shall dilute the standard laid down in Shreya Singhal v.
Union of India: platforms acquire ‘actual knowledge’ only upon a court order or order by a
competent authority under Section 69A.”

AJAY MAKAN 3
FINAL DISSENT NOTE OF Sh. AJAY MAKAN
2. Regarding Penalties for Non-Compliance, including Suspension of Operations
(Reference: Para 3.2.16)
• Existing Recommendation:The Committee recommends amending the IT Act to
introduce graded penalties for non-compliance with takedown orders, including
"potential suspension of operations" for social media intermediaries.
• Concern:Granting the executive the power to suspend a platform's operations is
disproportionate and dangerous. It acts as a "kill switch" that can be used to coerce
platforms into complying with politically motivated takedown demands, thereby
silencing dissent and independent media.
• Proposed Amendment:I strongly urge theremoval of the "suspension of
operations" clause. The recommendation should be revised to focus on proportionate
financial penalties adjudicated by an independent body. The amended
recommendation should state:
"The Committee recommends that a system of calibrated and proportionate monetary
penalties be introduced for non-compliance withreasoned court orders or orders under
Section 69A and its 2009 Rules. The imposition of any such penalty must be subject to a
transparent process and an effective right to appeal before an independent judicial tribunal,
ensuring that the executive cannot unilaterally impose punitive measures."
3. Regarding Mandatory Pre-Certification for OTT Content (Reference: Para 3.2.17)
• Existing Recommendation:The Committee recommends amending the IT Rules,
2021, to introduce a "mandatory pre-certification process for OTT content" under a
body similar to the Central Board of Film Certification (CBFC).
• Concern:This is a regressive proposal that seeks to apply an anachronistic censorship
model to the dynamic and diverse world of digital media. It is logistically unworkable
and would severely stifle artistic freedom and creativity. Crucially, since "digital
media" includes news and current affairs, this could subject journalistic work like
documentaries to state censorship before publication.
• Proposed Amendment:This recommendation should bewithdrawn in its entirety.
Instead, we should focus on empowering users and strengthening the existing self-
regulatory framework. The alternative recommendation should be:

AJAY MAKAN 4
FINAL DISSENT NOTE OF Sh. AJAY MAKAN
"The Committee recommends that the Government should mandate OTT platforms to
implementtechnologically robust and reliable age-verification mechanismsand enhance
the efficacy and awareness of parental controls. This approach empowers viewers to make
informed choices while preserving the creative freedom that defines the digital medium. Thus,
it should be ensured that the OTTmaintain self-classification with independent self-
regulatory bodies, require robust age-assurance and parental controls, and set fast post-
publication grievance/appeal timelines for demonstrably harmful content to minors.”
4. Regarding Mandatory National Registration for IT Intermediaries (Reference: Para
4.1.8)
• Existing Recommendation:The Committee recommends establishing a "mandatory
national registration system for all IT intermediaries operating in India".
• Concern:A mandatory registration regime shifts the regulatory model from one
based on conduct to one based on permission. This creates a significant barrier to
entry for smaller, innovative startups and provides the government with a tool to deny
market access for arbitrary reasons.
• Proposed Amendment:The goal of accountability can be achieved through less
intrusive means. I propose amending the recommendation to:
"The Committee recommends that all IT intermediaries operating in India, regardless of their
size or origin, be required toappoint and publicly disclose the details of a resident
Grievance Officer and a Nodal Contact Personfor 24x7 coordination with law enforcement
agencies. This ensures a formal point of contact for legal processes without creating a
permission-based system that could stifle innovation."
Suggestions For Additions:
A) The Need for Independent Judicial Oversight
A common thread running through my concerns is expanding executive power at the expense
of judicial oversight. To address this systemic issue, I propose an additional, overarching
recommendation:
"The Committee recommends the establishment of an independent Digital Tribunal,
headed by a retired judge of a High Court or the Supreme Court. This Tribunal shall serve

AJAY MAKAN 5
FINAL DISSENT NOTE OF Sh. AJAY MAKAN
as the primary appellate body for all disputes related to content moderation, takedown
orders, and penalties imposed under the IT Act and its Rules. This will ensure that
decisions impacting the fundamental rights of citizens and the freedom of the press are
subject to impartial judicial review, not executive discretion."
B) Cross-cutting DPDP alignment (place after para 3.2.14)
3.2.14 already urges integrating grievance redressal with emerging data-protection
frameworks.
My proposed amendment
“All data-access, retention, and KYC obligations recommended herein shall comply with
the Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023 and its Rules at notification—ensuring
purpose limitation, storage limitation, audit trails, and security safeguards.” (DLA Piper
Data Protection)
Reason
The DPDP Draft Rules, 2025 were released for consultation on January 3, 2025 and full
enforcement was still pending as of August 2025; this clause future-proofs compliance and
protects privacy.
C) Directions by the Government Agencies Vs Court Orders/ Orders under 69 A of
the IT Act
“Wherever in the Report there is “written orders by Government Agencies”, they should be
replaced with or construed to be Court Orders or Orders under 69A of the IT Act under
relevant circumstances.”
Reason
1. Directly Rectifies the Ambiguity in Paragraph 4.1.11
• The Original Problem:As it stands, Para 4.1.11 recommends making intermediaries
liable for compensating victims for inaction "even after being alerted by Government
agencies about unlawful or malicious content". The term "alerted" is dangerously
vague. It could mean anything from a formal notice to an informal email or phone
call, creating an environment where platforms could be held liable for failing to act on
non-binding or even illegitimate requests. This would inevitably lead to over-
compliance and the suppression of legitimate speech to avoid risk.

AJAY MAKAN 6
FINAL DISSENT NOTE OF Sh. AJAY MAKAN
• How This Amendment Fixes It:This amendment completely transforms this
recommendation. An intermediary's liability would no longer be triggered by a vague
"alert" but only by their failure to comply with a formal Court Order or a procedurally
sound Order under Section 69A. This introduces due process and a high legal
threshold, ensuring that a platform's duty to act is based on a formal, legally
adjudicated determination of unlawfulness, not the discretion of an agency.
2.Clarifies "Lawful Takedown Orders" in Paragraph 3.2.16
• The Original Problem:Para 3.2.16 recommends introducing penalties, including
"potential suspension of operations," for intermediaries that fail to comply with
"lawful takedown orders". The critical ambiguity here lies in who determines if an
order is "lawful." Without clarification, a government agency could issue an order and
unilaterally declare it to be lawful, placing an immense coercive burden on
platforms.
• How This Amendment Fixes It:This amendment provides a precise and
constitutionally sound definition for "lawful takedown orders." By applying this
principle, these orders would be understood to mean only Court Orders or Orders
under 69A. This prevents the executive from becoming the judge in its own cause and
ensures that the most severe penalties are reserved for non-compliance with orders
that have already passed a legal or stringent procedural test.
3.Strengthens "Safe Harbour" Protections Discussed in Paragraphs 3.2.15 and 4.1.7
• The Original Problem:Both paragraphs recommend a review of the "safe harbour"
protections under Section 79 of the IT Act, citing intermediaries' failure to act on
"unlawful content" or non-compliance with "legitimate request made by the Law
Enforcement Agencies". These recommendations open the door to weakening
intermediary immunity based on vague and subjective criteria.
• How This Amendment Fixes It:This amendment provides a clear and objective
benchmark for evaluating an intermediary's conduct. The discussion about revoking
safe harbour would no longer be about a platform's general failure to moderate
content, but about its specific and repeated defiance of Court Orders or Orders under
69A. This aligns the report with the spirit of the Supreme Court's judgment in Shreya
Singhal v. Union of India, which established that an intermediary's "actual

AJAY MAKAN 7
FINAL DISSENT NOTE OF Sh. AJAY MAKAN
knowledge" of unlawful content is triggered by a formal order, thereby protecting
platforms from arbitrary private or executive censorship.
Respected Chairman, my intention in presenting these amendments is not to dilute our fight
against cybercrime but to ensure that our response is precise, proportionate, and in harmony
with the constitutional ethos of our great nation. We must craft laws that are sharp enough to
target criminals but not so broad that they ensnare the innocent, the critical, and the creative.
Kindly treat it as a minute of dissent under 274(2) of Rules of Procedure and Conduct of
Business in the Council of States (Rajya Sabha).
With sincere regards,
AJAY MAKEN

To,
The Honorable Chairman,
Department-Related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Home Affairs,
Parliament of India, New Delhi
Subject:
Minute of Dissent – Rule 274 of Rules of Procedure and Conduct of
Business in the Council of States.
Proposed Amendments to the Recommendations in the Two Hundred and
Fifty Fourth Draft Report on ‘Cyber Crime – Ramifications, Protection
and Prevention’
Respected Chairman,
I would like to place on record my appreciation for the diligence with which the
committee has produced draft report #254. The draft report has outlined in great
detail, the significantly increased threat to our national assets, infrastructure,
and the financial, physical and psychological well-being of our citizens, all of
which are paramount and must be secured at all costs.
The objective to create a robust legal and institutional framework to protect the
nation from cybercriminals, financial fraudsters and malicious actors is well
served by the report’s recommendations however they also bring to light some
imperative concerns within the realm of privacy, personal and creative
freedoms, and Fundamental Rights guaranteed by the Constitution of India.
In this regard, my colleagues from the Congress party have already placed
before the committee, a set of recommendations with which I am in agreement
and I wholly endorse. I do not wish to repeat them here. However, I would like
to add the following points for your perusal:

1. Mandatory Pre-certification for OTT content (Para 3.2.17)
As per the draft report “The Committee, recommends that the
Government may consider amending the IT Rules, 2021 to introduce a
mandatory pre-certification process for OTT content, by establishing an
OTT content regulatory ecosystem within the ambit of CBFC, bringing
it in line with CBFC practices for films and ensuring that explicit or
culturally sensitive material is reviewed prior to public release”.
As regards the above recommendation, I suggest the following:
•“Aside from the extremely grave objections cited by my colleagues
in their recommendations addressing the issue of the Fundamental
Rights of Freedom of speech and expression being curtailed by the
above recommendation, its implementation is likely to prove
practically inefficient and virtually impossible. The number of
films produced by the entire Indian film industry is far exceeded by
the number of films, videos, reels, and other content produced
across the range of OTT platforms. Funnelling all these through a
single, central board equivalent to the CBFC will create a massive
backlog and regulation will become extremely problematic”.
•“This will also seriously affect the OTT industry in terms of the
frequency and ease of content distribution which will in turn affect
employment opportunities in which a large number of youth are
now engaged”.
•“Since the stated objective appears to be primarily curbing access
to adult content by minors, it would be more feasible to allow these
platforms to continue self-regulating and self-rating while
interfacing with them to apply more stringent parental controls”.
•“Instituting a joint forum at which stakeholders can formulate
norms and guidelines that are conducive to required cultural and
social standards as agreed upon by the said stakeholders (if
required to be separate from those of other countries) should be
considered and such guidelines released as directives to be
followed by the OTT platforms. Infringement of the guidelines can
be made to attract relevant penalties.”

•“It is also to be noted that OTT platforms are not the primary
platforms for minors to access adult material. These are already
better regulated than other social media apps and platforms on
which such material is far more easily accessible”.
2. Regulation of Financial Influencers (Para 3.8.9)
As per the draft report “The Committee further recommends SEBI for
exploring the possibility of "verified tick" (a verification mark
indicating that an account or entity has been authenticated and
confirmed as legitimate by a platform or service) for the SEBI
registered investment advisors on social media platforms”.
As regards the above recommendation, I suggest the following:
•“In addition, a mandatory legal provision may be made for
financial influencers to disclose all conflicts of interest on each
video in a standardised, visible manner so as to warn the consumer
of any beneficial interest in disseminating financial advice. Non-
compliance should attract punitive legal action”.
3. Definition of Cybercrime and Organised Cybercrime
As per the draft report, “The National Cybercrime Reporting Portal
(NCRP) defines the Cybercrime as “any unlawful act where a
computer, computer network, or electronic device is used as a tool or
target to commit or facilitate a crime.” This definition includes both
traditional crimes that are facilitated by technology, as well as new age
crimes that specifically target digital systems. Traditional crimes in
cyber space include theft, fraud, forgery, defamation and mischief, all
of which can be committed using electronic devices or networks. There
are some new-age crimes that are emerging, these offenses are
specifically enabled by technology, such as hacking, denial-of-service
attacks, phishing, malware distribution, cyber terrorism, etc.”

As regards the above recommendation, I suggest the following:
• “Crimes committed by minors against minors (which form a
significant part of Cybercrimes) must be differentiated within the
ambit of Cybercrimes as applicable laws will be different”.
• “A clear differentiation between less serious crimes such as uploading
defamatory content and more serious crimes such as disseminating
child pornography for example, should be made and commensurate
laws applied”.
• “Crimes infringing on the privacy of an individual that are enabled by
technology should be given adequate recognition and punitive
measures for the same should be clearly defined”.
• “The definition of Organised Cybercrimes should be made to include
large organisations (corporate and political entities) directing
Cyberattacks on individuals and other organisations so as to cause
them physical, psychological, defamatory, criminal or financial harm.
For example, the use of corporate machinery and finance to engage
trolls and other technological means to attack the reputation of another
corporate entity in an organised fashion”.
4. Size and Origin of IT intermediaries and appointment of local
grievance officers (Para 4.1.8)
As per the draft report “The Committee, therefore recommends
establishing a mandatory national registration system for all IT
intermediaries operating in India, regardless of size or origin, requiring
them to appoint local grievance officers and nodal contact persons with
clear timelines for responses. Further, for SSMIs, (Facebook,
WhatsApp, Telegram etc), the Committee suggests increasing the
number of nodal officers by appointing regional representatives to

facilitate faster and region specific coordination with law enforcement
agencies”.
As regards the above recommendation, I suggest the following:
• “Many IT Intermediaries are small enterprises that may not be able to
afford such appointments and services. It is advisable to allow those
smaller than a defined size to operate without appointments of such
nodal contact persons or local grievance officers and enforce this
recommendation for such intermediaries that are in the position to
afford the same. This distinction can be made according to
standardised norms”.
In addition, I suggest the following recommendations in reference to
regulatory measures mentioned in the draft report:
• “Especially with regard to operators of YouTube channels, news
portals, influencers, comedians, musicians, film makers and digital
news portals, safeguards should be provided for their ability to express
and disseminate their views without oppression by regulatory
mechanisms, harassment by agencies and other means of suppression.
These measures should be clearly articulated and laid out”.
• “Regulatory measures should not be allowed to become excuses for
pressurising or arm-twisting individuals, intermediaries, SSMI/SMI
and IT companies into suppressing material, altering content or
limiting the views of any content for political or ideological reasons.
They should be confined to regulating the entities within the limits of
legality and for the express purpose of curbing Cybercrime of any
kind. A clear policy regarding this distinction should be articulated
and instituted.”

The above suggestions do not in any way, dilute the pressing need to ensure
Cyber security for our citizens, they simply attempt to balance the realistic
environment with the need to regulate and control the players within it.
Our constitution guarantees us the Fundamental Right to Free Speech and
Expression, both of which have flourished in creative form both as part of our
freedom movement and as an integral part of the fabric of our Nationhood since
India’s Independence. It is imperative for us to preserve and strengthen these
Rights that are the bedrock of our democracy while simultaneously doing all we
can to protect and secure our citizens.
It is in this belief that I place on record my agreement and strong endorsement
of the concerns expressed by my colleagues in the Congress Party regarding the
balance between securing our Fundamental Rights and the protection of our
Nation and its Citizens from the menace of Cybercrimes of all kinds. I do hope
that you will accept these recommendations in the same spirit.
Yours sincerely,
Priyanka Gandhi Vadra

practices, cyber threat alert tools, response strategies and data protection measures; 
promoting cyber capacity building activities; cyber threat exercises, workshops, 
webinars, seminars, conferences, trainings on combating cyber crimes; coordinating 
negotiations and adop tion of the UN Convention against cyber crime;   cyber crime 
arising from South -East Asia countries affecting India; regular advisories by our 
Missions/Posts against illegal agents, social media recruitments, fake job rackets; 
regular coordination between MEA/Missions/Posts and MHA on these issues, etc. 
5.       The next presentation was made by the Secretary, MCA. She informed the 
Committee about the regulatory role of the Ministry in addressing cyber crime, 
Companies Act, 2013 and associated rules under it  to maintain secure digital 
records, backups, audit trails and protect data integrity; its role in preventing fake 
companies, identity theft and digital fraud; initiatives by the Ministry for secure e -
governance & digital filings; compliance with CERT -In, NCIIPC and MeitY cyber 
advisories; its responsibility in enabling investigation & enforcement like 
investigating complex frauds including cyber-enabled corporate frauds under various 
Sections of the Companies Act and   IT Act; tracking of shell companies; m ule 
accounts and siphoning of funds; inter -agency collaboration on cyber crime; actions 
for promoting cybersecurity awareness and professional training. 
6.        Thereafter, a presentation was made by the Director, FIU on the subject. He 
apprised the Comm ittee about FIU -IND activities in connection with combating 
cyber crime that inter alia included its role as the central national agency responsible 
for receiving, processing, analyzing and disseminating information relating to 
suspected financial transact ions; how it works closely with all the stakeholders in 
combating cyber frauds such as I4C (Indian Cybercrime Coordination Centre), RBI 
(Reserve Bank of India), DFS (Department of Financial Services), DoT (Department 
of Telecommunications), State Law enforcement Agencies and the wider network of 
financial institutions. He also highlighted the FIU -IND’s role in regularly providing 

142 
 
all the financial institutions with feedback and guidance notes on the evolving trends 
in Anti-Money Laundering/Countering the Financing of Terrorism( AML/CFT) risks 
with suggestions on mitigating cyber fraud risks; regular coordination with the banks 
and payment intermediaries to identify latest trends on cyber frauds; sharing of cyber 
crime related Operational Analysis (OA) report s related to suspected cyber frauds 
with law enforcement agencies.  
7.       The Chairman and Members of the Committee raised some issues to the 
representatives of MEA pertaining to cyber attack by  some hostile countries , 
bilateral talks with different Sta tes to combat cyber crime; efforts for rescue and 
repatriation of victims of fake job rackets from some South East Asian countries, 
rising cases of digital arrest scam, technology initiatives to combat cyber crime 
incidents, difficulties being faced by the  Ministry while combating cyber crime, 
coordination between MHA and MEA to deal with cyber crime, cross -border cyber 
attacks as instruments of hybrid warfare, coordination mechanism of the Ministry 
with the friendly foreign Governments to track these down and then counterattack, 
cyber diplomacy through international cooperation, mechanism that need to be 
developed for statutory and legal framework to deal with perpetrator of cyber crime 
who is outside the territorial jurisdiction of the country; tangible ou tcomes of the 
bilateral dialogues with foreign countries in terms of operational cooperation in

raised by the nation for United Nation Convention on cyber Crime.

and adaptive approach in combating cyber frauds like strengthening the regulatory 
and supervisory frameworks across all Regulated Entities, supervisory measures and 
enforcement actions, stakeholder engagement - collaborating with various 
stakeholders such as MHA, Ministry of Finance, Deptt. of Telecommunications and

the RBI such as onsite/offsite surveillance covering KYC/AML (Anti -Money 
Laundering) and fraud monitoring processes in banks, as part of regular inspections; 
monitoring cash withdrawal thro ugh overseas ATMs and ATMs outside state of 
customer’s location; putting in place IP based transaction monitoring; monitoring of 
current accounts of sole proprietary firms with high transaction volume; the major 
communications sent by RBI to the banks to d eter cyber-enabled frauds; advice to

issued to the banks/Financial Institutions to integrate with Financial Fraud Risk 
Indicator (FRI) system developed by the DoT; payment relate d security measures; 
supervisory vigil and enforcement actions; engagement with stakeholders; 
MuleHunter.ai- a supervised machine learning (ML) model designed for near -real-
time identification of mule accounts; awareness and education initiatives of RBI; etc.
