2.       At the outset, the Chairman welcomed the Members to the meeting of the 
Committee and apprised them that as per the agenda, the Committee will he ar the 
views of representatives of Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI), National 
Stock Exchange (NSE), National Payments Corporation of India (NPCI), National 
Critical Information Infrastructure Protection Centre (NCIIPC) and National 
Informatics Centre (NIC) on the subject 'Cyber Crime – Ramifications, Protection 
and Prevention’.  
 (On arrival of witnesses) 
3.  The Chairman welcomed Whole Time Member, Securities and Exchange 
Board of India (SEBI); Chief Information Security Officer, National Stock 
Exchange (NSE) and Chief Risk Officer, National Payments Corporation of India 
(NPCI) and other senior   officers to the meeting of the Committee.   The proceeding 
of the meeting was commenced with the presentation of the SEBI followed by NSE 
and thereafter, NPCI. 
4.       After the introduction of the representatives of the organisations, the Whole 
Time Member, SEBI gave a brief overview of the role of SEBI in combating cyber 
crime.  He informed the Committee about how the innocent retail investors are often 
subjected to fraud by using social media platforms; a series of measures that aims at 
enhancing investor protection and market integrity; as a policy move, SEBI has 
prohibited registered intermediaries from associating with unregistered 
“fininfluencers” to curb financial misinformation.   He also informed that on the 
enforcement front, SEBI has conducted inspections and search -and-seizure 
operations in cases of market manipulation and expanded investor awareness 
initiatives through intermediaries and m utual fund associations. He mentioned that 
to counter fraudulent investment software apps (applications), SEBI has introduced 
an optional centralized fee collection system for registered advisors and is going to 
introduce a UPI -based validation mechanism t o ensure investor funds are routed 
only to SEBI -registered entities. The Committee was apprised that the lists of 
legitimate investment software apps are now available on NSE and BSE websites, 
and SEBI is working with app stores and social media platforms to implement 
verified identification marks. It was also informed that SEBI has a cybersecurity and 
resilience framework for all registered intermediaries, with safeguards, and to secure 
IT system whenever there is a cyber attack.  
5.       The next present ation was made by the Chief Information Security Officer, 
National Stock Exchange (NSE). The presentation covered in detail about key 
aspects of the NSE which included information  on Indian capital market ecosystem; 
layers of control which are put up to m ake it resilient and available for all the 
investors and customers at all points of time; technology operations initiatives i.e. 
intelligent monitoring and quick incident response, automated operations, 
identification of single point of failure, contingenc y drill, seamless switchover, 
process improvements to better efficiencies, increase flexibility and enhancement 

154 
 
of  IT service performance; technology infrastructure of NSE with state of the art co-
location facility, Multi -tier security with appropriate se gmentation fully resilient 
Infrastructure, fault tolerant servers with an uptime guarantee of 99.999%, etc.   He 
also informed about the threat intelligence backdrop, specific to capital markets; the 
potential impact of cybercrime on market integrity; globa l threat updates and 
evolving attack vector patterns over time like DDoS (Distributed Denial of Service), 
ransomware, data based attacks, application attacks;  supply chain attacks; cyber 
security tools of NSE and its cyber security framework. 
6.       After the completion of the presentation by NSE, the representative of 
National Payments Corporation of India (NPCI) made a presentation before the 
Committee. The Committee was informed about the various key aspects, including 
the role of NPCI in the payments ecosystem and an overview of the Unified 
Payments Interface (UPI) landscape which has played a significant role in driving 
digital payments and it accounts for roughly 80% of all payments nowadays.   The 
representative also briefed the Committee on prevaili ng scams, particularly current -
day frauds and investment scam flows, challenges in the system and nuances of 
money movement. The Committee was apprised of the actions taken by NPCI to 
combat fraud, like device binding and 2FA, cooling period & Debit/Credit  side 
limits, a real time Fraud Risk Monitoring (FRM) Solution with AI/ML based risk 
scoring, close coordination with agencies like I4C, FIU, DoT, IB, Banks, fintechs 
and Law enforcement agencies, sending information on suspicious MULE accounts 
to Banks, implementation of an early warning system for UPI payments. Further, the 
Committee was informed about the ongoing fraud awareness campaigns and media 
engagement initiatives aimed at educating users and enhancing security. The 
presentation was concluded with  an emphasis on targeted awareness campaigns, 
collaborative efforts for strengthening the digital payments ecosystem and stringent 
law to ensure convictions and create a deterrent for fraudsters.  
7.       After the presentation, the Chairman and Members of the Committee raised 
some issues that inter-alia included seeking clarification pertaining to unregistered 
identities promoted on social media platforms; unregistered influencers; fake 
investment apps whether SEBI proposed to take any legal action on such platforms; 
any cyber drills conducted by SEBI in collaboration with major financial 
institutions; awareness programmes for investors from villages and small towns; 
action against shell companies. The information was also sought regarding any plan 
of SEBI to strengthen its surveillance of social media platforms which are 
increasingly being used by pump and dump schemes; plan to improve infrastructure 
status of local offices of SEBI in small cities;  whether SEBI has found any cyber 
security weakness or loophole that high frequency trading firms could use to unfairly 
affect the market, steps taken by SEBI to keep a check on such firms. The 
representative of NSE was asked whether NSE has implemented an y partnership 
with cyber security firms or any academic institutions to build threat intelligence or 
R&D in market security.  During the meeting queries were raised about NPCI’s 
coordination with banks and RBI in case of cyber frauds involving Bhim, UPI or  

155 
 
Rupee platforms; matters related to linking UPI and demat accounts; the provisions 
to deal with authorized push payment frauds and mule accounts and similarly, the 
responsibility of account holder if he receives money in his account without his 
knowledge or permission. 
8.  The representatives of SEBI, NSE and NPCI replied to some of the queries 
raised by the Chairman and Members of the Committee. On other queries, they were 
asked to furnish written replies to the Secretariat within five days.  
(The witnesses withdrew) 
(The Committee re-assembled at 02:30 p.m. to hear the views of National 
Critical Information Infrastructure Protection Centre (NCIIPC) and National 
Informatics Centre (NIC) on the subject). 
9.     The Chairman welcomed the Director General, Na tional Critical Information 
Infrastructure Protection Centre (NCIIPC), and Deputy Director General, National 
Informatics Centre (NIC) and other senior officers to the meeting of the Committee 
and asked them to make a presentation on the subject ‘Cyber Crim e - Ramifications, 
Protection and Prevention’ one after the other.  
 10.     After the introductions of the team of officers from NCIIPC, representative of 
the Centre made a presentation before the Committee. The presentation covered an 
overview of NCIIPC, including its mandate and functions. It inter- alia included the 
significance of protection of Critical Information Infrastructure (CII); the critical 
sectors identified in India; various CII protection -related activities and support from 
the NCIIPC such as capacity building/raising cyber security awareness, sharing 
vulnerabilities/ threats, risk assessment and practical suggestions for mitigation, 
cross sectoral exchange/ dissemination of best practices, etc. The Committee was 
also apprised about the Auto mated Threat Dissemination Platform (ATDP), an 
indigenously developed platform for sending real time cyber threat intelligence to 
more than 180 organisations; incident response activities; cyber security gap 
analysis; and risk assessments undertaken by NCI IPC for Critical Sector Entities 
(CSE); new protection strategies, mechanisms developed for monitoring CIIs and 
the Sectoral Coordinator -based System for monitoring critical sectors; constitution 
of the Information Security Steering Committee (ISSC) by org anisations having 
protected systems.  
11.     At the end, the DDG, NIC apprised the Committee that the NIC provides 
technology-driven solutions to enhance capacities at Central, State, and District 
levels, offering services through its organizational struc ture, and services/enterprise 
level applications to many sectors in Government of India & State Governments. 
The presentation on the subject also highlighted information on National Data 
Centres and the Government of India’s National Cloud initiative- MeghRaj, aimed at 
optimizing e -Governance; Information and Communication Technology (ICT) 
Network; National Knowledge Network - multi-gigabit PAN -India 
network;  maintaining a secure digital environment for government infrastructure. It 
was informed that NIC's cyber security framework covers computer network, 

156 
 
sensitive information and data, software applications, individual devices, data and 
services in cloud environments, managing user access to systems & data. NIC 
implements a comprehensive cybersecurity strat egy through technical, 
administrative, and physical measures, as well as continuous maintenance, 
monitoring, and deployment of specialized tools, ensuring robust protection for 
government IT infrastructure. 
12.     The Chairman and Members of the Committee  also raised several issues 
relating to the NCIIPC, which inter alia included recent vulnerability assessments; 
details of cyber attacks on critical infrastructure —such as their origin, mitigation 
efforts; and response strategies; cyber intrusions/attacks by some hostile countries 
and changes in cyber threat levels and mitigation measures to counter these attacks. 
Updates were sought on data protection protocols, safeguards against insider threats 
and the role of AI in countering such risks. Additionally, q ueries were also raised 
about NCIIPC’s balance between intelligence gathering and collaborative protection, 
adherence to quarterly ISSC meetings by 114 notified entities, measurable 
improvements in cyber security, and frameworks for evaluating cyber maturi ty 
levels in these organizations. 
13.       The Chairman and Members of the Committee raised some issues with the 
representative of National Informatics Centre (NIC) regarding clearance and 
approval process for infrastructure upgrades and cyber security pr otocols; whether 
the existing data centres are adequate; suggestions for expansion to meet growing 
cyber resilience needs. Clarification was also sought on whether existing data 
centres function as data recovery centres.  
  
14.     The representatives of NCIIPC, NIC and MHA replied to some of the queries 
raised by the Chairman and Members of the Committee. On other queries, they were 
asked to furnish written replies to the Secretariat within five days.  
                                                (The witnesses withdrew) 
15.     A Verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept. 
16.     The meeting adjourned at 04:56 p.m.

WITNESSES 
  
Domain Experts   
  
1. Dr. Pavan Duggal, Founder & Chairman, International Commission on Cyber 
Security Law and Advocate, Supreme Court of the India; and 
2. Shri Trishneet Arora, CEO, TAC Security 
  
Representatives of Ministry of Home Affairs 
Ms. Roopa M., Inspector General 
  
2.       At the outset, the Chairman welcomed the Members to the meeting of the 
Committee and stated that the Committee has already heard the views of the related 
Ministries and some Organisations on the subject 'Cyber Crime – Ramifications, 
Protection and Prevention’. He apprised them that as per the agenda, in the present 
meeting, the Committee will hear the views of two domain experts on the subject, 
namely Dr. Pavan Duggal, Founder & Chairman, Internat ional Commission on 
Cyber Security Law and Shri Trishneet Arora, CEO of TAC Security who have been 
invited to share their views on the subject.  
 (On arrival of witnesses) 
3.       The Chairman welcomed Dr. Pavan Duggal, Founder & Chairman, 
International Commission on Cyber Security Law and Shri Trishneet Arora, CEO of 
TAC Security to the meeting of the Committee and requested them to present their 
views on the subject 'Cyber Crime – Ramifications, Protection and Prevention’.  The 
proceedings of the meeting commenced with the presentation by Dr. Pavan Duggal. 
4.       Dr. Pavan Duggal informed the Committee that cyber crimes has become an 
epidemic and it is thriving due to fear and panic among general public and these 
factors are being misused by cyber crim inals to commit frauds. He made a detailed 
presentation before the Committee and informed the Committee about the best 
practices adopted by major countries in preventing cyber crime which includes 
robust laws & public -private collaboration; harmonized regu lations & focus on 
privacy; comprehensive strategy & cyber hygiene culture; intelligence -driven 
defence & innovation. The Committee was also provided information on cyber 
crime caseloads and convictions; international nature of cybercrime & preventive 
steps; global Treaties and Conventions; international agencies in cybercrime 
prevention; global cybercrime bodies & coalitions; best practices by Indian States 
and Police agencies like cybercrime portals & helplines, cybercrime training & 
capacity building, sp ecialized units & policies, use of advanced 
technology;  limitation of IT Act, 2000 to deal with cyber crime; challenges in 
enforcement & obtaining evidence; role of Government Ministries in preventing 
cyber crimes; etc. 
  

159 
 
5.       To strengthen cybercrime  legislation, he suggested that a dedicated 
comprehensive cybercrime Law is to be formulated to consolidate offences and 
expand definitions for modern crimes like ransomware, DoS (denial of Service), and 
phishing, explicitly criminalize modern cybercrimes and provide graded 
punishments for organized or repeat offenders and mandate cybersecurity audits and 
breach disclosure for companies, with penalties for negligence. He also suggested 
for aligning with global cybercrime frameworks, incorporating provisions  like 
expedited data preservation and cross-border data sharing into Indian laws, ensuring 
crime definitions match global standards for easier cooperation.   Other suggestions 
inter alia  included specialized cybercrime courts; strengthening of data protecti on 
and privacy; need for legislation on misuse of Artificial Intelligence; strengthen 
provisions for courts to award restitution or compensation to cybercrime victims as 
part of sentencing; strengthening of legal & regulatory framework; capacity building 
for law enforcement agencies; public awareness and digital literacy; improving 
cyber hygiene across organizations; etc.  
6.       The next presentation was made by Shri Trishneet Arora, while making his 
presentation he highlighted the financial frauds, Stat e-sponsored cyber threats by 
hostile neighbouring countries; threats to critical infrastructures; technology gaps; 
involvement of multiple ministries and organisations to deal with cyber frauds, cyber 
threats and cyber security; need for comprehensive sing le nodal agency to deal with 
matters related to cyber security; best practices adopted by major countries, like 
creation of cyber security ministry. He suggested there is a need to assess the current 
state of cyber security of nationwide critical infrastru ctures, like, energy, power 
grids, ports, airports, defence system, etc.   He also suggested that only Indian origin 
cyber auditing companies be considered for auditing of national critical 
infrastructures, creation of an organisation to help victims especi ally women who 
become victims of cyber crime, online harassment; nation -wide cyber awareness 
campaign; provision for enforcement of cyber insurance for common citizens in case 
one falls victim to cyber frauds; provision for cyber ambulance facility, etc.  
7.       The Chairman and Members raised some queries and sought clarification 
pertaining to best global practices for victim compensation, responsibility of banks 
in case of financial frauds, need for improvement and changes in existing laws; 
strengthening of infrastructure, sufficient funds; very low conviction rate; children 
becoming victims of cyber crimes, best mechanism to tackle this menace and 
increase conviction, cyber attacks from hostile countries, capacity building of 
personnel of law enforcemen t agencies, cyber army and commandos for cyber 
security, time taken by Indian law enforcement agencies and US agencies to 
investigate cyber crimes, provision of cyber insurance and cyber ambulance, cyber 
threats to power grids, etc. 
8.     The experts replied to some of the queries raised by the Members and on other 
queries, they were asked to furnish written replies to the Secretariat within a week. 
                                                  (Witnesses withdrew) 

160 
 
9.       A Verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept. 
10.     The meeting adjourned at 5:10 p.m.

Committee on the subject “Cyber Crime – Ramifications, Protection and

their views on the recommendations, raised certain issues, and requested time for
